Fundamentals / Mechanical engineering / Machine dynamics

Machine dynamics

Derive equations of motion, identify modes and damping, and use them to design isolation, balance rotors and assess finite-amplitude vibration.

Chapters, problem sheets and the applied study in reading order. For a paper copy, use your browser’s print command. Open the worked solutions you want to include before printing.

Read the subject in depth

Each volume develops its arguments in sequence, with worked problems and links to experiments.

  • Vibration and identification in machinery

    Connect modes, moving components and measured frequency responses. Investigate how forcing, damping and the observation point change an inferred dynamic model.

Advanced study across the subject

Each sequence connects the course to detailed chapters in neighbouring subjects. The complete course PDF includes their derivations, examples and exercises.

  1. Coordinates, invariants and integration

    Derive a coordinate description for a loaded mechanism, follow its energy through a cycle and assess numerical drift before assigning a measured decay to damping.

  2. Dynamic coupling and measured response

    Combine structural elasticity, a transducer and an estimator. Compare a split resonance, a free decay and a periodically loaded shaft.

How the course fits together

  1. Chapters 1–4

    Choose a coordinate and compute the kinetic and elastic energy it carries. Free and driven vibration follow from inertia, stiffness and damping. Coupled coordinates permit several natural frequencies and mode shapes.

  2. Chapters 5–8

    Distinguish base motion from a directly applied force and a transient from a steady response. Rigid-body kinematics and impulse balances tell you how an initial motion is created before vibration begins.

  3. Chapters 9–12

    An unbalanced rotor provides a periodic load. Lagrange’s equations assemble a multibody model, modal forces identify which vibrations a load excites, and nonlinear stiffness can move the resonances with amplitude.

Chapter 1 · Coordinates, energy and equivalent inertia

Reduce a constrained drive to one coordinate and use a power balance to check its equation of motion.

From first principles

Refer moving masses to one shaft by matching kinetic energy

Choose shaft angle \(\theta\) as the independent coordinate of a rigid mechanism. Suppose the angular speeds of its rotating components and the linear speeds of its translating components are fixed multiples of \(\dot\theta\). Assume ideal constraints with no slip, so specifying \(\theta\) and \(\dot\theta\) fixes the motion. Total kinetic energy is the sum of the individual translational and rotational energies.

\[T=\sum_i\tfrac12m_i v_i^2+\sum_j\tfrac12J_j\omega_j^2=\tfrac12 J_{\rm eq}\dot\theta^2 .\]

Here rotational inertias are about the component centres or fixed rotation axes as appropriate, with no motion counted twice. Dividing by \(\dot\theta^2/2\) gives the inertia seen at the chosen shaft.

\[J_{\rm eq}=\sum_i m_i\left(\frac{v_i}{\dot\theta}\right)^2+\sum_jJ_j\left(\frac{\omega_j}{\dot\theta}\right)^2 .\]

A force on a translating part contributes the shaft torque that gives the same instantaneous power,

\[T_i\dot\theta=F_iv_i.\]

For constant speed ratios and constant equivalent inertia, energy balance then gives

\[\sum T_i=J_{\rm eq}\ddot\theta.\]

The square on the speed ratio in inertia reflection follows from energy and is essential when changing the reference shaft.

Background · Generalised inertia
\[M(q)=2T/\dot q^2\]

This scalar definition applies to one independent moving coordinate with purely quadratic kinetic energy. Changing the coordinate changes the numerical inertia.

Derivation and applications

Derive the extra drive term for position-dependent inertia

A motor can require a torque at constant angular speed if a mechanism changes the linear speed of its payload during a revolution. Use a single angular coordinate \(\theta\), input inertia \(J_0\), and a guided mass \(m\) with position \(x=f(\theta)\). Assume a rigid lossless linkage and a horizontal guide, so gravity does no work. The kinematic constraint sets the payload velocity and fixes the instantaneous reflected inertia by equating the total kinetic energy to that of one shaft.

\[\dot x=f'(\theta)\dot\theta,\]
\[T=\tfrac12[J_0+m(f')^2]\dot\theta^2=\tfrac12 J(\theta)\dot\theta^2 .\]

Differentiate the energy with respect to time. The shaft input supplies the full change in kinetic energy through its power \(\tau\dot\theta\). Equating the two and cancelling speed gives the required drive torque. At a turning point the same equation follows directly from Lagrange's equation.

\[\tau=J(\theta)\ddot\theta+\tfrac12J'(\theta)\dot\theta^2.\]

For an ideal sinusoidal guide \(x=a\sin\theta\), substitution makes the speed-squared contribution explicit. It changes sign within each turn because the translating payload first absorbs and then releases kinetic energy.

\[J=J_0+ma^2\cos^2\theta,\]
\[\tau=J\ddot\theta-ma^2\sin\theta\cos\theta\,\dot\theta^2.\]

A constant-inertia approximation omits this alternating torque even if it predicts the total energy averaged over a turn. Its integral over a full constant-speed revolution vanishes as the payload returns to its initial speed.

Worked example

Predict a constant-speed torque measurement

A 2.00 kg carriage follows \(x=0.0500\sin\theta\) metres with shaft speed 20.0 rad/s. At \(\theta=45^\circ\) its required torque is \(-2.00(0.0500)^2(0.5)(20.0)^2=-1.00\) N m. The motor absorbs 20.0 W at that instant. At 135° the required torque is \(+1.00\) N m. Both readings occur at the same shaft speed and the same reflected inertia.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Identify a payload from the torque harmonic

A separate horizontal mechanism has stroke parameter \(a=40.0\) mm and runs at 30.0 rad/s. After removing its empty-linkage trace, the measured second-harmonic torque amplitude is 0.720 N m. Since \(|\sin\theta\cos\theta|_{\max}=1/2\), the fitted mass is

\[2(0.720)/[(0.0400)^2(30.0)^2]=1.00\,\mathrm{kg}.\]

A run at half speed should show 0.180 N m amplitude if the measured harmonic is inertial.

Start from this chapter. Write translation and rotation as distinct kinetic-energy terms. Adding material near the reference point mostly changes the translational term. Predict which change the frame model makes when only rotary inertia is doubled.

OneSimulation laboratory · Separate the effect of mass from rotary inertia

Follow the motion through a mechanism

A motor accelerating a load feels inertia from components that turn at different speeds. Simply adding their physical moments of inertia would treat all of those speeds as equal. Instead, choose one independent coordinate and express every kinetic energy in terms of it. No-slip belts, rigid gears and inextensible cables give useful constraints when their compliance and backlash are negligible over the motion of interest.

Consider a drum of radius \(R\) winding a cable carrying a vertically guided mass \(m\). Choose the drum angle \(\theta\) positive when the mass rises. If the effective winding radius is constant, displacement, velocity and acceleration satisfy \(x=R\theta\), \(\dot x=R\dot\theta\) and \(\ddot x=R\ddot\theta\). These equalities are kinematic. A torque relation requires force balance or work in addition. When the cable builds successive layers on a spool, the effective radius varies with angle and the constant-ratio assumption must be revisited.

Background · What changes when the reflected inertia depends on position?

For a piston linkage let the piston position be \(x(q)\), where \(q\) is shaft angle. Its kinetic energy contributes \(m(dx/dq)^2\) to the shaft inertia. As the linkage moves through a stroke this contribution varies, even for a piston of constant mass. Use the energy derivative to find the generalised inertial torque.

\[\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial T}{\partial\dot q}-\frac{\partial T}{\partial q}=J(q)\ddot q+\tfrac12J^{\prime}(q)\dot q^2,\]
\[T=\tfrac12J(q)\dot q^2.\]

Uniform shaft speed sets the first term to zero. The second term represents kinetic energy flowing into and out of the accelerating piston. Its shaft work over a complete cycle vanishes when the mechanism returns to the same angle and speed. An average torque meter can consequently miss a substantial alternating torque.

Reflect mass and forces to a common shaft

Let \(J_d\) be the drum inertia. Add the rotational energy of the drum to the translational energy of the load, then express both in terms of the drum speed.

\[\begin{aligned} K&=\tfrac12 J_d\dot\theta^2+\tfrac12 m\dot x^2\\ &=\tfrac12\bigl(J_d+mR^2\bigr)\dot\theta^2,\\ J_{\rm eq}&=J_d+mR^2. \end{aligned}\]

A small positive angle raises the mass through \(R\,d\theta\), increasing its potential energy by \(mgR\,d\theta\). Thus gravity contributes a resisting torque \(mgR\). A positive applied torque \(T_d\) supplies power \(T_d\dot\theta\). Applying the energy balance gives the equation of motion.

\[\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\left(\tfrac12J_{\rm eq}\dot\theta^2+mgR\theta\right)&=T_d\dot\theta,\\ J_{\rm eq}\ddot\theta&=T_d-mgR . \end{aligned}\]

The second expression also holds at zero speed by force balance. It separates steady effort, needed to hold or raise the load against gravity, from the extra effort needed to accelerate the two inertias. With the motor disconnected, an ideal light cable does not make the mass accelerate quite at \(g\), since the drum must gain rotational kinetic energy too.

Calculate a motor torque

A drum with \(R=0.10\) m and \(J_d=0.15\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}\) lifts 25 kg. A reduction gear gives

\[\dot\theta_m=4\dot\theta.\]

The rotor has inertia \(J_m=0.020\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}\). Refer the train to the motor coordinate. The drum-plus-load inertia becomes \((0.15+25(0.10)^2)/4^2=0.025\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}\), so the total at the motor is 0.045 kg·m². Gravity contributes \(25(9.81)(0.10)/4=6.13\) N·m at that shaft.

Demand upward acceleration 0.80 m/s². The drum must accelerate at 8.0 rad/s² and the motor at 32 rad/s². The motor supplies

\[6.13+0.045(32)=7.57\,\mathrm{N\,m}.\]

A separate check at load speed 1.0 m/s gives motor speed 40 rad/s. Its 303 W then divide into about 245 W raising the weight and 57.6 W increasing kinetic energy. Ideal gearing changes torque and angular speed inversely while preserving transmitted power.

Identify which approximation controls the answer

At constant lifting speed the inertial torque vanishes. At constant angular acceleration, doubling the transmission ratio decreases reflected load inertia by four but also doubles the motor acceleration for the same load motion. The rotor’s own inertia remains at the motor and eventually dominates. A gear ratio therefore cannot reduce every part of acceleration torque indefinitely. Bearings, cables and gears also dissipate energy or store it elastically. Include their effects when a measured transient shows a repeatable lag, oscillation or loss that an ideal rigid constraint cannot explain.

Is the largest reduction always preferable?

For a prescribed drum acceleration \(\alpha_d\) and reduction \(n=\omega_m/\omega_d\), collect gravity and load-side acceleration into the drum demand \(T_d=mgR+(J_d+mR^2)\alpha_d\). The motor torque becomes

\[T_m=T_d/n+J_mn\alpha_d.\]

The first term falls as reduction rises, while rotor acceleration grows. Differentiating with respect to \(n\) gives an ideal minimum at \(n=\sqrt{T_d/(J_m\alpha_d)}\). For the stated lift \(T_d=27.73\) N m and \(\alpha_d=8\) rad/s², giving \(n=13.2\) approximately. This torque minimum assumes the motor can achieve the associated speed. At a drum speed of 10 rad/s it would turn at 132 rad/s, over three times its speed with the original reduction. Shaft-speed and voltage limits can therefore select a different practical ratio.

Derive speed ripple from a torque diagram

A crank-driven machine can require very different torque at different angles even when the average load is constant. A flywheel stores kinetic energy when driving torque exceeds resisting torque, then releases it when the load peaks. Refer all inertias and torques to the same shaft and suppose the equivalent inertia \(J\) is constant. The signed difference \(M(\theta)=T_{\rm drive}-T_{\rm load}\) gives

\[J\dot\omega=M.\]

For continuous positive rotation write \(\dot\omega=\omega\,d\omega/d\theta\). Integration between two angular positions gives an exact work–energy relation.

\[\tfrac12 J[\omega_b^2-\omega_a^2]=\int_{\theta_a}^{\theta_b}M(\theta)\,d\theta .\]

A periodic steady sequence requires zero net area under the torque-difference curve over a whole revolution. Its cumulative area nevertheless has a maximum and a minimum. Their difference \(\Delta E\) is the energy that must swing into and out of the rotating inertia. Define mean speed in the ripple specification as the arithmetic mean of the two extreme speeds \(\bar\omega=(\omega_{\max}+\omega_{\min})/2\) and fluctuation coefficient \(C_s=(\omega_{\max}-\omega_{\min})/\bar\omega\). Factoring the difference of squared speeds gives

\[J=\Delta E/(\bar\omega^2 C_s).\]

A time-average tachometer reading approximates this mean for small ripple, but the definitions are distinct for a strongly pulsating shaft.

Size a flywheel for a repeating work demand

A laboratory indexing machine runs at a nominal 300 rpm. A motor supplies approximately constant 30 N m. The resisting torque is 70 N m for the first quarter revolution and a lower constant value for the remaining three quarters. Equate total input and output work over each revolution: \(30(2\pi)=70(\pi/2)+T_l(3\pi/2)\), giving \(T_l=16.67\,\mathrm{N\,m}\). The first quarter produces a 40 N m deficit, so the cumulative energy falls by \(40(\pi/2)=62.83\,\mathrm J\). The remaining 13.33 N m surplus restores exactly that energy.

For a maximum peak-to-peak speed variation of 2% and \(\bar\omega=31.42\,\mathrm{rad/s}\), total inertia required is

\[62.83/[31.42^2(0.02)]=3.18\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}.\]

If the machine already has 1.00 kg m² on this shaft, an extra 2.18 kg m² is required. A thin-ring estimate at mean radius 0.30 m gives mass \(2.18/0.30^2=24.2\,\mathrm{kg}\). A solid disc of the same radius needs twice the mass because its material lies closer to the shaft. At half speed the same torque-versus-angle work deficit requires four times the total inertia for the same fractional ripple. Startup torque or bearing load may therefore limit an inertia-based solution even when steady motor power suffices.

The torque balance also predicts where a shaft encoder should record the two speed extremes. The energy derivative with respect to angle equals the torque difference, so the maximum occurs just before the high resisting plateau begins and the minimum where that plateau ends. The maximum torque itself persists over a finite angular interval, while minimum speed occurs only at its end. This distinction matters when timing a torque pulse from a speed record: the sign of the speed derivative, rather than the absolute value of speed, identifies when the flywheel is discharging.

Chapter 2 · Free vibration, energy loss and ring-down data

Infer stiffness and damping from a released machine mount and check the implications of a viscous damping model.

From first principles

Determine the frequency and decay of a released mount

Displace a mass \(m\) from its static equilibrium on a linear spring of stiffness \(k\) and release it. A viscous damper exerts force opposite to velocity with coefficient \(c\). Measuring displacement \(x\) relative to static equilibrium removes any constant force already balanced by the spring. Newton's second law gives the free-motion equation.

\[m\ddot x=-kx-c\dot x .\]

An exponential trial \(e^{st}\) turns differentiation into multiplication by \(s\), so nonzero motion requires a root of the characteristic polynomial. Its two roots depend on the undamped natural frequency \(\omega_n\) and damping ratio \(\zeta\).

\[ms^2+cs+k=0,\]
\[\omega_n=\sqrt{k/m},\]
\[\zeta=\frac{c}{2\sqrt{km}}.\]

For \(0<\zeta<1\) the roots are a conjugate pair. Their oscillatory contributions combine to give a real damped sinusoid with envelope \(e^{-\zeta\omega_nt}\).

\[s=-\zeta\omega_n\pm i\omega_n\sqrt{1-\zeta^2}.\]

The initial position and velocity determine amplitude and phase, while the parameters set decay and period. Same-sign peaks one period apart therefore measure the exponential envelope without requiring an accurately known release time.

Background · Damping ratio
\[\zeta=c/(2\sqrt{km})\]

This compares a viscous damper with critical damping for a single linear oscillator. It cannot represent Coulomb friction at every amplitude.

Derivation and applications

Infer damping from the loss of mechanical energy per cycle

A lightly damped mount sheds a measurable fraction of stored energy on each oscillation. Consider a single translating mass \(m\) with linear stiffness \(k\), viscous damping \(c\), and no forcing after release. Measure displacement from static equilibrium. Multiply the equation of motion by velocity to convert inertia and spring terms into total time derivatives.

\[m\ddot x+c\dot x+kx=0,\]
\[\frac{d}{dt}\left(\tfrac12m\dot x^2+\tfrac12kx^2\right)=-c\dot x^2 .\]

For weak damping, amplitude \(A\) changes little during one period and \(x\simeq A\cos\omega_nt\), with \(\omega_n=\sqrt{k/m}\). Integrating viscous power over the cycle then gives the leading loss. Divide by the stored maximum spring energy evaluated at the same amplitude.

\[\Delta E_{\rm loss}\simeq c A^2\omega_n^2\frac{\pi}{\omega_n}=\pi c\omega_nA^2,\qquad \frac{\Delta E_{\rm loss}}{E}\simeq4\pi\zeta,\]
\[\zeta=\frac{c}{2m\omega_n}.\]

For appreciable damping use two same-sign turning points directly. At each peak the kinetic energy vanishes, so the exact energy ratio equals the square of the amplitude ratio. The measured logarithmic decrement relates to viscous damping for an underdamped oscillator.

\[\frac{E_{j+1}}{E_j}=\left(\frac{A_{j+1}}{A_j}\right)^2=e^{-2\delta},\]
\[\zeta=\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{4\pi^2+\delta^2}},\]
\[\delta=\ln\frac{A_j}{A_{j+1}} .\]

The energy fraction is independent of starting amplitude for linear viscous damping. Amplitude dependence in repeated runs is therefore a direct reason to examine extra friction or nonlinear damping.

Worked example

Determine heat release from two peak readings

For \(k=800\) N/m, consecutive positive peaks are 10.0 and 9.00 mm. The spring energies are 40.0 mJ and 32.4 mJ, so 7.60 mJ is dissipated. The decrement is \(\ln(10/9)=0.1054\), giving \(\zeta=0.0168\). If the first amplitude were doubled and the same linear damper retained, the energy lost in that cycle would quadruple.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Test a damper from its measured cycle work

A separate mount has \(k=2000\) N/m, \(\omega_n=50.0\) rad/s and small oscillations of amplitude 2.00 mm. A damper dynamometer measures 0.250 mJ of work per cycle. The leading estimate gives \(c=\Delta E/(\pi\omega_nA^2)=0.398\) N s/m. From \(m=k/\omega_n^2=0.800\) kg, \(\zeta=0.00497\). The estimated loss fraction \(4\pi\zeta=0.0625\) supports the nearly constant-amplitude approximation for a single cycle.

OneSimulation laboratory

When does a released mass cross equilibrium?

Release a 1 kg mass from rest 10 mm to the right of equilibrium on a spring of stiffness 4 N/m. A viscous damper exerts a force opposite to the velocity. In a driven machine this free response is the transient left by the initial displacement. Here the external drive is zero, so the transient can be measured on its own.

Predict a crossing. With displacement measured from static equilibrium, the weight has already been balanced. Newton’s second law gives one restoring term for displacement and one for velocity. Write the trial motion as an exponential. Dividing the resulting equation by the nonzero exponential leaves a quadratic:

\[m\ddot x=-kx-c\dot x,\]
\[x=Ae^{st}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad s^2+2\zeta\omega_n s+\omega_n^2=0,\]
\[\omega_n=\sqrt{k/m},\]
\[\zeta=\frac{c}{2\sqrt{mk}}.\]

Here \(\omega_n=2\) rad/s. At \(\zeta=0.2\) the roots are \(-0.4\pm1.960i\) s⁻¹. The imaginary part permits a zero crossing. At critical damping the double root is \(-2\) s⁻¹. Applying the initial conditions to the two independent solutions gives \(x=10(1+2t)e^{-2t}\) mm. At \(\zeta=2\) the slowest root is about \(-0.536\) s⁻¹. Which of the last two releases is closer to equilibrium at three seconds?

Download this run (CSV)

Read the comparison. At three seconds the critical solution is about 0.174 mm from equilibrium. Increasing damping again leaves about 2.16 mm. The slower real root explains the long tail. The underdamped trajectory has already crossed the equilibrium. The points come from integrating the two first-order equations for displacement and velocity. The line comes from their characteristic roots.

Try a fourth case. Change the damping ratio to 0.7. Predict the first zero from the damped frequency and the phase fixed by release from rest, then find the first sign change in the output CSV.

Download the OneSimulation model (Python). Set up the local runtime, then run the downloaded file:

python oscillator.py --ratio 0.7

Start from this chapter. The one-coordinate frequency varies as the inverse square root of mass at fixed stiffness. Test that expectation against three frame modes when the payload mass changes.

OneSimulation laboratory · Separate the effect of mass from rotary inertia

Displace a machine from its static position

A pump on a spring mount settles under its weight. Pull it slightly farther down and release it. The weight still acts, but the extra motion is driven by the change in spring force. Define \(x\) as displacement from the loaded equilibrium, take constant local spring stiffness \(k\), and represent dissipation by a force opposing velocity of magnitude \(c|\dot x|\). Newton’s law becomes

\[m\ddot x+c\dot x+kx=0.\]

This model describes one isolated mode over an amplitude range where inertia, stiffness and damping are approximately constant.

Multiply the equation by \(\dot x\). The mass term differentiates kinetic energy, and the spring term differentiates stored elastic energy. Consequently \(d(m\dot x^2/2+kx^2/2)/dt=-c\dot x^2\). Energy cannot grow for positive damping. Displacement can increase temporarily as kinetic energy converts back into spring energy, so the instantaneous displacement alone is not an energy meter.

Obtain the decay and oscillation rates

Trying \(x=e^{st}\) gives

\[ms^2+cs+k=0.\]

Define \(\omega_n=\sqrt{k/m}\) and \(\zeta=c/(2m\omega_n)\). The roots are

\[s=-\zeta\omega_n\pm\omega_n\sqrt{\zeta^2-1}.\]

For \(0<\zeta<1\) their imaginary part gives the damped angular frequency \(\omega_d=\omega_n\sqrt{1-\zeta^2}\). The real part gives a decaying exponential. A general underdamped trace is

\[x(t)=e^{-\zeta\omega_nt}\left[A\cos(\omega_dt)+B\sin(\omega_dt)\right].\]

At \(\zeta=1\) the repeated root gives \((A+Bt)e^{-\omega_nt}\). Above critical damping both roots are negative and real. A lack of visible oscillation therefore does not by itself determine the stiffness. For an underdamped trace, use two peaks of the same sign: their separation is \(T_d=2\pi/\omega_d\), and their amplitude ratio depends only on the envelope decay.

Background · Which energy is represented by a sequence of displacement peaks?

At a turning point the velocity is zero. If displacement is measured from static equilibrium, the energy of the linear oscillator there is entirely elastic. Two peaks of the same sign one damped period apart give an exact energy ratio even though the instantaneous dissipation varies within that period.

\[\frac{E_{n+1}}{E_n}=\left(\frac{x_{n+1}}{x_n}\right)^2,\]
\[\frac{\Delta E_{\rm lost}}{E_n}=1-e^{-2\delta},\]
\[\delta=\ln\frac{|x_n|}{|x_{n+1}|}.\]

If successive positive peaks fall from 10 to 8 mm, 36% of the initial peak energy has been lost in one cycle. The amplitude has fallen by only 20%. Near critical damping it may be difficult to resolve a full period above sensor noise. The definition then remains valid for a measurable underdamped cycle, but the inferred ratio becomes sensitive to peak errors.

Infer parameters from four cycles

A 20.0 kg mounted component rings down from a positive peak of 8.0 mm to a positive peak of 2.0 mm four cycles later, after 1.60 s. The period is \(T_d=0.400\) s and \(\omega_d=15.71\) rad/s. The log decrement per cycle is

\[\delta=\ln(8/2)/4=0.3466.\]

Since \(x_j/x_{j+1}=\exp(\zeta\omega_n T_d)\), the envelope decay rate is

\[\alpha=\delta/T_d=0.8664\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}.\]

Now \(\omega_n=\sqrt{\omega_d^2+\alpha^2}=15.73\) rad/s and \(\zeta=\alpha/\omega_n=0.0551\). The inferred stiffness is \(m\omega_n^2=4.95\) kN/m, while \(c=2m\alpha=34.7\) N·s/m. After eight cycles the prediction is 0.50 mm. Using peaks separated by several cycles reduces sensitivity to noise in any one amplitude or timestamp.

Challenge the damping assumption

Subtract the equilibrium sensor reading before taking amplitude ratios. A fixed offset changes the ratio of small late peaks more strongly than large early peaks. Plot \(\ln |x_j|\) against time for peaks whose signal clearly exceeds the noise floor. A straight trend supports the viscous envelope. Approximately constant loss of amplitude per cycle suggests a different mechanism such as dry friction. Two nearby natural frequencies may produce a beat envelope that grows and shrinks even though total energy decays.

A second experiment can separate stiffness from added inertia. If a small known mass is bolted to the same moving assembly and the support stiffness is unchanged, \(1/\omega_n^2\) should increase in proportion to effective mass. This check assumes that the added mass participates fully in the chosen coordinate. A flexible appendage that moves out of phase changes the system being identified.

Correct a biased displacement signal

Suppose a displacement sensor reads 0.50 mm high relative to the true equilibrium. The first and fifth positive peaks then appear as 8.50 and 2.50 mm. Their raw log ratio over four cycles gives \(0.25\ln(8.5/2.5)=0.306\), underestimating the true 0.347 decrement. The error grows as the decaying signal approaches the offset. One remedy is to measure equilibrium independently when excitation has stopped. Another is to use the difference between consecutive positive and negative extrema and compare such differences one whole period apart. The constant offset cancels while, for a single underdamped mode, successive peak-to-trough ranges retain the same exponential ratio. Late peaks below the sensor resolution still carry little reliable damping information.

Derive the repeated-root response and its crossing condition

The oscillatory ring-down described above assumes \(\zeta<1\). For a return mechanism holding a fragile component, the question can instead be whether it reaches equilibrium without crossing it. At critical damping, \(c=2\sqrt{mk}\), the characteristic equation is

\[(s+\omega_n)^2=0.\]

The repeated root gives two independent free solutions \(e^{-\omega_nt}\) and \(te^{-\omega_nt}\). Imposing \(x(0)=x_0,\dot x(0)=v_0\) yields

\[x(t)=[x_0+(v_0+\omega_n x_0)t]e^{-\omega_nt}.\]

For release from rest at positive displacement, the bracket stays positive and \(\dot x=-\omega_n^2 x_0t e^{-\omega_nt}\) stays negative for \(t>0\). If a mechanism reaches the same \(x_0\) with negative incoming velocity, the bracket can cross zero once even at critical damping. In particular \(v_0<-\omega_n x_0\) gives crossing time \(-x_0/(v_0+\omega_n x_0)\). Critical damping rules out repeated free oscillations, but the initial velocity still determines whether the target is overshot.

For overdamping the distinct real decay rates are \(\omega_n[\zeta\pm\sqrt{\zeta^2-1}]\). Their product is \(\omega_n^2\). Increasing viscous resistance makes the fast root decay more rapidly but the slow root approach zero. Simply adding damping can consequently prolong the final approach to a sensing threshold.

Select a damper for a spring-return slide

A slide of mass 3.0 kg has a return spring of 300 N/m, so \(\omega_n=10\,\mathrm{rad/s}\) and \(c_{\rm cr}=60\,\mathrm{N\,s/m}\). Released from rest at \(x_0=50\) mm with critical damping, its normalised position is \((1+10t)e^{-10t}\). To enter a 5% band, solve \((1+s)e^{-s}=0.05\) with \(s=10t\). Testing 4.7 and 4.8 gives 0.0518 and 0.0477; the crossing is about 4.74, or \(t=0.474\) s.

The same slide at the same 50 mm location might arrive with \(v_0=-0.80\,\mathrm{m/s}\) after an actuator disconnects. The bracket is now \(0.050-0.30t\), which crosses at 0.167 s. Differentiating gives its negative peak at \(t=0.267\,\mathrm s\), with an overshoot magnitude about 2.08 mm. An optical stop positioned only 1 mm beyond equilibrium would register a crossing even though the damper is critical. Compare that with a slow overdamped return from rest: for \(\zeta=2\) its dominant decay rate falls to \(10(2-\sqrt3)=2.68\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\). An assessment based simply on whether the motion oscillates would overlook both the clearance requirement and this much slower return.

The return time depends on which event the instrumentation calls completion. Some switches trigger when the position first enters a band and remain latched even if it later leaves. For the critical release with negative entering speed, first crossing the equilibrium plainly does not mean motion has stopped. A position-plus-velocity criterion is stricter. At the first zero crossing, the bracket in \(x\) vanishes, so the velocity is its constant derivative multiplied by the exponential, \(-0.30e^{-10(0.167)}=-0.0567\,\mathrm{m/s}\). A slide with a maximum docking speed of 0.020 m/s would fail that condition despite its position being exactly correct at that instant. The appropriate response test must state both allowed path excursion and the speed at any mechanical contact.

Checking only the first displacement peak cannot identify this velocity-limited docking event.

Distinguish sliding-friction decay from a viscous ring-down

Suppose a slide moves on a dry horizontal guide under a spring force \(-kx\). While the slide moves left, kinetic friction \(F_c\) acts right, and \(m\ddot x+kx=+F_c\). The solution is harmonic about the shifted equilibrium \(+F_c/k\). During rightward sliding the equilibrium switches to \(-F_c/k\). A complete undisturbed sliding half-cycle still lasts \(\pi\sqrt{m/k}\), but friction dissipates energy in proportion to distance travelled. At zero speed, static friction can take any value up to \(F_s\), so checking the spring force before starting the next half-cycle is essential.

Let a leftward excursion start at \(x=A>0\) and reach a turning point at \(x=-B\), with \(A,B>0\). The spring energy drop equals friction work,

\[k(A^2-B^2)/2=F_c(A+B).\]

Cancellation leaves \(B=A-2F_c/k\). Same-side peak magnitudes fall by \(4F_c/k\) each complete sliding cycle. This is a constant absolute decrement. A viscous exponential gives a constant ratio instead. If a late half-cycle does not cross zero, use its signed turning-point coordinate \(x_{\rm next}=2F_c/k-x_{\rm start}\) for leftward motion and apply the static criterion there.

A 2 kg slide has \(k=800\,\mathrm{N/m}\), \(F_c=2.0\) N and \(F_s=2.4\) N. Release it from 18 mm. Its initial spring force is 14.4 N, so it moves. Each half-cycle reduces the alternating peak magnitude by 5.0 mm, giving signed positions \(+18,-13,+8,-3\) mm. At −3 mm the spring pull is 2.4 N, equal to the static threshold. Under an ideal static-friction law it can remain there. It took \(3\pi\sqrt{2/800}=0.471\) s to reach that position. The final displacement need not be zero. Interpreting its first two positive peaks with a logarithmic decrement would give a damping estimate that fails to predict the finite stop.

Practise on sheet 1 · Identify a dry guide from three same-side peaks. Further reading · IIT Kharagpur · Aircraft Structural Dynamics · Module 4, Lecture 3, Free Vibration of Damped System (Contd.), “Coulomb Damping”.

Estimate damping from repeated peaks

Release an initially displaced mass with zero initial velocity. The underdamped waveform contains both cosine and sine terms; the latter enforces zero initial velocity. Compare successive same-sign peaks using a logarithmic decrement.

Parametric resonance under periodic stiffness modulation · Vibration and identification in machinery

A carriage supported by a spring whose stiffness changes periodically can gain oscillation energy with no additive force at its natural frequency. Compare this mechanism with an ordinary forced resonance, estimate the first instability band and learn how to measure growth before nonlinear saturation invalidates the prediction.

Problem sheet 1 · Equivalent inertia and ring-down

Refer all energy terms to one coordinate, then use measured peaks to identify a vibrating support.

1. Accelerate a conveyor

A roller of inertia 0.080 kg·m² and radius 0.12 m drives an effective translating belt mass of 30 kg without slip. A constant resisting belt force of 40 N acts. (a) Find the roller-referred inertia. (b) Find the torque for belt acceleration 0.60 m/s². (c) At belt speed 1.20 m/s, check the input power against friction and the kinetic-energy increase.

Reduce and check the drive

The inertia is \(0.080+30(0.12)^2=0.512\) kg·m². Angular acceleration is \(0.60/0.12=5.0\) rad/s² and the resistance torque is \(40(0.12)=4.80\) N·m. The total torque is

\[4.80+0.512(5)=7.36\,\mathrm{N\,m}.\]

At 10 rad/s this supplies 73.6 W. Resistance consumes 48.0 W and kinetic energy increases at \(0.512(10)(5)=25.6\) W, giving the same total.

2. Identify an unknown damper

A 12 kg instrument rings down from 6 mm to 3 mm over exactly three full cycles taking 0.75 s. (a) Infer \(\omega_d\) and envelope rate \(\alpha\). (b) Obtain \(k,c,\zeta\). (c) Predict when the envelope will reach 1.5 mm and state one measurement that could falsify the viscous model.

Fit the measured peaks

The period is 0.25 s, so \(\omega_d=25.13\) rad/s. The envelope satisfies \(\alpha=\ln(6/3)/0.75=0.924\) s⁻¹. Thus \(c=2m\alpha=22.2\) N·s/m, \(k=m(\omega_d^2+\alpha^2)=7.59\) kN/m and \(\zeta=0.0367\). Another 0.75 s halves the envelope again, so 1.5 mm is expected 1.50 s after the first peak. If successive same-sign peak ratios change substantially above the noise floor, a constant viscous damper does not explain the trace by itself.

3. Identify an effective mass with an added mass

A nearly undamped mount oscillates at 5.00 Hz. A compact added mass of 4.50 kg lowers the frequency to 4.00 Hz. (a) Infer the original effective mass. (b) Find the spring stiffness. (c) Estimate the static additional sag and identify the assumption needed to compare with a ruler.

Solve the two frequency equations

If the stiffness does not change, \((5/4)^2=(m+4.50)/m\), giving \(m=8.00\) kg. Therefore \(k=8(2\pi5)^2=7.90\) kN/m. The extra weight gives sag \(4.5(9.81)/7896=5.59\) mm for a vertical mount. This comparison requires the measured displacement and added mass to participate in the same coordinate and the spring to remain linear over the extra static travel.

Identify a dry guide from three same-side peaks

A slide with stiffness 1.20 kN/m has successive positive maxima at 21, 15 and 9 mm before sticking occurs. Find the kinetic friction and predict its following negative turning point. Would it restart from there if static friction can supply 8 N?

Show the worked solution

The constant full-cycle decrement is 6 mm, hence

\[F_c=k(0.006)/4=1.80\,\mathrm{N}.\]

The half-cycle decrement is 3 mm, so a release from +9 mm reaches −6 mm. There the spring force is \(1200(0.006)=7.2\) N, within the 8 N static limit. It remains at −6 mm. The 9 mm positive peak can start this last half-cycle because its 10.8 N spring force exceeds the same static bound.

Revisit chapter 2 · Distinguish sliding-friction decay from a viscous ring-down.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: Coordinates, energy and equivalent inertia; Free vibration, energy loss and ring-down data. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Chapter 3 · Harmonic forcing, phase and dissipated power

Predict the steady vibration under a rotating force and relate the phase lag to the power absorbed by damping.

From first principles

Obtain forced vibration amplitude from dynamic equilibrium

A machine on a linear spring and viscous damper is driven by force \(F_0\cos\omega t\) on its mass \(m\). Newton's second law sums inertia, damping and stiffness forces. Assume a stable mount and wait until the transient part has decayed. Seek a sinusoidal response at the forcing frequency, written \(x(t)=\operatorname{Re}[Xe^{i\omega t}]\) to retain both magnitude and phase.

\[m\ddot x+c\dot x+kx=F_0\cos\omega t,\qquad (k-m\omega^2+ic\omega)X=F_0 .\]

The real part of the dynamic stiffness combines spring and inertia, while the damping contribution is in quadrature. Taking its modulus gives displacement amplitude; taking its argument gives the lag of displacement behind the applied force.

\[|X|=\frac{F_0}{\sqrt{(k-m\omega^2)^2+(c\omega)^2}},\]
\[\phi=\operatorname{atan2}(c\omega,k-m\omega^2).\]

At low frequency the displacement approaches static deflection \(F_0/k\). Near \(\sqrt{k/m}\) the spring and inertia terms cancel, leaving damping to limit the steady response. High-frequency response falls approximately as \(F_0/(m\omega^2)\) for a fixed force amplitude.

Background · Damped frequency
\[\omega_d=\omega_n\sqrt{1-\zeta^2}\]

This free-decay oscillation frequency exists in the underdamped regime. A resonance peak in a driven response uses a different frequency condition.

Derivation and applications

Separate displacement resonance from maximum absorbed power

The frequency of largest displacement can differ from the frequency of largest mean heating in a damper. Drive a single mass \(m\) on a linear spring \(k\) and viscous damper \(c\) with a fixed peak force \(F\) at angular frequency \(\omega\). Assume sinusoidal steady state, a rigid base and coefficients independent of amplitude. With complex displacement amplitude \(X\), mean energy dissipated per unit time is \(c\) times mean squared velocity. Force equilibrium gives

\[X=\frac{F}{k-m\omega^2+ic\omega},\]
\[\bar P=\frac12c\omega^2|X|^2 .\]

Rewrite the power by dividing numerator and denominator by \(\omega^2\). The resulting mechanical impedance has a real dissipative part \(c\) and a reactive part \(m\omega-k/\omega\). The power peak occurs where this latter term vanishes.

\[\bar P=\frac{cF^2/2}{c^2+(m\omega-k/\omega)^2},\]
\[\omega_{\rm peak}=\sqrt{k/m},\]
\[\bar P_{\max}=\frac{F^2}{2c}.\]

At half maximum the magnitude of the reactive term equals \(c\). The positive solutions of the two resulting quadratic equations give lower and upper half-power frequencies. Their difference is exact for this force-driven, velocity-power measurement.

\[\omega_\pm=\frac{\sqrt{c^2+4mk}\pm c}{2m},\]
\[\omega_+-\omega_-=\frac{c}{m},\]
\[\omega_+\omega_-=\frac{k}{m}.\]

By comparison, the displacement peak contains the additional \(1/\omega\) in converting velocity to displacement, so applying these exact bandwidth relations to a displacement trace requires care.

Worked example

Predict the heating bandwidth

Use \(m=2.00\) kg, \(k=800\) N/m, \(c=8.00\) N s/m and \(F=4.00\) N. The peak is at 20.0 rad/s and maximum mean dissipation is

\[16/16=1.00\,\mathrm{W}.\]

The half-power frequencies are 18.10 and 22.10 rad/s. At the peak, velocity is in phase with force and has peak amplitude \(F/c=0.500\) m/s. The corresponding displacement peak amplitude at this frequency is 25.0 mm.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Identify spring and damper from two half-power points

Another oscillator of mass 1.50 kg has measured half-power frequencies of 12.0 and 18.0 rad/s. Their difference gives \(c=1.50(6.00)=9.00\) N s/m and their product gives

\[k=1.50(12)(18)=324\,\mathrm{N/m}.\]

The predicted power peak is at the geometric mean, \(\sqrt{216}=14.7\) rad/s. A direct power sweep can test this prediction without a calibrated displacement amplitude.

Open this live calculation

Extract one sinusoidal component

A slightly eccentric rotating part exerts a periodic force on its housing. Measure the housing displacement from static equilibrium and first represent a single transverse force component by \(F_0\cos\omega t\). Linear response then satisfies

\[m\ddot x+c\dot x+kx=F_0\cos\omega t.\]

Its total solution contains the free transient plus a steady periodic part. Wait several decay times before comparing measured peak amplitudes with a harmonic prediction.

Write the steady part as \(\mathrm{Re}\{Xe^{i\omega t}\}\), taking the real force phasor \(F_0\) as phase reference. Each differentiation contributes \(i\omega\). The displacement phasor therefore satisfies

\[(k-m\omega^2+i c\omega)X=F_0.\]

The factor in parentheses is dynamic stiffness in N/m. At low speed the phasor is close to \(F_0/k\). At high speed the inertia term dominates and displacement is almost opposite the applied force. A phase measurement can distinguish those regimes even if their displacement amplitudes happen to be equal.

Read magnitude and phase separately

Introduce \(r=\omega/\omega_n\), \(\omega_n=\sqrt{k/m}\) and \(\zeta=c/(2m\omega_n)\). Take the magnitude and argument of the dynamic stiffness to obtain

\[|X|/(F_0/k)=[(1-r^2)^2+(2\zeta r)^2]^{-1/2}.\]

The displacement lags by \(\phi=\operatorname{atan2}(2\zeta r,1-r^2)\). At \(r=1\) elastic and inertia forces cancel in phase and the remaining quadrature force is viscous. The displacement lags by 90 degrees and has amplitude \(F_0/(c\omega_n)\).

For constant force amplitude, maximum displacement occurs at \(r=\sqrt{1-2\zeta^2}\) if \(\zeta<1/\sqrt2\). This follows by differentiating the squared denominator with respect to \(r\). Thus the frequency of greatest displacement need not coincide exactly with the undamped natural frequency. A rotating imbalance also changes force amplitude with speed, so its peak cannot be inferred from a constant-force plot alone.

Check a resonant measurement by energy

Let \(m=10\) kg, \(k=4000\) N/m, \(c=40\) N·s/m and \(F_0=8.0\) N. Then \(\omega_n=20\) rad/s and \(\zeta=0.10\). At resonance the predicted displacement amplitude is

\[8/[40(20)]=10.0\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

The velocity amplitude is 0.200 m/s. The time-average input power of a sinusoidal force and velocity is half the product of their peak magnitudes times the cosine of their phase difference. Since velocity is in phase with force at this frequency, the average is 0.800 W.

Independently, the damper dissipates \(\overline P=c\,\overline{\dot x^2}=c\omega^2|X|^2/2=0.800\) W. The spring returns the energy it stores each cycle and the mass has no net change in kinetic energy. If force and displacement peaks are simply multiplied and reported as watts, the dimensions and the missing phase both reveal the error.

Apply the result to a rotor

A rotating eccentric mass \(m_e\) displaced a radius \(e\) generates force amplitude \(m_e e\omega^2\) when its circular motion is prescribed. With \(m_e e=0.020\,\mathrm{kg\,m}\) and \(\omega=20\) rad/s, this is the 8.0 N used above. At 40 rad/s the applied force grows to 32 N. The dynamic stiffness magnitude becomes \(\sqrt{(4000-10(40)^2)^2+(40(40))^2}=12.1\) kN/m, giving about 2.64 mm displacement. The force grew fourfold while the response fell because the system moved away from resonance. A speed sweep therefore needs both the changing excitation and the changing dynamic stiffness.

Estimate damping from a frequency sweep

A useful second identification uses the vibration velocity at constant force amplitude. At resonance the velocity amplitude is \(F_0/c\). At the two frequencies where mean dissipated power has fallen to half its maximum, the velocity amplitude is \(F_0/(\sqrt2 c)\). Inverting the velocity response gives

\[|k/\omega-m\omega|=c.\]

Solving the two sign cases and subtracting gives the bandwidth \(\omega_2-\omega_1=c/m\) for this viscous single-mode system. In the numerical example the difference is 4 rad/s; the predicted limits are about 18.10 and 22.10 rad/s. This result applies to the power or velocity response at constant force amplitude. A sweep driven by a growing rotor imbalance would also require correcting the measured amplitudes for their changing excitation.

Decompose a nonsinusoidal periodic machine force

A cam, pump or reciprocating actuator can generate a nearly rectangular force over each cycle. For a linear system in periodic steady operation, expand this force into Fourier components and apply the dynamic stiffness separately at each harmonic. The construction assumes linear coefficients over the motion range and that the free transient has decayed. A periodic input does not imply that the fundamental component dominates the measured displacement; a higher harmonic near a natural frequency can be amplified strongly.

For a symmetric square force \(F(t)=F_s\,\operatorname{sgn}(\sin\Omega t)\), the mean vanishes and half-wave symmetry cancels all even harmonics. Its sine coefficient over one period is \(b_n=(2/\pi)\int_0^\pi F_s\sin(n\theta)d\theta\) for the half-wave-symmetric terms, giving \(4F_s/(n\pi)\) for odd \(n\). Each odd harmonic has its own displacement amplitude and phase:

\[X_n=\frac{4F_s/(n\pi)}{\sqrt{[k-m(n\Omega)^2]^2+(cn\Omega)^2}},\]
\[\phi_n=\operatorname{atan2}(cn\Omega,k-m(n\Omega)^2).\]

Reconstruct the time history by adding \(X_n\sin(n\Omega t-\phi_n)\). Adding the magnitudes gives a peak upper bound, not generally the actual peak because phase shifts differ. Orthogonality gives an exact mean-square contribution \(X_n^2/2\) for each distinct harmonic, providing a convenient comparison of vibration levels without finding coincident maxima.

Identify the dominant vibration in an indexing machine

Take \(m=5.0\,\mathrm{kg}\), \(k=4500\,\mathrm{N/m}\), and \(c=9.0\,\mathrm{N\,s/m}\), so \(\omega_n=30\,\mathrm{rad/s}\) and \(\zeta=0.03\). The force alternates between ±12 N at fundamental \(\Omega=10\,\mathrm{rad/s}\). The first-harmonic force is

\[48/\pi=15.28\,\mathrm N.\]

Its dynamic stiffness magnitude is \(\sqrt{4000^2+90^2}=4001\,\mathrm{N/m}\), giving \(X_1=3.82\,\mathrm{mm}\) with a small lag. The third harmonic has force 5.09 N but falls exactly at the natural frequency, giving \(X_3=5.09/(9\times30)=18.9\,\mathrm{mm}\) at 90° lag.

At 50 rad/s the fifth component has force 3.06 N and stiffness magnitude \(\sqrt{8000^2+450^2}\), giving only 0.381 mm. The first three nonzero harmonics give RMS displacement \(\sqrt{(3.82^2+18.9^2+0.381^2)/2}=13.6\,\mathrm{mm}\). The largest measured spectral peak would appear at three times the operating frequency. It could arise from the drive’s waveform rather than a separate three-times-speed rotating fault. Altering the rise time of the periodic force can reduce high-order Fourier coefficients, while adding damping around this resonance reduces the mechanical amplification. Which intervention addresses the observed peak can be tested by measuring the force spectrum as well as the motion.

The energy entering the damping element gives a further spectral check because high frequencies carry more velocity for the same displacement. The mean dissipated power of harmonic \(n\) is \(c(n\Omega X_n)^2/2\). In the example the first harmonic dissipates about \(9(10\times0.00382)^2/2=0.0066\) W, while the third dissipates \(9(30\times0.0189)^2/2=1.45\) W. The third harmonic therefore dominates heating even more strongly than it dominates displacement. A sensor attached near a nodal point could suppress a local displacement reading, but the overall energy dissipation still has to be provided by the drive. Synchronous force and velocity measurements over a whole period can independently check this average input power.

A centred force–velocity recording also excludes a false average power component introduced by a sensor’s DC offset.

Which speed excites an unbalanced rotor most strongly?

A rotating unbalance excites a single lateral mode with force \(m_e e\omega^2\). Divide the harmonic displacement by \(m_e e/m\). The resulting factor has \(r^2\) in its numerator because the exciting force grows with speed.

Random base motion, spectral density and rms response · Vibration and identification in machinery

A support vibration is described by a power spectral density rather than a single sine-wave amplitude. Derive the relative-motion filter for a mounted instrument, calculate mean-square motion from a measured spectrum and distinguish a stationary rms prediction from a peak displacement guarantee.

Chapter 4 · Coupled oscillators and normal coordinates

Derive and interpret the two modes of a coupled machine, then decide which mode an applied load can excite.

From first principles

Find natural modes from the free force balance

For small motions of a conservative system with several coordinates, linear inertia forces are represented by a positive-definite mass matrix \(M\) and linear restoring forces by a symmetric stiffness matrix \(K\). Collect the free balance and try a motion in which the coordinate amplitudes stay in one fixed ratio as each oscillates at frequency \(\omega\).

\[M\ddot{\mathbf x}+K\mathbf x=0,\]
\[\mathbf x=\boldsymbol\phi\cos(\omega t)\ \Longrightarrow\ (K-\omega^2 M)\boldsymbol\phi=0 .\]

A nonzero shape \(\boldsymbol\phi\) can solve these homogeneous equations only when the coefficient matrix is singular. The determinant condition gives candidate squared frequencies, and the nullspace at each frequency gives a mode shape up to scale.

\[\det(K-\omega^2M)=0,\]
\[K\boldsymbol\phi_j=\omega_j^2M\boldsymbol\phi_j .\]

For identical masses linked by a spring, a common translation leaves that link unstretched and an opposite motion stretches it from both ends. This observation identifies common and differential shapes even before evaluating the determinant. Initial displacement and velocity select their amplitudes; the eigenproblem identifies the motions the structure can support.

Derivation and applications

Quantify the splitting of two weakly coupled modes

When two machines have the same uncoupled natural frequency, adding a weak connecting spring splits that frequency into two. Consider identical sliders of mass \(m\) each attached to ground by stiffness \(k\) and linked to each other by stiffness \(k_c\). Neglect damping and choose displacements in the same direction from equilibrium. The coupling spring responds only to the displacement difference. Adding and subtracting the two force balances therefore gives separate equations for \(s=(x_1+x_2)/2\) and \(d=(x_1-x_2)/2\).

\[m\ddot s+ks=0,\]
\[m\ddot d+(k+2k_c)d=0.\]

Release slider one at \(A\) and slider two at zero, both from rest. The two modal coordinates then both start at \(A/2\). Write \(\omega_s=\sqrt{k/m}\) and \(\omega_d=\sqrt{(k+2k_c)/m}\) and combine their solutions.

\[x_1=\tfrac A2(\cos\omega_st+\cos\omega_dt),\]
\[x_2=\tfrac A2(\cos\omega_st-\cos\omega_dt).\]

Introduce \(\bar\omega=(\omega_s+\omega_d)/2\) and \(\Delta\omega=\omega_d-\omega_s\). The sum-to-product identities separate the fast motion from slow modulation. When the split is small, the modulation resembles a repeated transfer of motion between the sliders.

\[x_1=A\cos(\bar\omega t)\cos(\Delta\omega t/2),\]
\[x_2=A\sin(\bar\omega t)\sin(\Delta\omega t/2).\]

The first minimum of the slider-one displacement envelope occurs at \(t=\pi/\Delta\omega\). Its instantaneous velocity there need not vanish because the envelope itself is changing. The envelope timing remains identifiable from repeated peaks at weak coupling.

Worked example

Time the exchange of motion

For \(m=1.00\) kg, \(k=100\) N/m and \(k_c=10.5\) N/m, the two frequencies are 10.0 and 11.0 rad/s. The first envelope minimum appears at 3.14 s and the first full recovery of its magnitude at 6.28 s. Releasing the first slider by 8.00 mm sets both modal initial amplitudes to 4.00 mm. Releasing both sliders together instead excites only the slower common motion.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Estimate coupling from a beat measurement

A second pair has mass 2.00 kg per slider and measured lower frequency 25.0 rad/s. Consecutive magnitude maxima of one slider's envelope occur 5.00 s apart. Thus

\[\Delta\omega=2\pi/5.00=1.257\,\mathrm{rad/s},\]
\[k_c=m[(25+1.257)^2-25^2]/2=64.4\,\mathrm{N/m}.\]

The independently inferred grounding stiffness is \(m\omega_s^2=1250\) N/m, making the connector about 5.2 percent as stiff.

Start from this chapter. A mode sensitive to rotary inertia carries appreciable rotational kinetic energy. Compare the fractional change in each eigenfrequency under the same imposed change of tip inertia.

OneSimulation laboratory · Separate the effect of mass from rotary inertia

Two masses allow two independent shapes

Two identical instruments sit on separate spring mounts and are connected by a flexible link. Pull both by the same amount and the link keeps its natural length. Pull them in opposite directions and the link stretches twice as much as either displacement. The two tests reveal distinct restoring mechanisms. A model with one equivalent spring and the sum of both masses would discard one of them.

Let \(x_1,x_2\) be horizontal displacements from equilibrium. Each mass is \(m\), each ground spring has stiffness \(k\), and the coupling spring has stiffness \(k_c\). With light springs, small displacements and no damping, the kinetic energy is \(m(\dot x_1^2+\dot x_2^2)/2\) and the elastic energy is \(k(x_1^2+x_2^2)/2+k_c(x_1-x_2)^2/2\). The coupling energy contains the difference of displacements because it is the extension between its ends.

Separate space from time

Taking the derivative of elastic energy with respect to each coordinate gives \(m\ddot x_1+(k+k_c)x_1-k_cx_2=0\) and the symmetric second equation. Look for \(\mathbf x=\boldsymbol\phi\cos\omega t\). Nonzero modal shapes solve \((\mathbf K-\omega^2\mathbf M)\boldsymbol\phi=0\). For this pair the determinant equation is

\[(k+k_c-m\omega^2)^2-k_c^2=0.\]

Taking both signs of the square root gives

\[\omega_1^2=k/m,\]
\[\omega_2^2=(k+2k_c)/m.\]

Substitution back yields shapes proportional to \((1,1)\) and \((1,-1)\). For the first, the connector transmits no incremental force. For the second, the connector acts strongly. Reversing both signs of a plotted eigenvector or multiplying it by a nonzero number describes the same physical mode.

Predict a release test at both instruments

Take \(m=5.0\) kg, \(k=2000\) N/m and \(k_c=1500\) N/m. The two angular frequencies are 20.0 and 31.62 rad/s, or 3.18 and 5.03 Hz. Pull only the first instrument 4.0 mm and release from rest. The initial displacement vector equals \(2(1,1)+2(1,-1)\) mm. Each modal amplitude starts at its corresponding value with zero initial velocity. Hence

\[x_1(t)=2\cos(20t)+2\cos(31.62t),\]
\[x_2(t)=2\cos(20t)-2\cos(31.62t)\]

in millimetres. At zero time the second displacement vanishes while the connector exerts 6.0 N on its mass. Its initial acceleration is 1.2 m/s² toward the first instrument’s displacement. This apparent transfer of motion needs neither forcing nor damping: modal displacements add and cancel differently at the two observation points.

Distinguish available modes from visible modes

Define average and relative coordinates \(q_s=(x_1+x_2)/2\) and \(q_a=(x_1-x_2)/2\). Adding or subtracting the original equations decouples them. Equal external forces excite only \(q_s\); equal and opposite forces excite only \(q_a\). Taking the signal \((x_1+x_2)/2\) likewise hides the antisymmetric mode. A missing spectral peak can therefore reveal the symmetry of a load or sensor arrangement, even though the corresponding eigenmode still exists.

If the two masses differ, the simple sum and difference cease to diagonalise the equations. The general route is to form the physical mass and stiffness matrices and solve the symmetric generalised eigenproblem. Distinct modes of an undamped conservative system are orthogonal with respect to mass, \(\boldsymbol\phi_i^T\mathbf M\boldsymbol\phi_j=0\). This weighted orthogonality is what permits a measured motion or distributed load to be projected into separate modal coordinates.

Predict which mode responds to adding rotary inertia

For a unit mode shape \(\phi\), the quotient \(\omega^2=\phi^TK\phi/(\phi^TM\phi)\) compares stored elastic energy with inertia. Increasing inertia at a coordinate matters most when that coordinate has appreciable motion in the mode. A frame with three tip coordinates offers a way to check this reasoning beyond the symmetric two-mass illustration.

Compare two changes to the same frame: double only the tip mass from 200 to 400 kg, then restore it and double only the tip rotary inertia from 60 to 120 kg m². Predict whether either change can raise a natural frequency for the same stiffness and compute the fractional shift of the third mode in each case. Use the negligible-member-mass row throughout. Check the frame frequency measurements.

Derive bounce and pitch modes of a supported machine

A long machine supported on two mounts can translate and rotate as a nearly rigid body even if there is negligible frame bending. Let the mass centre move vertically by \(z\) and rotate by small angle \(\theta\), with positive rotation raising a point to the right. Vertical spring deflections relative to ground are \(z-a\theta\) on the left and \(z+b\theta\) on the right. Measure displacements from static equilibrium. The kinetic energy is \((m\dot z^2+I_G\dot\theta^2)/2\), and the spring energy is \([k_L(z-a\theta)^2+k_R(z+b\theta)^2]/2\).

Differentiating potential energy gives stiffness terms \(K_{zz}=k_L+k_R,\ K_{\theta\theta}=k_La^2+k_Rb^2,\ K_{z\theta}=-k_La+k_Rb\). The mass matrix is diagonal because translation was measured at the centre of mass. A vertical translation produces a moment whenever the weighted mount locations fail to balance, and a pure rotation then also produces a vertical force by reciprocity. If \(k_La=k_Rb\) the modes are pure translation and pure pitch. Equal spring rates alone do not guarantee decoupling when the lever arms differ.

For free motion solve \((K-\omega^2 M)\phi=0\). In mixed coordinates, metres and radians label different displacement entries, so a numerical component ratio needs units or a stated length scale. Measured vertical motions at two actual sensor locations provide an unambiguous observable mode shape.

Separate a heaving mode from a rocking mode

Let \(m=200\) kg, \(I_G=40\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}\), \(a=b=0.50\) m and mount rates \(k_L=30000,\ k_R=50000\,\mathrm{N/m}\). The stiffness entries are 80000 N/m, 20000 N m/rad and 10000 N in the mixed term. Setting the determinant to zero gives \((80000-200\lambda)(20000-40\lambda)-10000^2=0\) with \(\lambda=\omega^2\). Dividing through by 8000 yields

\[\lambda^2-900\lambda+187500=0.\]

The squared frequencies are 327.5 and 572.5 s⁻², giving 2.88 and 3.81 Hz.

For the lower mode the first row implies

\[\theta/z=-(80000-200\times327.5)/10000=-1.45\,\mathrm{rad/m}.\]

If mass-centre motion has 1 mm amplitude, the mount-point displacements are 1.725 and 0.275 mm, in phase but strongly unequal. The higher mode gives \(\theta/z=+3.45\,\mathrm{rad/m}\), so the left point now moves oppositely to the right. An accelerometer placed close to a mode’s nodal point can scarcely detect it although that mode remains physically present. For the low mode the vertical node lies about \(1/1.45=0.69\) m to the right of the centre, beyond the right mount. Two well-separated measurement points make the rotational contribution identifiable.

A static load experiment can identify the stiffness coupling before a modal test. Apply a known vertical force \(F\) at the centre and no external moment. The pitch-equilibrium equation gives \(\theta=-K_{z\theta}z/K_{\theta\theta}=-0.50z\) rad per metre for these mounts. Substitution into the vertical balance leaves effective static stiffness \(80000-10000^2/20000=75000\,\mathrm{N/m}\). Under 150 N centre load, predicted motion is 2.00 mm and pitch −0.0010 rad, giving left and right mount readings of 2.50 and 1.50 mm. A centre gauge alone would only indicate an apparent softening, while the unequal pair exposes the rotational coupling that later appears in the two mode shapes.

Find a sensor position that can see a mode

Use the first three bending modes of an ideal simply supported uniform beam as a continuous analogue of coupled oscillators. A local displacement transducer reports a modal coordinate multiplied by the signed shape at the measurement location.

Why a local damper couples otherwise separate modes · Vibration and identification in machinery

Undamped mode shapes diagonalise a symmetric mass and stiffness model. Adding a damper at one attachment does not generally leave modal equations separate. Derive the resulting coupling, test its magnitude and learn when a diagonal modal-damping approximation can hide an observable phase error.

Problem sheet 2 · Frequency response and modes

Use complex dynamic stiffness and modal symmetry to predict what a force and a sensor can observe.

1. Inspect a forced oscillator

A 2.0 kg mass on a 200 N/m spring and 4.0 N·s/m damper is driven at 10 rad/s by a 1.0 N peak sinusoidal force. (a) Find the displacement amplitude and phase. (b) Find average power. (c) Repeat the amplitude calculation at 5 rad/s with the same force.

Solve the phasor balance

The natural angular frequency is 10 rad/s. At this speed the real dynamic stiffness vanishes, giving \(|X|=1/(4\cdot10)=0.0250\) m and 90° displacement lag. The velocity peak is 0.250 m/s and average input power is

\[1(0.25)/2=0.125\,\mathrm{W}.\]

At 5 rad/s the dynamic stiffness is \(150+20i\) N/m, hence

\[|X|=1/\sqrt{150^2+20^2}=6.61\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Proximity to resonance has a larger effect than the static-force displacement alone suggests.

2. Invert measured modal frequencies

Two identical 4.0 kg masses have equal ground springs and a shared coupling spring. Their measured angular frequencies are 10 rad/s in phase and 16 rad/s in opposition. (a) Infer both stiffnesses. (b) Apply displacements +3 mm and −1 mm. Decompose this state into the two modes. (c) Predict the average displacement over time after release from rest.

Separate symmetric and antisymmetric coordinates

The first mode gives

\[k=m\omega_s^2=400\,\mathrm{N/m}.\]

The other gives

\[k_c=(4(16)^2-400)/2=312\,\mathrm{N/m}.\]

The initial mean is 1 mm and the initial half-difference is 2 mm, corresponding to \(1(1,1)+2(1,-1)\) mm. With zero initial velocities both modes start as cosines. Averaging the two signals removes the antisymmetric term, leaving \(1\,\mathrm{mm}\cos(10t)\). A single average sensor would miss a mode that is actually populated.

3. Choose the force pattern

Use the preceding system. Two actuators apply harmonic force amplitudes \(F\) and \(-F\) simultaneously. (a) Find the drive of the mean coordinate and half-difference coordinate. (b) Which frequency is resonant? (c) If one actuator fails, which resonances can now appear?

Project the load into each mode

Adding the two physical equations and dividing by two gives forcing \((F_1+F_2)/2\) for the mean, which is zero. Subtracting gives \((F_1-F_2)/2=F\) for the half-difference. Only the antisymmetric mode is driven, at 16 rad/s. With one actuator operating, the force pattern decomposes as \((F,0)=(F/2)(1,1)+(F/2)(1,-1)\), so both 10 and 16 rad/s can produce resonant responses. Real damping limits amplitudes in a steady sweep.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: Harmonic forcing, phase and dissipated power; Coupled oscillators and normal coordinates. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Estimate how a frame mode distributes its kinetic energy

For the highest tabulated mode, doubling tip translation mass lowers its frequency from 44.645 to 42.379 Hz. Doubling its rotary inertia instead lowers it to 34.791 Hz. Freeze the baseline mode shape for an estimate. Derive the fraction of its original modal mass associated with each changed contribution. Compare the estimates and state what the approximation omits.

Work through the solution

The Rayleigh quotient is \(\omega^2=K_{\rm mode}/M_{\rm mode}\). For an unchanged shape, doubling one contribution \(M_i\) replaces the denominator with \(M_{\rm mode}+M_i\). Thus

\[M_i/M_{\rm mode}=(f_0/f_i)^2-1.\]

Translation gives 0.110 and rotation 0.647. The much stronger rotary sensitivity is observable from the computed frequencies even if the frozen-shape estimates are imperfect. A finite change in the mass matrix can rotate the eigenvectors, so these fractions are estimates rather than an exact decomposition of modal kinetic energy.

Chapter 5 · Base excitation and vibration isolation

Distinguish absolute motion, mount travel and transmitted force when choosing a support for a moving base.

From first principles

Distinguish mount travel from the absolute motion of the mass

A moving foundation imposes a position rather than a force directly on a machine mass. Let \(y(t)\) denote base displacement, \(x(t)\) absolute mass displacement, and take positive directions alike. A spring of stiffness \(k\) and damper of coefficient \(c\) act through relative deformation and relative velocity. Newton's second law therefore gives

\[m\ddot x=-k(x-y)-c(\dot x-\dot y).\]

For steady sinusoidal base motion at frequency \(\omega\), replace each derivative by \(i\omega\). Group absolute displacement terms on the left and prescribed motion on the right to obtain the transfer between their complex amplitudes.

\[\frac XY=\frac{k+ic\omega}{k-m\omega^2+ic\omega},\]
\[\frac{X-Y}{Y}=\frac{m\omega^2}{k-m\omega^2+ic\omega}.\]

At low frequency the mass follows the base and mount travel approaches zero. At sufficiently high frequency absolute motion decreases, while the mount must accommodate nearly the full base displacement. A clearance sensor spanning the mount and an inertial sensor attached to the mass therefore respond to different quantities during the same isolation experiment.

Derivation and applications

Choose damping by comparing two isolation frequencies

Damping can limit a resonant motion while increasing high-frequency vibration transmitted through a mount. Consider a machine mass \(m\) on a spring \(k\) and damper \(c\) in parallel, driven by a harmonic force on the mass with rigid support. Take \(\omega_n=\sqrt{k/m}\),

\[\zeta=c/(2m\omega_n),\]
\[r=\omega/\omega_n.\]

The complex force transmitted into the support equals \((k+ic\omega)X\), where \(X\) is the steady displacement. Dividing by the applied force gives

\[T_F^2=\left|\frac{F_{\rm base}}{F_{\rm drive}}\right|^2=\frac{1+4\zeta^2r^2}{(1-r^2)^2+4\zeta^2r^2}.\]

At a fixed nonzero frequency define \(h=4\zeta^2r^2\). Differentiating in \(h\) leaves a numerator independent of damping. Its sign determines whether the transmitted fraction increases or decreases as damping is added.

\[\frac{\partial T_F^2}{\partial h}=\frac{(1-r^2)^2-1}{[(1-r^2)^2+h]^2}=\frac{r^2(r^2-2)}{[(1-r^2)^2+h]^2}.\]

For \(r<\sqrt2\), added damping lowers transmissibility; above \(\sqrt2\) it raises it. At the boundary all damping choices give unity. A limit specified at resonance turns directly into a minimum damping ratio because the spring and inertial force cancel in the displacement denominator there.

\[T_F(1)=\frac{\sqrt{1+4\zeta^2}}{2\zeta},\]
\[\zeta_{\min}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{T_{\rm lim}^2-1}}\quad(T_{\rm lim}>1).\]

These results assume the same applied force amplitude across frequencies. A rotating imbalance has a force magnitude proportional to frequency squared, requiring that additional factor when predicting an absolute support load.

Worked example

Trade a resonant limit against running isolation

A mount must keep resonant support force below three times the drive amplitude. The minimum damping ratio is

\[1/(2\sqrt8)=0.1768.\]

At operating ratio \(r=3.00\) this choice gives \(h=1.125\) and \(T_F=\sqrt{2.125/65.125}=0.181\). A 50.0 N peak harmonic force then transmits 9.03 N at the operating frequency.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Determine whether more damping can meet a running target

Another design runs at \(r=2.00\). Its present \(\zeta=0.250\) gives \(h=1.00\) and \(T_F=\sqrt{2/10}=0.447\). To reach \(T_F=0.400\), solve \((1+h)/(9+h)=0.160\), giving \(h=0.524\) and \(\zeta=\sqrt h/(2r)=0.181\). The reduction of damping improves the running value but raises the predicted resonant ratio to about 2.94.

A moving foundation is a different input

Place a microscope on a spring-and-damper mount above a vibrating floor. An accelerometer on its body reports absolute acceleration, whereas a displacement gauge connected across the mount reports relative displacement. These can be very different. First write both coordinates in the same inertial frame. Let \(y(t)\) be the motion of the floor and \(x(t)\) the motion of the instrument. The spring force on the mass is \(-k(x-y)\) and the damper force is \(-c(\dot x-\dot y)\).

For mass \(m\), Newton’s law therefore becomes

\[m\ddot x+c(\dot x-\dot y)+k(x-y)=0.\]

A floor that moves sufficiently slowly carries the mass along with almost no relative motion. A floor moving very rapidly can move substantially beneath a nearly stationary mass. Whether the design is successful depends on which motion is constrained by the application.

Derive two transfer functions

For harmonic input \(y=\mathrm{Re}(Ye^{i\omega t})\), substitute \(x=\mathrm{Re}(Xe^{i\omega t})\). Collect terms in \(X\) and \(Y\). Writing \(r=\omega/\sqrt{k/m}\) and \(\zeta=c/(2\sqrt{km})\) gives

\[\frac{X}{Y}=\frac{1+i2\zeta r}{1-r^2+i2\zeta r},\]
\[\frac{X-Y}{Y}=\frac{r^2}{1-r^2+i2\zeta r}.\]

Subtract the complex quantities before taking magnitudes. In general the magnitude of the relative displacement is not the difference of the absolute amplitudes, since the motions can have different phase angles. The acceleration ratio for harmonics equals \(|X/Y|\) because both displacements acquire the same factor \(-\omega^2\). That shortcut is inapplicable to arbitrary time records without decomposition into frequencies.

Compare the squares of numerator and denominator in the first ratio. Transmissibility falls below one when \((1-r^2)^2>1\), or \(r>\sqrt2\) for positive frequency. The crossing is independent of damping in this ideal model. Damping reduces the peak around resonance but provides a force path at high frequency, increasing the transmitted motion there compared with a lighter damper.

Choose an isolation range and a clearance

Take \(m=80\) kg and desired undamped frequency \(f_n=4.0\) Hz. Then \(k=m(2\pi f_n)^2=50.5\) kN/m. With \(\zeta=0.15\), the required viscous coefficient is \(c=2m(2\pi f_n)\zeta=603\) N·s/m. Effective isolation begins above \(4\sqrt2=5.66\) Hz. A floor excursion of 0.30 mm amplitude at 12 Hz gives \(r=3\) and \(2\zeta r=0.90\). The absolute ratio is \(\sqrt{1.81/64.81}=0.167\), hence instrument motion is 0.050 mm. The relative amplitude is

\[9(0.30)/\sqrt{64.81}=0.335\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

The smaller number is appropriate for motion performance and the larger for mount clearance.

At 4 Hz the same base displacement gives relative amplitude \(0.30/(2\zeta)=1.00\) mm. Before checking that a stop is far enough away, add the static spring compression \(mg/k=15.5\) mm if the mount is vertical and its available stroke is specified from the unloaded position.

Ask what a sensor can distinguish

Measure floor and instrument motion simultaneously with a shared clock. An isolated mass at high frequency can seem quiet despite substantial relative movement. Conversely, close to resonance both acceleration and support force may be amplified. For harmonic motion the force on the base is the spring-plus-damper force. Its magnitude equals \(m\omega^2|X|\) when no other dynamic forces act on the mass. Comparing it with the measured acceleration is an independent check on force calibration and on the assumption of one moving coordinate.

Calculate what an accelerometer would report

A sensor bolted to the instrument measures absolute acceleration. With harmonic motion its amplitude is \(\omega^2|X|\), even when the clearance requirement is governed by \(|X-Y|\). For the 12 Hz condition, the 0.050 mm instrument displacement gives

\[(2\pi12)^2(0.000050)=0.284\,\mathrm{m/s^2}.\]

The floor acceleration is 1.71 m/s², so the acceleration transmissibility is the same 0.167 as the absolute displacement ratio at this frequency. Inferring spring force from the instrument’s absolute displacement would instead be wrong: the extension of the spring is the relative motion. The damper force also depends on relative velocity, and the two transmitted force components differ in phase.

Derive the response of a mounted accelerometer

An accelerometer is itself a base-excited oscillator. Let its casing follow the machine displacement \(y(t)\) while an internal proof mass moves at \(x(t)\). A spring and viscous element connect the two, and a transducer detects relative motion \(z=x-y\). Because damping and stiffness act on relative movement, Newton’s law gives

\[m\ddot z+c\dot z+kz=-m\ddot y.\]

The instrument manufacturer may scale the output as estimated acceleration \(\hat a=-\omega_n^2z\). This scale is exact at zero frequency in the linear model, but both gain and phase change as frequency approaches the sensor resonance.

For harmonic casing acceleration with phasor \(A\), the transfer from actual to indicated acceleration is

\[\frac{\hat A}{A}=\frac{1}{1-r^2+i2\zeta r},\]
\[r=\omega/\omega_n .\]

At small \(r\), the proof mass is almost in force equilibrium with the spring and the output tracks acceleration. At large \(r\), the proof mass is nearly inertially stationary and the relative displacement approximates negative casing displacement. This is the regime exploited by certain displacement-based seismic instruments. The very same equation permits two measurement principles, but their useful frequency ranges differ.

For \(\zeta=1/\sqrt2\) the squared magnitude denominator simplifies to \(1+r^4\). The leading magnitude error then appears at fourth order, although the phase still changes at first order. A sensor can consequently have an excellent amplitude calibration and a material timing error in the same frequency range.

Select a transducer for a base-isolation measurement

A vibration test uses an accelerometer with undamped frequency 100 Hz and \(\zeta=1/\sqrt2\). At a 30 Hz machine component, \(r=0.30\). The indicated magnitude is \(1/\sqrt{1+0.3^4}=0.9960\) times the true acceleration, whereas the lag is

\[\arctan[\sqrt2(0.30)/(1-0.30^2)]=25.0^\circ.\]

An amplitude requirement of 1% is met, but a phase comparison between this sensor and an independently calibrated force transducer would carry a large offset. For an actual acceleration amplitude of \(4.0\,\mathrm{m/s^2}\), proof-mass travel is \(0.996(4)/(2\pi100)^2=10.1\,\mathrm{\mu m}\), which also has to fit the transducer’s linear travel range.

For the same damping, an amplitude error below 1% requires \(1/\sqrt{1+r^4}\ge0.99\), or \(r\le0.378\). A record extending to 50 Hz would thus require a resonance of at least about 132 Hz by magnitude alone. A 5° phase specification is much stricter: solving \(\sqrt2r/(1-r^2)=\tan5^\circ\) gives \(r\simeq0.0616\), or resonance above about 812 Hz for the 50 Hz component. If two identical, well-matched sensors measure base and payload at the same frequency, much of their common transfer cancels in a ratio. It does not cancel in a phase comparison to another instrument with a different dynamics.

An instrument range may be specified in acceleration rather than proof-mass stroke. Near resonance the displacement per unit base acceleration increases according to the same frequency response. A sensor that remains linear at a given low-frequency acceleration can therefore approach its internal stop at a lower applied acceleration near resonance. Mechanical range and dynamic calibration must be checked together.
