Random base motion, spectral density and rms response · Vibration and identification in machinery

A support vibration is described by a power spectral density rather than a single sine-wave amplitude. Derive the relative-motion filter for a mounted instrument, calculate mean-square motion from a measured spectrum and distinguish a stationary rms prediction from a peak displacement guarantee.

Chapter 6 · Impulse response and finite-duration loading

Construct the response to a short pulse and determine when an impulse approximation preserves the peak motion.

From first principles

Build a transient response by accumulating elementary impulses

An impulse \(J\) is the time integral of applied force. Across an ideal instantaneous impulse the displacement of mass \(m\) stays continuous, while integrating Newton's law gives a velocity jump \(J/m\). Finite spring and viscous forces have vanishing impulse over that ideal interval. For an initially stationary undamped mount with stiffness \(k\), the following motion is free vibration with angular frequency \(\omega_n=\sqrt{k/m}\) and the new initial velocity.

\[x(0^+)=0,\]
\[\dot x(0^+)=J/m,\]
\[x(t)=\frac{J}{m\omega_n}\sin(\omega_nt).\]

Now divide a finite force trace into short intervals. The interval starting at \(s\) contributes impulse \(F(s)\,ds\). For a linear time-invariant system its later displacement is the impulse solution delayed by \(s\) and scaled by that impulse. Summing over all earlier intervals yields

\[x(t)=\frac{1}{m\omega_n}\int_0^t F(s)\sin[\omega_n(t-s)]\,ds .\]

An existing initial displacement or velocity contributes a separate free response by superposition. If the pulse is very short relative to the vibration period, the sine factor changes little during it and the area of the pulse controls the subsequent motion.

Derivation and applications

Obtain the residual vibration after a ramped force is removed

The time integral of a force sets momentum change only when the support forces have little time to act. For longer pulses a linear undamped oscillator retains information about the shape and timing of the load. Consider mass \(m\), stiffness \(k\), natural frequency \(\omega=\sqrt{k/m}\) and zero initial motion. For a force acting only from zero to \(T\), the post-pulse displacement follows the convolution with the impulse response.

\[x(t)=\frac{1}{m\omega}\int_0^T F(s)\sin[\omega(t-s)]ds,\qquad t\geq T.\]

Expand the sine of the difference into sine and cosine of \(t\). The two integrals multiply quadrature components of free vibration. Their resultant magnitude is the modulus of a single Fourier integral at the natural frequency. For a symmetric triangular pulse centred at \(T/2\), its area is \(J=F_pT/2\) and its transform factorises because the triangle is a convolution of two equal rectangles of duration \(T/2\).

\[A=\frac{1}{m\omega}\left|\int_0^T F(s)e^{-i\omega s}ds\right|,\]
\[\int_0^T F(s)e^{-i\omega s}ds=J e^{-i\omega T/2}\left[\frac{\sin(\omega T/4)}{\omega T/4}\right]^2.\]

Each rectangle contributes the average complex phase over its duration; the product of these averages produces the squared factor. The impulse estimate is recovered for a very short triangle, while certain longer pulses leave no residual motion in the ideal model.

\[\frac{A}{J/(m\omega)}=\left[\frac{\sin(\omega T/4)}{\omega T/4}\right]^2,\]
\[T_{\rm first\ zero}=\frac{4\pi}{\omega}.\]

Worked example

Compare a triangular pulse with its impulse limit

For \(m=2.00\) kg and \(\omega=20.0\) rad/s choose \(T=\pi/10\) s and \(F_p=4.00\) N. Pulse area is 0.628 N s and the impulse estimate is 15.7 mm. Here \(\omega T/4=\pi/2\) and the squared factor is

\[4/\pi^2=0.405.\]

Actual residual amplitude is 6.37 mm. The cancellation occurs because different increments of force reach different phases of the oscillator.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Design a pulse with zero residual motion

Another lightly damped test rig is idealised as undamped at 5.00 Hz. The first zero requires a symmetric triangular pulse lasting two periods, \(T=0.400\) s. To deliver impulse 0.300 N s, choose \(F_p=2J/T=1.50\) N. The mass can move during the pulse, but its final displacement and velocity are both zero in the ideal model. A measured ring-down identifies sensitivity to frequency error, damping or a distorted force shape.

Open this live calculation

A short event and a long response

A latch strikes a mounted instrument for only a few milliseconds, but its motion persists after contact has ended. Peak force alone gives an incomplete description of that motion. Duration, impulse and the system’s natural time scale all matter. Define impulse \(J=\int F(t)\,dt\), with units N·s. Integrating \(m\ddot x+c\dot x+kx=F\) over an ideal instantaneous event leaves \(m[\dot x(0^+)-\dot x(0^-)]=J\). The position remains continuous since no finite mass can jump a finite distance in zero time under a finite impulse.

For an initially resting underdamped oscillator, the subsequent response follows from the two initial conditions \(x(0^+)=0\) and \(\dot x(0^+)=J/m\). With \(\alpha=c/(2m)\) and \(\omega_d=\sqrt{k/m-\alpha^2}\), the displacement is \(x=Je^{-\alpha t}\sin(\omega_dt)/(m\omega_d)\) for \(t>0\). The response to a unit impulse is the same expression with \(J=1\). Its units are displacement per unit impulse.

Build a solution from elementary impulses

Divide a general forcing record into short intervals at times \(s\). Each interval contributes impulse \(F(s)\,ds\) and creates a response delayed by \(s\). Adding all contributions in a linear time-invariant system with zero initial state produces the convolution

\[x(t)=\int_0^t F(s)\,\frac{e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\sin[\omega_d(t-s)]}{m\omega_d}\,ds.\]

This construction makes causality explicit: later parts of the force record cannot influence the current response. Nonzero initial conditions add the free solution. In a multi-mode system each mode has its own impulse response, and the load’s location determines the force projection into it. Replacing the structure by its first mode may describe long-wave displacement well while missing a short, local acceleration peak.

Work a pulse of known duration

Neglect damping and apply constant force \(F_0\) for \(0<t<t_p\). During the pulse integration gives

\[x=(F_0/k)[1-\cos(\omega_n t)],\]
\[\dot x=F_0\omega_n\sin(\omega_n t)/k.\]

After unloading the mass oscillates freely. Compute its amplitude from the end displacement and velocity:

\[A=\sqrt{x(t_p)^2+\big[\dot x(t_p)/\omega_n\big]^2}=\frac{2F_0}{k}\left|\sin(\omega_n t_p/2)\right|.\]

Let \(m=4.0\) kg, \(k=1600\) N/m, \(F_0=20\) N and \(t_p=0.050\) s. Then \(\omega_n=20\) rad/s and the impulse is 1.0 N·s. The formula predicts \(A=25\sin(0.50)=12.0\) mm after unloading. Replacing the whole pulse by an instantaneous impulse gives

\[A_{\rm imp}=J/(m\omega_n)=12.5\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Some restoring force developed during the real pulse, so the impulse approximation is slightly high.

Find the criterion and its limitations

Divide the finite-pulse amplitude by the impulse approximation to obtain \(|\sin z/z|\) with \(z=\omega_nt_p/2\). Its small-\(z\) expansion is \(1-z^2/6+\cdots\). At fixed impulse, a sufficiently short pulse therefore gives about the same low-mode amplitude regardless of duration. A shorter event has a broader frequency content, potentially increasing the importance of higher modes.

A pulse lasting exactly one natural period gives zero residual amplitude in the ideal undamped oscillator, although the displacement during the pulse peaks at \(2F_0/k\). Checking only the post-event motion would miss that maximum. In experiments record the force and response during contact as well as after it. Damping, uncertain pulse shape or nearby modes make exact cancellation unlikely, but the example shows why pulse duration must accompany a quoted peak force.

Compare pulses of equal impulse

A force transducer may report identical total impulse for a sharp tap and a broader pulse. For an undamped mode, the amplitude after the force has ended is the modulus of its frequency-weighted impulse divided by \(m\omega_n\):

\[A=|\int F(t)e^{-i\omega_nt}dt|/(m\omega_n).\]

This follows by expanding the sine kernel of the convolution into two quadratures. For a rectangular pulse the ratio to the ideal instantaneous-impulse amplitude is \(|\sin(\omega_nt_p/2)/(\omega_nt_p/2)|\). At a duration equal to half the natural period that factor is

\[2/\pi=0.637.\]

Two force records with equal signed area can therefore have visibly different post-event amplitudes at one sensor if their durations differ. A short force pulse can consequently calibrate a low-frequency mode while underexciting a faster one in the same structure.

Derive an energy-consistent time update for a force record

A measured force history may be irregular enough that a closed-form convolution is inconvenient. The constant-average-acceleration Newmark update advances a linear oscillator one time step at a time. Let \(x_n,v_n,a_n\) be displacement, velocity and acceleration at \(t_n\). Approximate the acceleration over the step through its endpoint average in the velocity update, and use the corresponding trapezoidal velocity update for displacement. With step \(h\) the relations are

\[v_{n+1}=v_n+\tfrac h2(a_n+a_{n+1}),\]
\[x_{n+1}=x_n+hv_n+\tfrac{h^2}4(a_n+a_{n+1}).\]

Enforce the actual equation \(ma_{n+1}+cv_{n+1}+kx_{n+1}=F_{n+1}\) at the new time. Gathering unknown acceleration terms gives effective mass \(m+ch/2+kh^2/4\). The right side is \(F_{n+1}-c(v_n+ha_n/2)-k(x_n+hv_n+h^2a_n/4)\). Solve that scalar equation and back-substitute into both kinematic updates. For several coupled linear coordinates the same derivation gives a linear system with an effective matrix.

For undamped unforced systems, multiply the average endpoint equilibrium by \(x_{n+1}-x_n=h(v_{n+1}+v_n)/2\). Differences of squares show that the discrete change in kinetic energy plus potential energy vanishes exactly. This property detects sign or unit errors in an implementation. It does not guarantee accurate phase at a coarse time step.

Check one step and distinguish stability from temporal resolution

Take \(m=2\,\mathrm{kg}\), \(c=0\), \(k=200\,\mathrm{N/m}\). Begin from \(x_0=0.010\,\mathrm m,\ v_0=0\) and release without applied force. Initial acceleration is

\[-kx_0/m=-1.00\,\mathrm{m/s^2}.\]

With \(h=0.10\,\mathrm s\), effective mass is

\[2+200(0.10)^2/4=2.50\,\mathrm{kg}.\]

Its right side is \(-200[0.010+0.10^2(-1)/4]=-1.50\,\mathrm N\), giving \(a_1=-0.600\,\mathrm{m/s^2}\). The updates give

\[v_1=-0.0800\,\mathrm{m/s}, x_1=0.00600\,\mathrm m.\]

The initial energy is

\[200(0.010)^2/2=0.0100\,\mathrm J;\]

after one step it is

\[2(0.080)^2/2+200(0.006)^2/2=0.0100\,\mathrm J.\]

The exact displacement at 0.10 s is nevertheless \(0.010\cos(1)=0.00540\,\mathrm m\). The numerical oscillator advances through angle \(2\arctan(\omega_nh/2)=0.9273\) rad per step instead of 1.00 rad. Over many cycles this small period error accumulates. Halving the step is a direct temporal convergence test. For a measured force pulse, a second check is that its sampled impulse and timing converge as well; an accurate oscillator update cannot recover a force feature missed by the acquisition system.

It is possible to predict the amount of step refinement needed for a phase-sensitive comparison. For small \(z=\omega_n h/2\), expand \(2\arctan z=2z-2z^3/3+\cdots\). Dividing the numerical angular advance by the true advance gives \(1-(\omega_n h)^2/12+\cdots\). The leading relative frequency error therefore falls by a factor of four when the step is halved. A one-percent target suggests \(\omega_nh\lesssim\sqrt{0.12}=0.346\) for this single undamped mode. At 10 rad/s that means roughly 0.035 s or less, before checking the sampling of the force itself. This criterion addresses the fundamental frequency, not the fastest contributing mode in a multi-degree model. If a high-frequency mode contributes significantly at the measurement station, use its frequency to assess temporal phase resolution or test convergence of the complete measured signal directly.

Once these conditions are met, comparing the computed displacement and velocity at the end of loading also tests the predicted initial condition for the subsequent free ring-down.

Problem sheet 3 · Isolation and shock

Separate floor motion from mount travel, then distinguish peak response during and after an impact.

1. Choose an isolation frequency

A 100 kg platform must have \(|X/Y|<1\) for floor excitation at all frequencies from 8 Hz upward. (a) Find the upper limit on its natural frequency. (b) Choose 5.0 Hz and calculate \(k\). (c) At 10 Hz and \(\zeta=0.20\), find absolute and relative amplitudes for a 0.50 mm base motion.

Apply the displacement transfer function

For the specified inequality \(f_n<8/\sqrt2=5.66\) Hz. At 5.0 Hz the stiffness is \(100(2\pi5)^2=98.7\) kN/m. At \(r=2\) and \(2\zeta r=0.8\), the common denominator magnitude is

\[\sqrt{9+0.64}=3.105.\]

The absolute amplitude is \(0.50\sqrt{1.64}/3.105=0.206\) mm, while relative amplitude is

\[0.50(4)/3.105=0.644\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Size the allowable mount travel using the latter value.

2. Compare impacts with the same impulse

An undamped 5 kg component has \(\omega_n=40\) rad/s. Two rectangular forces each provide 1.0 N·s. Pulse A lasts 0.010 s and pulse B 0.100 s. (a) Calculate each force. (b) Calculate the two residual amplitudes. (c) Compare them with the ideal impulse limit.

Keep impulse fixed while changing duration

The forces are 100 and 10.0 N. The ideal limit is

\[J/(m\omega_n)=5.0\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Applying the finite-duration correction \(|\sin z/z|\) with \(z=0.20\) and 2.0 yields 4.97 and 2.27 mm. The longer force partly acts during a phase when the oscillator moves back. Its smaller residual response does not follow from impulse alone.

3. Check whether a stop is struck during a pulse

A 10 kg mass on a 1000 N/m spring starts at rest. A 5 N constant force lasts exactly one undamped period. A travel stop lies 8 mm from equilibrium in the force direction. (a) Find the period. (b) Find the predicted motion at half and full period without the stop. (c) Does a zero residual response prove safe clearance?

Inspect the complete history

The angular frequency is 10 rad/s, giving a period of 0.628 s. During the force \(x=5(1-\cos10t)/1000\) m. At half a period this is 10.0 mm, with zero velocity. At the full period both displacement and velocity vanish. The motion crosses the 8 mm stop beforehand at \(\cos10t=-0.6\), or \(t=0.221\) s. The real motion after contact must be recalculated with the additional force. A post-event sample would miss the predicted interference.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: Base excitation and vibration isolation; Impulse response and finite-duration loading. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Chapter 7 · Velocities and accelerations in planar mechanisms

Use the rigid-body velocity and acceleration relations to connect an instantaneously known crank speed to a slider acceleration.

From first principles

Differentiate a fixed link vector in a rotating body

For two material points \(A,B\) on a rigid planar link, their separation has constant length even while its direction changes. Let \(\mathbf r=\mathbf r_B-\mathbf r_A\) and angular velocity vector \(\boldsymbol\omega\) be perpendicular to the plane. A small rotation by \(d\theta\) sweeps a displacement perpendicular to the separation. Dividing by time gives the inertial derivative \(\dot{\mathbf r}=\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf r\) and hence

\[\mathbf v_B=\mathbf v_A+\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf r .\]

Differentiate once more using the product rule. Change of angular speed provides the tangential acceleration contribution; change of the separation's direction gives the second cross product.

\[\mathbf a_B=\mathbf a_A+\boldsymbol\alpha\times\mathbf r+\boldsymbol\omega\times(\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf r).\]

For planar rotation the final term is \(-\omega^2\mathbf r\), directed towards \(A\). It remains even when angular speed is constant. These relations concern two fixed points on one rigid body. A slider moving along the rotating link changes the body-relative separation and contributes additional velocity and acceleration terms.

Derivation and applications

Recover planar angular acceleration from two accelerometers

Velocities identify an instantaneous rotation centre, but a time-varying rotation is better characterised by both angular speed and angular acceleration. Mount two aligned accelerometers at points \(A\) and \(B\) on one rigid planar body. Assume the measured vectors have been expressed in common inertial axes and any gravity contribution cancels in their difference. The separation vector is \(\mathbf r=\mathbf r_B-\mathbf r_A\) with fixed length \(d\). Rigid-body kinematics gives

\[\Delta\mathbf a=\mathbf a_B-\mathbf a_A=\alpha\,\mathbf e_z\times\mathbf r-\omega^2\mathbf r .\]

The two terms point in perpendicular directions. Form the unit vector \(\mathbf e_r=\mathbf r/d\) from \(A\) to \(B\) and its counterclockwise normal \(\mathbf e_t=\mathbf e_z\times\mathbf e_r\). Projecting gives a radial signal proportional to squared angular speed and a tangential signal proportional to signed angular acceleration.

\[\Delta a_r=-d\omega^2,\]
\[\Delta a_t=d\alpha .\]

Thus no knowledge of the translational acceleration of the reference point is needed. An additional tangential relative-velocity measurement supplies the missing sense of rotation because the signed \(\omega\) enters linearly.

\[|\omega|=\sqrt{-\Delta a_r/d},\]
\[\alpha=\Delta a_t/d,\]
\[\omega=(\mathbf v_B-\mathbf v_A)\cdot\mathbf e_t/d .\]

A positive radial acceleration difference is inconsistent with a rigid pair under the stated conventions. Flexible mounting, sensor misalignment or treating differently oriented sensor components as parallel can all spoil the projection.

Worked example

Read the motion of a rotating link

At separation 0.200 m the measured acceleration difference is \(-3.20\,\mathbf e_r+0.600\,\mathbf e_t\) m/s². The link has speed magnitude \(\sqrt{3.20/0.200}=4.00\) rad/s and counterclockwise angular acceleration \(0.600/0.200=3.00\) rad/s². A measured relative tangential velocity of \(-0.800\) m/s identifies clockwise rotation. Its speed magnitude is decreasing at this instant.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Estimate a sensor spacing from a calibrated turn

A second instrument rotates steadily at 5.00 rad/s and records radial acceleration difference \(-3.75\) m/s². Its effective sensor separation is

\[3.75/25.0=0.150\,\mathrm{m}.\]

A later measurement with the same instrument gives tangential difference \(-0.450\) m/s², implying \(\alpha=-3.00\) rad/s². This calibration uses steady rotation for the length scale and a distinct transient to determine angular acceleration.

What can change between two points on a link?

The point at which a connecting rod drives a slider moves along a straight line although its other end moves around a crank circle. Writing every trajectory as a function of crank angle is possible, but often only one configuration matters. The rigidity constraint transfers instantaneous velocity and acceleration between points on a link. Choose a fixed inertial frame and use the positive out-of-plane direction for counterclockwise angles. Every vector component below refers to these fixed axes.

Write \(r_B=r_A+r_{B/A}\). The vector between two body points keeps its length but changes direction. Its derivative is \(\omega\times r_{B/A}\). Differentiating a second time introduces both the changing magnitude of angular velocity and the changing direction of the relative vector:

\[v_B=v_A+\omega\times r_{B/A},\]
\[a_B=a_A+\alpha\times r_{B/A}+\omega\times(\omega\times r_{B/A}).\]

The last term is \(-\omega^2r_{B/A}\) for planar motion. It points along the rod towards \(A\), even when the rod has constant angular speed. Enforce constraints on the actual point \(B\); a slider constrained horizontally has zero vertical velocity and zero vertical acceleration if its guide is straight and fixed.

A crank at its uppermost position

Place crank centre \(O\) at the origin and the slider along the positive horizontal axis. The crank length is 50 mm and the connecting rod length is 130 mm. At the configuration considered its pin is \(A=(0,50)\,\mathrm{mm}\) and slider pin \(B=(120,0)\,\mathrm{mm}\), since \(120^2+50^2=130^2\). The crank rotates counterclockwise at constant 20 rad/s. Accordingly \(v_A=(-1,0)\,\mathrm{m/s}\) and \(a_A=(0,-20)\,\mathrm{m/s^2}\).

The rod vector from \(A\) to \(B\) is \((0.120,-0.050)\,\mathrm m\). Its relative velocity is \(\omega_r(0.050,0.120)\). The vertical velocity constraint gives \(0=0+0.120\omega_r\), so \(\omega_r=0\) at this instant and \(v_B=(-1,0)\,\mathrm{m/s}\). The rod is instantaneously translating. That description concerns velocity only. A moment later its two ends no longer have the same velocity.

Recover the slider force input

Since \(\omega_r=0\) here, the relative centripetal term for the rod vanishes. The angular-acceleration term gives \(0=-20+0.120\alpha_r\), hence \(\alpha_r=166.7\,\mathrm{rad/s^2}\). The slider’s horizontal acceleration is

\[a_{Bx}=0+0.050(166.7)=8.33\,\mathrm{m/s^2}.\]

At this instant the slider is moving to the left while accelerating to the right. A bare slider mass of 3 kg needs a net force of 25.0 N to the right before guide friction or process forces are considered.

A useful independent check follows from the exact geometry \(x_B=r\cos\theta+\sqrt{l^2-r^2\sin^2\theta}\). At \(\theta=\pi/2\) the first derivative with respect to angle is \(-r\); multiplication by crank speed gives −1 m/s. A numerical second derivative must resolve the curvature of this path. Differentiating noisy measured positions twice can produce a large fictitious force, so comparing observed positions with this geometric constraint is often a better starting point.

Change the drive speed

Repeating the same configuration at twice the constant crank speed doubles every point velocity. Its centripetal terms and the constraint-induced angular acceleration scale with the square of that speed, so the predicted slider acceleration becomes 33.3 m/s². This scaling is one reason mechanism inertia loads can grow rapidly with production rate.

Differentiate a coordinate measured along a rotating guide

The rigid-body formula above assumes that the position vector joining the two points is fixed in their common body. A slider moving along a rotating guide violates that assumption. Introduce a guide-fixed unit vector \(e_r\) along the slot and its positive quarter-turn \(e_\theta\). At an inertially fixed pivot the slider’s position is \(r(t)e_r\); the basis changes according to \(\dot e_r=\omega e_\theta,\ \dot e_\theta=-\omega e_r\). Differentiate both the scalar radial position and the changing basis at every step.

\[v=\dot r\,e_r+r\omega\,e_\theta,\]
\[a=(\ddot r-r\omega^2)e_r+(r\dot\omega+2\dot r\omega)e_\theta .\]

The factor two in the transverse contribution has two origins. The outward relative velocity vector rotates with the guide, and the speed of the circumferential transport changes as the slider moves outwards. Each contributes \(\dot r\omega\). This Coriolis term is an actual part of the acceleration measured in an inertial frame. In a rotating-frame force balance an apparent Coriolis force of opposite sign must be introduced only if the inertial acceleration has been removed from that balance. Counting both would double the effect.

If the guide’s pivot itself translates, add its inertial acceleration to the expression. For an instantaneously stationary guide \(\omega=0\) eliminates centripetal and Coriolis terms, but a nonzero angular acceleration can still produce transverse acceleration \(r\dot\omega\). This distinction parallels the translating rod in the first worked mechanism.

Determine drive and guide reactions on a rotating table

A 2.5 kg carriage slides outwards along a straight radial slot in a horizontal table. At the given instant \(r=0.30\,\mathrm m\), \(\dot r=0.20\,\mathrm{m/s}\), \(\ddot r=0.50\,\mathrm{m/s^2}\), \(\omega=4.0\,\mathrm{rad/s}\) counterclockwise, and \(\dot\omega=1.0\,\mathrm{rad/s^2}\). The radial acceleration is \(0.50-0.30(4)^2=-4.30\,\mathrm{m/s^2}\) and the transverse acceleration is

\[0.30(1)+2(0.20)(4)=1.90\,\mathrm{m/s^2}.\]

A frictionless slot supplies a 4.75 N transverse reaction in the positive rotational direction. The radial actuator must exert a net 10.75 N inward, even though the carriage is moving outwards and accelerating relative to the slot in that direction.

The transverse reaction produces a 1.425 N m moment about the centre. Independently, differentiating the angular momentum \(mr^2\omega\) gives

\[m(2r\dot r\omega+r^2\dot\omega)=1.425\,\mathrm{N\,m}.\]

A table drive that assumes fixed radius would include only \(mr^2\dot\omega=0.225\,\mathrm{N\,m}\), missing most of the required moment in this manoeuvre. Holding angular speed constant would remove this last term but leave the 1.20 N m moment associated with increasing inertia. A torque sensor at the turntable shaft therefore detects radial carriage motion even when no angular acceleration is commanded.

Mechanical power supplies an independent sign check. The rail reaction transmits positive power \(4.75(r\omega)=5.70\) W through the transverse motion at this instant. The inward radial actuator supplies \(-10.75(0.20)=-2.15\) W. Their sum, 3.55 W, equals the instantaneous change of the point mass’s translational energy, \(m(\dot r\,a_r+r\omega a_\theta)=2.5[0.20(-4.30)+1.20(1.90)]\). The rail can supply power because it moves transversely with the table even though the contact has no work in the sliding direction.

Construct angular momentum when the rotation axis is not principal

A planar moment of inertia determines one scalar angular momentum. In three dimensions a balanced body can have angular momentum tilted relative to its angular velocity. About its centre of mass a particle at \(r\) has velocity \(\omega\times r\). Integrate \(r\times(\omega\times r)\) over mass and use the vector triple product. The result is

\[H_G=I_G\omega,\]

with tensor \(I_G=\int[(r^Tr)\mathbf1-rr^T]\,dm\). Its \(x\)-diagonal element is \(\int(y^2+z^2)dm\) and its \(xy\) element is \(-\int xy\,dm\). Equal and opposite points cancel their first moments but add their products of inertia. Symmetry about a coordinate plane, rather than symmetry only about the centre, is sufficient to make the associated product vanish.

In a body-fixed basis rotating at \(\omega\), the external moment components obey \(M_G=I_G\dot\omega+\omega\times(I_G\omega)\). This follows from differentiating \(H_G\) in an inertial frame: the body basis vectors rotate as well as the components. Even with constant body components of angular velocity, a transverse moment can be required to keep a nonprincipal axis fixed.

Model a compact instrument carrier as two equal 0.50 kg masses at \((0.20,0.10,0)\) m and its negative, neglecting the frame mass. Its tensor in kg m² has entries \(I_{xx}=0.010,\ I_{yy}=0.040,\ I_{zz}=0.050,\ I_{xy}=-0.020\). At \(\omega=(20,0,0)\) rad/s, \(H_G=(0.20,-0.40,0)\) kg m²/s. Maintaining that rotation requires \(M_G=(0,0,-8.0)\) N m. There is no offset centre of mass to correct with a single static counterweight. Repositioning the masses to remove the product of inertia, or reorienting the shaft along a principal axis, eliminates this transverse couple in the model. A bearing reaction can reverse the sign convention for the load read by its support. Always state whether the reported moment acts on the rotor or on the frame.

Practise on sheet 4 · Predict the reaction moment from a tilted mass arrangement. Further reading · MIT · 16.07 Dynamics · Lecture L26, 3D Rigid Body Dynamics: The Inertia Tensor.

Chapter 8 · Impulse, angular momentum and impact

Find the motion immediately after a short off-centre impact by combining angular impulse with a local restitution condition.

From first principles

Close a short collision with impulse and restitution

During a short collision, contact force can be large while the position changes little. Let \(g^-=v_1^--v_2^->0\) be the relative approach speed along a fixed impact normal for two freely translating masses. Ignore tangential impulse and external impulses during contact. A compressive contact impulse of magnitude \(J\) slows the first mass and speeds the second. Integrating each momentum balance gives

\[v_1^+=v_1^- - J/m_1,\]
\[v_2^+=v_2^- + J/m_2 .\]

Momentum conservation alone leaves the impulse undetermined. A measured normal coefficient of restitution \(e\) supplies the separation speed as a fraction of the approach speed. Substitution then determines the impulse.

\[v_2^+-v_1^+=eg^-,\]
\[J=\frac{(1+e)g^-}{1/m_1+1/m_2}.\]

For a rigid body struck away from its centre the impulse also changes angular momentum by \(\mathbf r\times\mathbf J\). The normal velocity used in restitution must then be evaluated at the contact point, combining centre translation and rotation. A restitution value is a contact model or measurement, whereas the impulse balances follow directly from mechanics.

Background · Force impulse
\[J=\int F\,dt\]

An impulse changes linear momentum over an event. A coefficient of restitution supplies a separate relative-velocity condition for an impact.

Derivation and applications

Locate the impact point that leaves a pivot without an impulse

A rapid blow on a hinged body can set it rotating while producing zero instantaneous horizontal impulse at the hinge. Model a rigid pendulum initially at rest, with mass \(m\), centre of mass distance \(a\) from its frictionless pivot, and moment of inertia \(I_O\) about the pivot. Apply a short tangential impulse \(J\) at distance \(b\) along the same radial line. Neglect the impulse from gravity during contact and take positive velocity along \(J\). Angular impulse about the pivot removes the unknown hinge reaction.

\[I_O\omega^+=Jb,\]
\[v_G^+=a\omega^+ .\]

Linear impulse along the impact direction includes both \(J\) and the unknown hinge impulse \(H\). Substitution of angular speed therefore gives the signed hinge impulse as a function of the impact radius. A reaction in the opposite direction from \(J\) corresponds to \(H<0\).

\[m v_G^+=J+H,\]
\[H=J\left(\frac{mab}{I_O}-1\right).\]

Set \(H=0\) and use the parallel-axis theorem \(I_O=I_G+ma^2\). The resulting radius locates the centre of percussion; it lies beyond the centre of mass for any extended body with \(I_G>0\).

\[b_{\rm cp}=\frac{I_O}{ma}=a+\frac{I_G}{ma}.\]

This result concerns the net hinge impulse during a short tangential event. Later hinge forces as the body swings need a separate force balance. A finite-duration or oblique impact also requires examining the other force components.

Worked example

Locate the point on a laboratory pendulum

A uniform slender bar of length 0.900 m and mass 1.50 kg is hinged at one end. Its \(a=0.450\) m and \(I_O=mL^2/3=0.405\) kg m², giving \(b_{\rm cp}=0.600\) m. A tangential impulse of 0.300 N s there produces

\[\omega^+=0.300(0.600)/0.405=0.444\,\mathrm{rad/s}.\]

Its centre momentum is \(1.50(0.450)(0.444)=0.300\) N s, accounting for all of the applied impulse.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Infer inertia from a zero-reaction test

A different hinged fixture has \(m=4.00\) kg and \(a=0.180\) m. An instrumented hinge shows vanishing tangential impulse at \(b=0.320\) m. This gives \(I_O=mab=0.2304\) kg m² and \(I_G=0.1008\) kg m². At \(b=0.160\) m, a 0.500 N s impulse should instead produce \(H=0.500(0.160/0.320-1)=-0.250\) N s, a direct sign and calibration check.

Does equal impulse leave equal vibration?

A force hammer and a soft contact pad can deliver the same total impulse in quite different times. The impulse fixes the change of momentum of a free mass. A mounted instrument also feels a spring force during the contact. The instrument may have already started a substantial fraction of an oscillation while the applied force is still acting, allowing later parts of the pulse to cancel some of the earlier response.

Start an undamped oscillator at rest and apply a force \(F(t)\) only between zero and \(T\). Passing a small impulse \(F(s)\,ds\) to the oscillator gives a later displacement \(F(s)\sin[\omega(t-s)]\,ds/(m\omega)\). Integrating over arrival times for \(t>T\) and collecting the coefficients of \(\sin\omega t\) and \(\cos\omega t\) gives the residual amplitude

\[A=\frac1{m\omega}\left|\int_0^T F(s)e^{-i\omega s}\,ds\right|.\]

The relevant measure is the pulse spectrum at the oscillator frequency. Its zero-frequency value is the ordinary impulse. Their difference is negligible when the complex phase changes very little during contact. A wide-band impact model that fits only impulse can otherwise give the wrong residual vibration even when it closely predicts the overall force integral.

Choose a half-sine pulse \(F(s)=J\pi\sin(\pi s/T)/(2T)\) with prescribed impulse \(J\). Write the sine as a pair of exponentials, integrate each exponential and take the magnitude. With \(r=\omega T\), this produces

\[A/[J/(m\omega)]=\pi^2|\cos(r/2)/(\pi^2-r^2)|.\]

At \(r=\pi\) the numerator and denominator both vanish. Expanding about \(\pi\) or integrating the sine pulse directly gives the finite limit \(\pi/4\). The notebook evaluates a pair of sinc functions to avoid asking the computer to divide zero by zero.

For a 2 kg instrument with natural frequency 8 Hz and a pulse of 0.6 N s, an instantaneous impulse gives \(A=5.968\) mm. A 50 ms contact occupies 0.4 natural periods and leaves only 5.123 mm. In the notebook, compare the closed result with direct numerical integration of the complex forcing history. Halve the duration while preserving impulse and inspect the approach to the impulsive limit. The force peak must increase to conserve the area.

If an accelerometer is used to check the result, separate the interval during which the hammer is in contact from the ring-down after release. During free vibration the acceleration satisfies \(\ddot x=-\omega^2x\), and the measured acceleration amplitude divided by \(\omega^2\) gives the residual displacement amplitude. For the 50 ms example this predicts about 12.94 m/s² during the ring-down. During contact the force term \(F(t)/m\) also contributes to the acceleration. Applying the same conversion to the largest acceleration in the whole record could therefore assign the forcing pulse to the free vibration. A force transducer identifies the release time independently.

At a duration of 1.5 natural periods, the remaining oscillation of this model vanishes. Does this make such a pulse equivalent to a force that never acted?

Work through the solution

At \(r=3\pi\) the cosine numerator is zero and the denominator is finite. Positive parts of the pulse delivered at different phases cancel in the final modal state. During contact there is displacement, stored spring energy and a transient force in the support. A specimen containing a second mode will weight the same pulse at another frequency. A cancellation for one mode says little about the maximum support displacement during contact or the other mode’s final amplitude.

Vary the notebook inputs and compare both plotted observables. The same equations generate the browser curves and the notebook reference; fixed material, geometry and model assumptions are stated in the readout.

Open editable notebook Download .ipynb

Select a time interval and a reference point

For a short collision, a finite change of velocity occurs over a time in which positions change very little. Integrate Newton’s law over that interval. A force that is harmless in an ordinary static calculation can deliver a large impulse if sustained long enough. Conversely, gravity often contributes very little over a millisecond. Compare its estimated impulse \(mg\Delta t\) with the contact impulse before neglecting it.

For planar rigid-body motion the impulse–momentum balances about the mass centre \(G\) and about an inertially fixed point \(O\) are

\[m(v_G^+-v_G^-)=\sum J,\]
\[H_O^+-H_O^-=\sum r_{i/O}\times J_i .\]

The superscripts denote states just before and after contact. A hinge can deliver an unknown impulse but exerts no moment impulse about its own centre when modelled as an ideal pin. Choosing that point can eliminate unknowns when calculating angular speed. Kinetic energy is generally lost to deformation, contact vibration and other processes. It cannot supply a conservation equation without an additional elastic-impact assumption.

Apply the contact condition at the points that meet

For a smooth frictionless collision choose the normal from the incoming body towards the target. The separation speed along that normal equals the specified coefficient of restitution \(e\) times the approach speed. Evaluate velocities of the actual points in contact; the target’s mass-centre speed can be different if the force also rotates it. For a contact point at distance \(a\) along a pinned bar and normal perpendicular to the bar, the normal contact velocity immediately after impact is \(a\omega^+\).

Take a small slider of mass 1.0 kg moving at 3.0 m/s into a stationary slender uniform bar of mass 3.0 kg, length 0.60 m, pinned at one end. It strikes at \(a=0.40\,\mathrm m\), perpendicular to the bar. The bar inertia about the pin is

\[I_O=m_bL^2/3=0.360\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}.\]

Let \(J\) be the positive impulse delivered to the bar. The slider exits at \(3-J/1\), and the bar receives angular speed \(Ja/I_O\).

Close the collision and audit its energy

With \(e=0.50\), the restitution condition is \(a(Ja/I_O)-(3-J)=0.50(3)\), giving \(J=4.5/[1+0.4^2/0.36]=3.115\,\mathrm{N\,s}\). Thus \(\omega^+=3.46\,\mathrm{rad/s}\) and the slider rebounds slowly at −0.115 m/s. The bar’s contact point recedes at 1.385 m/s, exactly 1.50 m/s faster than the slider after separation.

The initial kinetic energy was 4.50 J. The final energy is

\[\tfrac12(1)(0.115)^2+\tfrac12(0.360)(3.46)^2=2.16\,\mathrm J.\]

The energy loss is physically consistent with the specified restitution. The pin impulse need not vanish although its moment impulse vanished. The bar’s centre moves at \(0.30(3.46)=1.04\,\mathrm{m/s}\); its linear momentum gain happens to equal about 3.12 N s for this impact position, very close to the contact impulse. Indeed \(a=I_O/(m_bL/2)=0.40\,\mathrm m\) is the centre-of-percussion location, for which the ideal transverse pin impulse vanishes exactly.

Determine whether a rough impact sticks or slides

The smooth collision model leaves tangential velocities unaffected. A rough planar impact can also exchange tangential impulse and rotational momentum. Consider a solid disc of radius \(R\) striking a fixed horizontal surface at its lowest point. Take horizontal right and vertical up as positive; angular speed is counterclockwise positive. Just before impact let centre velocities be \(u^-,v^-\) and angular speed \(\omega^-\). The horizontal speed of its contact point is \(g_t^-=u^-+R\omega^-\). A rightward tangential impulse \(J_t\) produces both a centre velocity increase \(J_t/m\) and a positive angular change \(RJ_t/I_G\).

The post-impact contact velocity is \(g_t^+=g_t^-+(1/m+R^2/I_G)J_t\). For the horizontal surface the normal impulse passes through the centre; restitution requires \(J_n=-m(1+e)v^-\) for \(v^-<0\). The tangential impulse needed to eliminate slip is

\[J_t^{\rm stick}=-g_t^-/(1/m+R^2/I_G).\]

Under a simple Coulomb impulse approximation compare this required magnitude with \(\mu J_n\). If it lies inside the bound the contact can finish without tangential slip. Otherwise take the limiting friction impulse opposing the pre-impact relative slip. This elementary model assumes no change in slip direction before the limiting impulse is reached, negligible external impulse, and no tangential elastic restitution. Using only the disc centre’s velocity to form the stick condition would miss its rotational compliance.

Compare two surfaces with the same normal restitution

A solid disc has \(m=2.0\,\mathrm{kg}\), \(R=0.10\,\mathrm m\),

\[I_G=mR^2/2=0.010\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}.\]

Its centre approaches at \(u^-=3.0,\ v^-=-2.0\,\mathrm{m/s}\), with zero initial spin. For \(e=0.50\), \(J_n=6.0\,\mathrm{N\,s}\) and \(v^+=1.0\,\mathrm{m/s}\). The inverse tangential effective mass is

\[1/2+0.10^2/0.010=1.50\,\mathrm{kg^{-1}}.\]

Thus \(J_t^{\rm stick}=-2.0\,\mathrm{N\,s}\) and the friction coefficient needed is at least \(2/6=0.333\).

On a surface with \(\mu=0.20\), \(J_t=-1.2\,\mathrm{N\,s}\), giving \(u^+=2.4\,\mathrm{m/s}\) and \(\omega^+=-12\,\mathrm{rad/s}\). The contact-point speed remains \(2.4-1.2=1.2\,\mathrm{m/s}\) to the right, consistent with the chosen sliding direction. On a surface with \(\mu=0.50\), the sticking impulse is achievable. It gives \(u^+=2.0\,\mathrm{m/s}\), \(\omega^+=-20\,\mathrm{rad/s}\), and zero instantaneous tangential contact speed. The disc still leaves the surface vertically because \(v^+=1.0\). Here sticking means zero relative tangential velocity at separation and does not mean a permanently attached body. The original 13 J of kinetic energy drops to 7.48 J in the sliding case and 7.00 J in the sticking case, consistent with frictional dissipation and a normal restitution below unity.

A high-speed camera that measures only the centre trajectory would determine the change in centre momentum and hence the tangential impulse, provided other horizontal impulses are negligible. Marking the disc edge permits an independent angular check: in the sliding case the ratio \(I_G|\Delta\omega|/(R m|\Delta u|)\) is

\[0.010(12)/[0.10(2)(0.60)]=1.\]

A substantial measured deviation could indicate an additional contact couple, a second impact point or uncertainty in inertia. Measuring spin is particularly important near the transition to sticking because centre velocity by itself cannot establish that the tangential contact speed has vanished.

A marked rim therefore provides an observable that a centre-tracking experiment alone cannot supply.

Problem sheet 4 · Mechanism kinematics and impact

Recover constrained point accelerations and close two impact balances with appropriate contact conditions.

A point on a rotating arm

Point A on a planar body has velocity (0.8, 0.1) m/s and acceleration (1, −2) m/s². Point B is 0.30 m to the right and 0.10 m above A. The body rotates counterclockwise at 4 rad/s with clockwise angular acceleration 3 rad/s². Find the velocity and acceleration of B. Which term survives at constant angular speed?

Reveal the worked solution

The relative velocity is \(4(-0.10,0.30)=(-0.4,1.2)\), hence \(v_B=(0.4,1.3)\,\mathrm{m/s}\). The relative tangential acceleration is \((-3)(-0.10,0.30)=(0.3,-0.9)\); the centripetal term is \(-16(0.30,0.10)=(-4.8,-1.6)\). Adding A gives \(a_B=(-3.5,-4.5)\,\mathrm{m/s^2}\). Even with zero angular acceleration the centripetal term remains.

Observe a collision

On a horizontal line a 2 kg cart moving at 3 m/s collides with a stationary 1 kg cart. The restitution coefficient is 0.60. Find their final velocities, impulse on the target and energy lost. State what allows linear momentum conservation here.

Reveal the worked solution

Horizontal external impulse is negligible. Thus

\[2v_1+v_2=6,\]
\[v_2-v_1=0.60(3)=1.8.\]

Solving gives

\[v_1=1.4,\ v_2=3.2\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

Target impulse is 3.2 N s. Initial energy is 9 J and final energy \(1.4^2+3.2^2/2=7.08\,\mathrm J\), giving loss 1.92 J.

A particle sticks to a pinned bar

A 0.20 kg clay mass strikes perpendicular to a 0.50 m slender bar at 4 m/s and sticks to its free end. The bar mass is 1.2 kg and its other end is pinned. Find final angular speed and energy lost. Why is angular momentum about the pin the convenient balance?

Reveal the worked solution

The incoming angular momentum is \(0.20(4)(0.50)=0.40\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s}\). Combined inertia about the pin is

\[1.2(0.50)^2/3+0.20(0.50)^2=0.150\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}.\]

Thus \(\omega=2.667\,\mathrm{rad/s}\). Final energy is \(0.150(2.667)^2/2=0.533\,\mathrm J\), versus 1.60 J initially. The ideal pin impulse has no moment about the pin, although it can change total linear momentum.

Predict the reaction moment from a tilted mass arrangement

Two 0.25 kg trim masses sit at \((0.12,0,0.08)\) m and its negative relative to a shaft centre. The shaft rotates steadily about positive \(z\) at 30 rad/s. Find \(I_{xz}\), angular momentum, required external moment on the rotor and the moment at twice the speed.

Show the worked solution

Here \(I_{xz}=-2(0.25)(0.12)(0.08)=-0.0048\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}\), and \(I_{zz}=2(0.25)(0.12)^2=0.0072\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}\). Therefore \(H=(-0.144,0,0.216)\) kg m²/s. The torque \((0,0,30)\times H=(0,-4.32,0)\) N m. Doubling speed doubles angular momentum and its rotation rate, so the required moment magnitude grows fourfold to 17.28 N m. The frame feels the equal and opposite moment.

Chapter 9 · Static and couple balance of rotors

Represent rotating imbalance as a vector and determine the corrections needed to cancel both net force and rocking moment.

From first principles

Represent a rotating offset by a force and moment phasor

A rigid rotor carries a small mass \(m\) at eccentricity \(e\) from its shaft axis. At constant angular speed \(\omega\), its radial acceleration has magnitude \(e\omega^2\). The shaft supplies the inward force and receives an equal outward reaction whose direction rotates with the shaft. Encode the offset orientation by phase \(\phi\) and the unbalance by \(U=me\,e^{i\phi}\). Complex notation retains both perpendicular force components.

\[F_x+iF_y=\omega^2 U e^{i\omega t}.\]

For several masses spinning together, force superposition makes the net phasor proportional to \(\sum U_j\). Axially separated forces can also produce a rotating couple even when the net force vanishes. Taking moments about a reference plane weights each unbalance by its axial distance \(z_j\).

\[\sum_j U_j=0,\]
\[\sum_j z_jU_j=0 .\]

These are the two rigid-rotor balance conditions. Changing the reference plane adds a multiple of the first sum to the second, so once net force vanishes the couple balance is independent of that choice. Flexible rotor modes at operating speed require additional information.

Derivation and applications

Separate the imbalance force and couple at the bearings

A rotor can have little net rotating force yet cause large opposed bearing reactions. Treat the shaft as rigid with bearings at axial positions \(z=0\) and \(z=L\). Assume speed \(\omega\) is constant and sufficiently below the first bending resonance for a rigid load distribution. Represent each unbalance by the complex vector \(U_j=m_je_j e^{i\phi_j}\). The physical rotating force on the support is the real part of the phasor times \(e^{i\omega t}\). Summing forces and taking moments about the first support gives the transmitted load phasors \(F_A,F_B\).

\[F_A+F_B=\omega^2\sum_j U_j,\]
\[L F_B=\omega^2\sum_j z_jU_j .\]

The moment equation weights each unbalance by axial position. Solving for the individual support loads identifies the force distribution even for a resultant whose direction rotates with the shaft. The weights can be negative for overhung masses.

\[F_B=\frac{\omega^2}{L}\sum_jz_jU_j,\]
\[F_A=\omega^2\sum_j(1-z_j/L)U_j .\]

Conversely, two synchronised bearing measurements supply both the summed unbalance and its first axial moment. Their relative phases must be retained, since adding magnitude readings would erase the signature of a rotating couple.

\[U_{\rm sum}=\frac{F_A+F_B}{\omega^2},\]
\[M_U=\sum_j z_jU_j=\frac{LF_B}{\omega^2}.\]

With \(U_{\rm sum}=0\), equal and opposite support phasors can remain. Their mount loads grow with squared speed even though the instantaneous vector sum vanishes.

Worked example

Predict bearing reactions from a pure couple

Take \(L=0.600\) m, \(\omega=100\) rad/s, and equal opposite unbalances of magnitude 200 g mm in planes at 0.100 and 0.500 m. Taking the first unbalance as positive, \(M_U=0.000200(0.100-0.500)=-8.00\times10^{-5}\) kg m². The bearing load \(F_B=-1.33\) N and \(F_A=+1.33\) N. A single accelerometer measuring common support translation could miss the rotating moment.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Identify the plane of a single residual mass

In a different machine, in-phase bearing load amplitudes are 3.00 N and 1.00 N at \(A,B\) respectively, \(\omega=200\) rad/s and \(L=0.800\) m. The inferred net unbalance is \(4.00/200^2=1.00\times10^{-4}\) kg m, or 100 g mm. For a single residual plane its equivalent position is

\[LF_B/(F_A+F_B)=0.200\,\mathrm{m}.\]

A pair of distributed errors may produce the same two rigid-body measurements.

Translate a mass offset into a periodic bearing load

A rotor can have its total mass centre on the shaft axis and still shake its housing. The reason is spatial: two equal and opposite centrifugal force vectors in separate transverse planes form a rotating couple. First describe one small excess mass \(m\) at radial offset \(e\) and clock angle \(\phi\). At constant angular speed \(\omega\), its required radial acceleration has magnitude \(e\omega^2\). The equal and opposite reaction transmitted to the rotating assembly has magnitude \(me\omega^2\).

Represent its radial unbalance by a complex number \(U=me(\cos\phi+i\sin\phi)\). This is shorthand for two transverse components at a reference shaft orientation, not a complex physical force. Multiplication by \(e^{i\omega t}\) rotates all vectors together. Since both \(\omega^2\) and this rotation are common to all masses, cancellation can be carried out using \(U\) alone.

\[\sum U_j=0,\]
\[\sum z_jU_j=0.\]

Here \(z\) is axial location from a chosen reference plane. The second condition cancels the rotating transverse moment; its vector direction includes a common quarter-turn from the cross product that drops out of the zero condition. If the first condition holds, shifting the axial origin leaves the second condition unchanged.

Split one known unbalance between two planes

A rigid rotor has an 18 g excess mass at radius 40 mm, angle 0°, and axial location 120 mm between two correction planes at \(z_A=0\) and \(z_B=300\,\mathrm{mm}\). Its unbalance is \(720\,\mathrm{g\,mm}\) along the reference direction. Moment balance gives \(300 U_B=-120(720)\), hence \(U_B=-288\,\mathrm{g\,mm}\). Force balance then gives \(U_A=-432\,\mathrm{g\,mm}\). Both corrections point at 180° because the original mass lies between the planes.

At correction radius 60 mm these vectors require added masses 4.80 g in plane B and 7.20 g in plane A. If material is removed instead, the equivalent removed masses lie at the original 0° clock position. At 1800 rpm the original force amplitude was \(0.000720(188.5)^2=25.6\,\mathrm N\). Doubling speed quadruples that force, yet the same mass corrections still balance the assumed rigid rotor.

Identify what a single-plane test can miss

Consider instead two unbalances \(+400\) and \(-400\,\mathrm{g\,mm}\) in planes 200 mm apart. Their resultant force is zero at every speed. On ideal low-friction supports there is no preferred heavy side in a static test. Their couple magnitude at 1800 rpm is \(0.000400(0.200)(188.5)^2=2.84\,\mathrm{N\,m}\), sufficient to generate equal opposing reactions at bearings. Measurements in two support planes can distinguish this pattern from a net shaking force. Near a flexible rotor’s bending critical speed, shaft deflection changes the mass distribution and response phase; the rigid-body correction formulas then require a modal or measured influence-coefficient model.

Interpret correction accuracy

An angular placement error also leaves a residual vector. A correction of the correct magnitude set one degree away leaves a residual approximately \(U\pi/180\) for small angle, since the tip of the vector follows a circle. At large operating speed even this residual produces a force scaling with the square of speed.

Derive a balance correction from a trial mass

An assembled rotor rarely reveals the angular location of its excess mass directly. A once-per-revolution vibration measurement supplies an amplitude and phase referred to a shaft mark. At fixed operating speed and linear support conditions, represent the measured complex vibration \(V\) by \(V=\alpha U\), where \(U\) is the complex unbalance in one plane and \(\alpha\) is the response per unit unbalance. This coefficient includes bearing compliance, dynamic amplification and the sensor’s phase lag. The phase of largest displacement is therefore not necessarily the angle where correction mass should be added.

Record the original vibration \(V_0\), add a known trial unbalance \(U_t\) and measure \(V_t\) at the same speed with the same phase reference. Subtraction cancels the original signal and gives

\[\alpha=(V_t-V_0)/U_t,\]
\[U_c=-V_0/\alpha .\]

Remove the trial before installing the calculated total correction. If the trial remains attached, install only \(U_c-U_t\) as further correction. Subtract complex vectors, not their magnitudes. A trial too small to resolve above measurement noise makes the inferred phase and correction unreliable; an unnecessarily large trial can take the rotor outside the measured linear range. A repeat baseline after removing the mass can expose a changing thermal or mounting condition.

Convert measured shaft phase into a physical correction

At fixed speed a laser reports an original synchronous displacement \(V_0=(30+40i)\,\mathrm{\mu m}\). Add a 5.0 g trial mass at a 40 mm radius at the reference angle, so \(U_t=200\,\mathrm{g\,mm}\). The new reading is \(V_t=(50+60i)\,\mathrm{\mu m}\). The influence coefficient is \((20+20i)/200=0.10+0.10i\) micrometres per g mm. The required correction is

\[U_c=-\frac{30+40i}{0.10+0.10i}=-(350+50i)\,\mathrm{g\,mm}.\]

Its magnitude is \(353.6\,\mathrm{g\,mm}\) and angle is 188.1° from the trial reference in the same positive rotational convention. With a correction radius of 50 mm, add about 7.07 g after removing the trial. Multiplying the correction vector by the measured coefficient gives \(-30-40i\) micrometres, cancelling the baseline in the linear model. If the trial must stay, the extra vector is \(-550-50i\) g mm. Installing the full correction in addition to the trial would leave its trial response as a predictable residual.

Single-plane correction minimises one measured response at one speed. For a long rotor with a separate couple unbalance, a response measured at one support may vanish while the opposite support continues to vibrate. Measuring both supports under trial masses in two planes produces a two-by-two complex influence matrix. Nearly proportional measured columns mean that the two trial planes cannot be distinguished reliably at those sensor locations, a conditioning issue that no amount of arithmetic precision can cure.

The initial amplitude in the example is 50 micrometres; the trial amplitude is 78.1 micrometres. Their scalar difference, 28.1 micrometres, happens to be close to the actual complex response increment’s magnitude of 28.3 micrometres, but the scalar comparison contains no correction angle. Even apparently favourable numbers cannot replace the shaft reference.

A balanced rotor can load its bearings during a turn

After a rotor is balanced, its spin angular momentum still has to change direction whenever its supporting structure turns. Consider an axisymmetric flywheel rotating rapidly at \(\omega_s\) about unit vector \(e_s\), with polar inertia \(I_s\). If its axis is steered at angular rate \(\Omega\), then \(\dot e_s=\Omega\times e_s\). Isolate the spin part of angular momentum and differentiate in an inertial frame:

\[H_s=I_s\omega_s e_s,\]
\[M_s=I_s\dot\omega_s e_s+\Omega\times H_s.\]

The first term spins the rotor up or down. The second turns its axis without changing its spin energy to leading order. For steady orthogonal steering the moment magnitude is \(I_s|\omega_s\Omega|\). Components of \(\Omega\) parallel to the spin axis do not turn the angular momentum. For slow regular precession of a fast rotor this spin-dominated relation also gives \(\Omega\simeq M/H_s\) for a transverse applied moment. Rapid steering acceleration, strong nutation or significant transverse angular momentum requires the full tensor equations.

A horizontally mounted flywheel on a turntable has \(I_s=0.12\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}\), spins in the positive \(x\) direction at 3000 rpm and is turned about positive \(z\) at 0.40 rad/s. Angular momentum is \(0.12(3000)(2\pi/60)=37.7\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s}\) along \(x\). Since \(e_z\times e_x=e_y\), the bearings must exert 15.1 N m along positive \(y\) on the rotor. A 0.30 m support separation along \(x\) requires a pair of equal opposite vertical bearing forces of 50.3 N to deliver this moment, in addition to weight and ordinary radial loads. Reversing the steering reverses the couple immediately at a given speed. A rigid imbalance force at fixed shaft speed does not reverse just because steering changes direction. That distinction is a practical way to diagnose the origin of a load measured on a moving platform.

Practise on sheet 5 · Balance the steering moment of a two-flywheel instrument. Further reading · MIT · 16.07 Dynamics · Lecture L30, 3D Rigid Body Dynamics: Tops and Gyroscopes, steady precession.

How accurately must a balance mass be placed?

A single-plane correction should cancel the original unbalance vector. If its magnitude ratio is \(k\) and its angle differs from perfect opposition by \(\delta\), vector addition leaves the normalised magnitude \(\sqrt{1+k^2-2k\cos\delta}\).

Critical speed, synchronous whirl and two different rotor centres · Vibration and identification in machinery

A stationary probe observes motion of a rotating shaft surface, while a balance calculation follows an eccentric mass centre. Derive both trajectories from one flexible-rotor model. Their difference explains the apparent recentring of a rotor at high speed and why a single vibration amplitude cannot identify unbalance and damping.

Chapter 10 · Energy coordinates and Lagrange’s equation

Derive a coupled translation–rotation equation with a rolling constraint and identify which reaction disappears from the energy balance.

From first principles

Derive the equation of motion using one independent coordinate

An ideal geometric constraint ties several motions together. Choose an independent coordinate \(q\) that automatically satisfies it and express kinetic energy \(T(q,\dot q)\) and potential energy \(V(q)\) in that coordinate. In the principle of virtual work, ideal constraint reactions do no work along admissible small motions. The balance of inertial, conservative and applied virtual work gives Lagrange's equation

\[\frac d{dt}\frac{\partial T}{\partial\dot q}-\frac{\partial T}{\partial q}+\frac{\partial V}{\partial q}=Q_{\rm nc},\]
\[\delta W_{\rm nc}=Q_{\rm nc}\,\delta q .\]

For a rigid cylinder rolling on a stationary surface without slip, take centre displacement \(q=x\) and angular speed \(\dot x/R\). Its translation and rotation contribute to kinetic energy; a horizontal spring attached at the centre supplies the potential.

\[T=\tfrac12(m+I_G/R^2)\dot x^2,\]
\[V=\tfrac12kx^2,\qquad (m+I_G/R^2)\ddot x+kx=Q_{\rm nc}.\]

Static friction supplies the contact reaction required for rolling. The contact point has zero instantaneous velocity, so that reaction contributes zero power here. The solution must still demand no more friction than the surface can supply.

Derivation and applications

Derive the effective stiffness of a pendulum on an accelerating cart

A continuously accelerating support changes both the equilibrium direction and the small-oscillation frequency of a hanging load. Model a point mass \(m\) on a massless rigid link of length \(\ell\) with an ideal hinge. Let the support move horizontally with imposed coordinate \(X(t)\) and measure angle \(\theta\) from the downward vertical towards positive horizontal displacement. Ignore drag. With mass coordinates \(x=X+\ell\sin\theta\) and \(y=-\ell\cos\theta\), the kinetic and potential energies are

\[T=\tfrac12m[\dot X^2+2\dot X\ell\dot\theta\cos\theta+\ell^2\dot\theta^2],\]
\[V=-mg\ell\cos\theta .\]

Differentiate \(\partial T/\partial\dot\theta\) with respect to time and subtract \(\partial T/\partial\theta\). The velocity cross terms involving \(\dot X\dot\theta\) cancel, leaving a forcing term set by support acceleration, independent of support speed.

\[\ell\ddot\theta+g\sin\theta+\ddot X\cos\theta=0 .\]

For constant support acceleration \(a\), a stationary angle obeys \(\tan\theta_0=-a/g\). Choose the hanging equilibrium. Expanding the restoring term about \(\theta_0\) gives the effective gravitational acceleration along this new direction and the frequency of small oscillations.

\[\theta=\theta_0+\eta,\]
\[\ddot\eta+\frac{\sqrt{g^2+a^2}}{\ell}\eta=0,\]
\[\omega_n=\sqrt{\frac{\sqrt{g^2+a^2}}{\ell}}.\]

A positive cart acceleration tips the pendulum backwards. The first correction to angle is linear in \(a/g\), while the first relative correction to frequency is quadratic. The exact equilibrium result holds at finite \(a\); the frequency expression requires small oscillations about it.

Worked example

Predict a suspended sensor's direction

A cart accelerates at 3.00 m/s² carrying a pendulum of length 0.600 m. Its steady angle is

\[-\arctan(3.00/9.81)=-17.0^\circ.\]

The effective acceleration is 10.26 m/s², giving \(\omega_n=4.13\) rad/s and period 1.52 s. A sensor that treats the pendulum direction as vertical during this manoeuvre would carry the full angular bias.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Infer acceleration from a steady deflection

In another rig a 0.400 m pendulum settles 8.00° backwards after weak damping removes its initial swing. Constant acceleration is

\[9.81\tan8.00^\circ=1.38\,\mathrm{m/s^2}.\]

The effective gravity is \(9.81/\cos8.00^\circ=9.91\) m/s², predicting a small-oscillation period \(2\pi\sqrt{0.400/9.91}=1.26\) s for weak damping. The angle is much more sensitive to modest acceleration than the period.

Let geometry remove a reaction

A cylinder rolls along a horizontal rail while a spring attached at its centre pulls it towards equilibrium. The rail friction is essential for rolling, but an ideal contact point is instantaneously at rest and the friction force does no work. One can write separate translational and rotational equations with an unknown friction force. Alternatively choose a coordinate that satisfies rolling from the beginning. The reduced energy then contains the rotational inertia without introducing a contact reaction.

Take the rightward displacement \(x\) of the cylinder centre as independent coordinate and clockwise angle as positive. On the fixed surface the no-slip condition gives \(x=R\theta\), up to a reference offset. A body of total mass \(m\) and centre inertia \(I_G\) has kinetic energy from both translation and rotation. A centre spring with stiffness \(k\) has elastic potential \(kx^2/2\).

\[T=\tfrac12(m+I_G/R^2)\dot x^2,\]
\[V=\tfrac12kx^2 .\]

Connect energy to a dynamical equation

The virtual work of a horizontal force \(F\) applied at the centre is \(F\delta x\). In this coordinate its generalised force is simply \(F\). A torque \(M\) acting clockwise would do \(M\delta\theta=(M/R)\delta x\) and would enter as a force \(M/R\). With a centre damper \(c\), the dissipative force is \(-c\dot x\). Lagrange’s equation for this system becomes

\[\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial T}{\partial\dot x}-\frac{\partial T}{\partial x}+\frac{\partial V}{\partial x}=F-c\dot x,\qquad (m+I_G/R^2)\ddot x+c\dot x+kx=F .\]

For coordinates whose inertia depends on position, retain both derivatives involving \(T\). The differentiation of \(\partial T/\partial\dot x\) then generates a term proportional to \(\dot x^2\), part of which cancels \(\partial T/\partial x\). Simply multiplying a configuration-dependent effective mass by acceleration would miss the remaining term.

Predict a frequency and check rolling

A solid cylinder has \(m=8.0\,\mathrm{kg}\), \(R=0.10\,\mathrm m\), and \(I_G=mR^2/2=0.040\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}\). The effective mass seen by the spring is

\[8+0.040/0.10^2=12.0\,\mathrm{kg}.\]

With \(k=480\,\mathrm{N/m}\) and negligible damping the natural frequency is

\[\sqrt{480/12}/(2\pi)=1.007\,\mathrm{Hz}.\]

Release it from rest at \(x=+0.060\,\mathrm m\). Its initial acceleration is

\[-480(0.060)/12=-2.40\,\mathrm{m/s^2}.\]

Writing only the centre’s translation and ignoring rotation would overpredict the acceleration magnitude.

Reconstruct the previously eliminated contact force from translation. The spring pulls leftwards with 28.8 N and the centre accelerates leftwards with \(m\ddot x=-19.2\,\mathrm N\). Friction must therefore point rightwards with 9.6 N to supply the appropriate angular acceleration. The coefficient of static friction must be at least \(9.6/(8\times9.81)=0.122\) at release. For a smaller coefficient, the assumed rolling constraint fails and the model needs separate rotational and translational coordinates together with a slipping contact law.

Compare two cylinders with identical mass

A thin-walled hoop of the same mass and rolling radius has \(I_G=mR^2\). With the original spring its effective mass doubles the translational mass, giving natural frequency \(\sqrt{480/16}/(2\pi)=0.872\,\mathrm{Hz}\). A no-slip ring-down can therefore distinguish two bodies whose masses and outer dimensions are identical but whose internal mass distributions differ.

Use a cyclic coordinate to reduce coupled motion

Mount a pendulum on a cart that can roll horizontally on a level frictionless guide. Let cart displacement be \(x\), bob angle \(\theta\) from downward vertical, length \(l\), bob mass \(m\) and remaining cart mass \(M\). The bob position is \((x+l\sin\theta,-l\cos\theta)\). Differentiating and squaring its speed gives a cross term \(2l\dot x\dot\theta\cos\theta\) because cart and pendulum velocities need not be perpendicular. The full energy terms are

\[T=\tfrac12(M+m)\dot x^2+ml\cos\theta\,\dot x\dot\theta+\tfrac12ml^2\dot\theta^2,\]
\[V=mgl(1-\cos\theta).\]

Horizontal position itself does not occur in either expression. It is a cyclic coordinate, so \(\partial T/\partial\dot x=(M+m)\dot x+ml\cos\theta\,\dot\theta\) is conserved if no external horizontal force acts. This is horizontal momentum written in generalised coordinates. The pendulum equation from Lagrange’s equation is

\[l\ddot\theta+\ddot x\cos\theta+g\sin\theta=0.\]

For small swings from rest, differentiate the momentum relation and linearise to obtain

\[\ddot x=-ml\ddot\theta/(M+m).\]

Substituting gives

\[[lM/(M+m)]\ddot\theta+g\theta=0.\]

Free horizontal motion makes the small-swing frequency larger than for a clamped cart, by \(\sqrt{(M+m)/M}\). This result reflects motion about the system’s horizontally constrained centre of mass. The extra degree of freedom also permits uniform horizontal translation at zero frequency. Its presence does not imply an additional oscillatory mode.

Predict opposing cart travel in a release test

Let \(M=3.0\) kg, \(m=1.0\) kg and \(l=0.60\) m. Clamp the cart: the oscillation period would be \(2\pi\sqrt{0.60/9.81}=1.55\,\mathrm s\). Release its horizontal guide and the period becomes

\[2\pi\sqrt{0.60(3)/(9.81\times4)}=1.35\,\mathrm s.\]

At release the bob is at \(\theta_0=10^\circ\) and both bodies are at rest. Because horizontal momentum remains zero, integrate the exact relation to obtain

\[(M+m)x+ml\sin\theta=\text{constant}.\]

As the bob first crosses the vertical, the cart has shifted \(ml\sin10^\circ/(M+m)=0.0260\,\mathrm m\) to the right from its starting point.

At the vertical the potential-energy decrease is

\[mgl(1-\cos10^\circ)=0.0894\,\mathrm J.\]

Substituting \(\dot x=-ml\dot\theta/(M+m)\) into the kinetic energy at this instant gives effective angular inertia \(ml^2M/(M+m)=0.270\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}\). Thus the angular speed magnitude is \(\sqrt{2(0.0894)/0.270}=0.814\,\mathrm{rad/s}\), and the cart speed magnitude is \(0.15(0.814)=0.122\,\mathrm{m/s}\). The frequency estimate used a small-angle approximation; the centre-of-mass and energy results at the vertical used the actual release angle. A horizontal cable pulling on the cart or measurable wheel friction would change the conserved momentum and should be measured before attributing deviations solely to pendulum nonlinearity.

The two-sensor experiment can measure conservation without estimating acceleration. Track the cart position and bob angle from video, then plot \(x+[ml/(M+m)]\sin\theta\). For the chosen masses its value should stay constant at the initial offset despite the cart and pendulum both moving. A steadily sloping line indicates nonzero initial horizontal momentum; a curved departure suggests an external force or tracking bias. This separation is useful because rolling resistance can be weak enough to leave the first measured period nearly unchanged while producing visible drift over several cycles. The conserved combination is specific to the inertial laboratory coordinate, so a camera fixed on the moving cart needs a separate record of cart displacement.

Problem sheet 5 · Balancing and constrained motion

Separate the net and couple unbalance of a rotor and recover the forces required by ideal rolling.

Add two angular unbalances

A 10 g mass at radius 30 mm and angle 0° shares a plane with an 8 g mass at radius 50 mm and angle 90°. Find the mass and angular position to add at radius 40 mm. Compute the original force amplitude at 1200 rpm.

Reveal the worked solution

The unbalance is \((300,400)\,\mathrm{g\,mm}\) with magnitude 500 g mm and angle 53.13°. The correction opposes it at 233.13°, and its mass is

\[500/40=12.5\,\mathrm g.\]

In SI units the force amplitude is

\[0.0005(2\pi1200/60)^2=7.90\,\mathrm N.\]

Correct an overhung imbalance

An imbalance of 600 g mm at angle 0° lies 50 mm beyond plane B. A and B are 200 mm apart. Find both correction vectors, then give the required added masses at respective radii of 25 and 50 mm. Why do the corrections point in different directions?

Reveal the worked solution

With A as origin, \(200U_B=-250(600)\) gives \(U_B=-750\,\mathrm{g\,mm}\). Force balance gives \(U_A=+150\,\mathrm{g\,mm}\). Add 15 g at 180° in B and 6 g at 0° in A. A correction between the planes cannot reproduce the larger moment arm of the overhang without an opposing correction to recover the required resultant.

Roll a ring down a slope

A thin hoop of mass 4 kg rolls without slipping down a 12° slope. Derive its acceleration, the friction force magnitude and minimum coefficient of static friction. Assume no rolling resistance.

Reveal the worked solution

The energy-coordinate effective mass is

\[m+I/R^2=2m.\]

Gravity supplies \(mg\sin12^\circ\), so

\[a=g\sin12^\circ/2=1.020\,\mathrm{m/s^2}.\]

Translational balance requires uphill friction \(mg\sin12^\circ-ma=4.08\,\mathrm N\). With normal load \(mg\cos12^\circ\), the minimum coefficient is

\[\tan12^\circ/2=0.106.\]

The friction supplies torque while its instantaneous contact point is at rest.

Balance the steering moment of a two-flywheel instrument

Two coaxial flywheels spin oppositely at 2400 rpm while their frame turns at 0.30 rad/s about a perpendicular axis. Their polar inertias are 0.080 and 0.060 kg m². Find the residual couple. What speed of the smaller wheel cancels the couple if the larger wheel retains its speed?

Show the worked solution

The signed net angular momentum magnitude is

\[(0.080-0.060)(2400)(2\pi/60)=5.03\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s}.\]

The moment is \(0.30(5.03)=1.51\,\mathrm{N\,m}\). Its direction follows the turn rate crossed into the larger wheel’s spin. Cancelling angular momentum requires \(0.060n_{\rm small}=0.080(2400)\), giving 3200 rpm in the opposing sense. Equal speeds alone cancel only if the polar inertias also match. Changes of either spin speed during the turn add separate axial torques.

Revisit chapter 9 · A balanced rotor can load its bearings during a turn.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: Static and couple balance of rotors; Energy coordinates and Lagrange’s equation. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Chapter 11 · Modal participation and absorber tuning

Project external forcing onto vibration modes and determine when a vibration absorber suppresses displacement at a selected frequency.

From first principles

Project a physical force onto a vibration mode

For small undamped motions a structure obeys \(M\ddot{\mathbf x}+K\mathbf x=\mathbf f(t)\) with symmetric mass and stiffness matrices. Its normal modes satisfy

\[K\boldsymbol\phi_j=\omega_j^2M\boldsymbol\phi_j.\]

Expand displacement in these mode shapes, using time-dependent coefficients \(q_j\). Premultiply the result by the transpose of one mode.

\[\mathbf x=\sum_j\boldsymbol\phi_j q_j,\]
\[\sum_j(\boldsymbol\phi_i^\mathsf TM\boldsymbol\phi_j\,\ddot q_j+\boldsymbol\phi_i^\mathsf TK\boldsymbol\phi_j\,q_j)=\boldsymbol\phi_i^\mathsf T\mathbf f .\]

For distinct frequencies, symmetry makes different modes orthogonal under both matrices. The corresponding off-diagonal products vanish, leaving a separate scalar oscillator for the selected mode.

\[m_i\ddot q_i+m_i\omega_i^2q_i=Q_i(t),\]
\[m_i=\boldsymbol\phi_i^\mathsf TM\boldsymbol\phi_i,\]
\[Q_i=\boldsymbol\phi_i^\mathsf T\mathbf f .\]

The force projection has a direct interpretation as work per unit modal displacement. A symmetric load can have zero projection onto an antisymmetric mode despite a large total force. The displacement at a particular sensor is obtained only after adding its components from all excited modes.

Background · Modal participation
\[Q_j=\phi_j^\mathsf T f\]

A mode is excited according to its overlap with the force. Always state how its mode vector and modal mass were normalised.

Derivation and applications

Determine how a point force appears in collocated modal measurements

The displacement measured at the force location can have a zero between two structural resonances. Consider an undamped linear structure with two relevant modes, distinct natural frequencies \(\omega_1<\omega_2\) and modal masses \(m_j>0\). Apply a harmonic point force of complex amplitude \(F\) at coordinate \(a\) and measure displacement at the same coordinate. For modal shape component \(\phi_j(a)\), the generalised force is \(\phi_j(a)F\), and reconstruction at the sensor supplies a second factor of that component.

\[q_j=\frac{\phi_j(a)F}{m_j(\omega_j^2-\omega^2)},\]
\[H_{aa}=\frac{x_a}{F}=\frac{A_1}{\omega_1^2-\omega^2}+\frac{A_2}{\omega_2^2-\omega^2},\]
\[A_j=\frac{\phi_j(a)^2}{m_j}.\]

The residues \(A_j\) are positive when both modes are visible. Between the two poles the first denominator is negative and the second positive. Set the sum to zero and multiply through by both denominators to find the antiresonance.

\[A_1(\omega_2^2-\omega_z^2)+A_2(\omega_1^2-\omega_z^2)=0,\]
\[\omega_z^2=\frac{A_1\omega_2^2+A_2\omega_1^2}{A_1+A_2}.\]

A zero in the measured displacement can therefore result from cancellation of two finite modal displacements. Its position also reveals their relative participation without requiring the absolute force calibration.

\[\frac{A_1}{A_2}=\frac{\omega_z^2-\omega_1^2}{\omega_2^2-\omega_z^2}.\]

Separated force and response locations can give signed residues and change the interlacing. With damping a finite minimum commonly replaces the exact zero, so using its frequency as an exact antiresonance is an approximation.

Worked example

Predict the dip of a two-mode stage

For \(\omega_1=10.0\) rad/s, \(\omega_2=20.0\) rad/s and \(A_1=2A_2\), the squared zero frequency is \((2\times400+100)/3=300\) s⁻², hence \(\omega_z=17.3\) rad/s. At that frequency the first contribution is \(2A_2/(100-300)=-0.0100A_2\) and the second is

\[A_2/(400-300)=+0.0100A_2.\]

The sensor is stationary while both modes respond.

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Recover a relative participation from a measured minimum

A different collocated test gives resonances at 30.0 and 50.0 Hz and a negligible-damping minimum at 40.0 Hz. Ratios of squared angular frequencies can equally be expressed through squared frequencies in hertz. The residue ratio is

\[(40^2-30^2)/(50^2-40^2)=7/9=0.778.\]

With equal modal masses the first shape magnitude at the force point is \(\sqrt{7/9}=0.882\) of the second.
