OneSimulation laboratory · Separate the effect of mass from rotary inertia

Recover motion from mode amplitudes

Knowing a structure’s natural frequencies does not yet tell you which frequencies a particular actuator will excite. For a linear undamped system write \(M\ddot x+Kx=f(t)\) and let \(\phi_j\) be a real mode shape solving \(K\phi_j=\omega_j^2M\phi_j\). Assume a complete set of independent modes and expand the displacement \(x=\sum_j\phi_j q_j\). Multiplying the equation by \(\phi_i^T\) uses mass and stiffness orthogonality to remove other modal coordinates.

\[m_i\ddot q_i+k_iq_i=\phi_i^Tf(t),\]
\[m_i=\phi_i^TM\phi_i,\]
\[k_i=\omega_i^2m_i .\]

Multiplying a mode shape by a constant changes the numerical modal mass and generalised force, while the reconstructed physical motion remains invariant. Thus a tabulated mode shape needs its normalisation if it is used to compute a physical response. A forcing pattern whose projection vanishes excites none of that mode from rest, even if the excitation frequency equals its natural frequency.

Select a mode by choosing the actuator pattern

Suppose two identical masses 2 kg each have mode shapes \((1,1)\) and \((1,-1)\), with frequencies 10 and 20 rad/s. Both modal masses are 4 kg. A 12 N harmonic force on the first mass projects 12 N onto each mode in this normalisation. At a forcing frequency of 5 rad/s the undamped steady modal amplitudes are

\[12/[4(10^2-5^2)]=0.0400\,\mathrm m,\]
\[12/[4(20^2-5^2)]=0.00800\,\mathrm m.\]

Recombination gives first and second physical amplitudes 0.0480 and 0.0320 m.

Now distribute the same total force as 6 N on each mass in phase. The first projection stays 12 N and the second becomes \(6-6=0\). The masses now both move 0.0400 m. Relocating or phasing actuators can therefore change observed vibration without changing any mass, spring or natural frequency.

Cause the primary displacement to vanish

Attach an auxiliary mass \(m_a\) by spring \(k_a\) to a primary mass \(m_p\) grounded by spring \(k_p\). A harmonic force of amplitude \(F\) acts on the primary mass. Seek displacement amplitudes \(X_p,X_a\). The absorber equation is

\[-k_aX_p+(k_a-m_a\omega^2)X_a=0.\]

At \(\omega^2=k_a/m_a\) this enforces \(X_p=0\). The primary equation then gives

\[X_a=-F/k_a.\]

The absorber’s spring force balances the excitation while the primary mass is stationary in this ideal steady response.

For \(m_a=0.50\,\mathrm{kg}\), a measured forcing frequency of 20 Hz requires

\[k_a=m_a(2\pi20)^2=7896\,\mathrm{N/m}.\]

An 8 N forcing amplitude gives absorber travel amplitude 1.01 mm. The two coupled resonances bracket this tuned antiresonance; startup or a change of shaft speed can pass through them. Damping limits the resonant peaks but generally prevents the exact cancellation obtained from these undamped equations.

OneSimulation · How can two resonant modes cancel the primary displacement?

Apply a sinusoidal force to a 5 kg primary mass supported by a 500 N/m spring. An attached 0.5 kg absorber connects through a 50 N/m spring. At the undamped tuning frequency the absorber equation forces the primary displacement to zero. The full coupled system still has two distinct natural frequencies. With mass-normalised modes and the force and displacement at coordinate 1, the receptance is

\[\frac{X_1}{F_1}=\sum_j\frac{\phi_{1j}^2}{\omega_j^2-\omega^2+2i\zeta_j\omega_j\omega}.\]

OneSimulation obtains the eigenpairs from the mass-scaled stiffness. The eigenfrequencies are 1.36 and 1.86 Hz. Between these frequencies the two undamped real contributions have opposite signs. They cancel at 1.59 Hz. The code checks this zero and independently verifies the static compliance. Increase the common modal damping ratio and inspect the notch: losses shift the modal phases, so the exact zero becomes a finite minimum.

"""A force on the primary mass excites two modes of a tuned absorber."""
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
from onesim.numerics.linear.matrices.dense_linear_algebra import symmetric_eigensystem

mass = np.array([5., 0.5])  # kg, primary and absorber
support, spring = 500., 50.  # N/m, support and connecting spring
stiffness = np.array([[support+spring, -spring], [-spring, spring]])
scale = np.diag(1/np.sqrt(mass))
omega2, shapes = symmetric_eigensystem(scale @ stiffness @ scale)
modes = scale @ shapes
residues = modes[0]**2      # applied force and measured motion both at mass 1
tuned = np.sqrt(spring/mass[1])
assert abs(np.sum(residues/(omega2 - tuned**2))) < 1e-12
assert np.isclose(np.sum(residues/omega2), 1/support)

frequency = np.linspace(1.0, 2.3, 600)  # Hz
omega = 2*np.pi*frequency[:, None]
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(7, 4), constrained_layout=True)
for damping, colour in [(0.005, "#19596a"), (0.04, "#a5442e")]:
    denominator = omega2 - omega**2 + 2j*damping*np.sqrt(omega2)*omega
    response = np.sum(residues / denominator, axis=1)
    ax.semilogy(frequency, 1000*abs(response), color=colour,
                label=f"Both modal damping ratios {100*damping:g}%")

ax.axvline(tuned/(2*np.pi), color="0.5", linestyle=":", label="Undamped cancellation")
ax.set(xlabel="Excitation frequency (Hz)", ylabel="Primary displacement / force (mm/N)")
ax.legend(fontsize=8)
fig.savefig("machine-dynamics.png", dpi=170)

Choose where to observe cancellation

At the tuned absorber frequency a sensor on the primary reports zero ideal steady displacement, while a sensor on the attached mass records finite motion. A force transducer between the bodies measures the full applied excitation in the opposing phase. Moving the sensor can therefore turn the same test from an apparent null into a readily measurable response, without altering the underlying eigenfrequencies.

Project a spatially distributed load onto a flexible member

A beam or string has motion distributed along its length. Its modal forcing is determined by a spatial integral, analogous to the vector dot product for discrete coordinates. For a taut uniform string of length \(L\), linear density \(\mu\) and constant tension \(H\), small transverse displacement \(w(x,t)\) obeys \(\mu w_{tt}-H w_{xx}=p(x,t)\). The tension term follows from the difference in vertical tension components on a short segment. Assume both ends fixed in displacement and neglect bending stiffness, damping and the change of tension caused by stretching. The modes are \(\phi_n=\sin(n\pi x/L)\), with frequencies \(\omega_n=(n\pi/L)\sqrt{H/\mu}\).

Expand \(w=\sum\phi_n q_n\), multiply by \(\phi_j\) and integrate over the length. The mass integral is

\[m_j=\mu\int_0^L\phi_j^2dx=\mu L/2.\]

Integration by parts of the tension term gives the modal stiffness \(k_j=m_j\omega_j^2\), with the fixed-end boundary terms vanishing. The modal input becomes

\[Q_j=\int_0^Lp\phi_jdx.\]

Under a uniform harmonic load amplitude \(p_0\),

\[Q_j=\frac{p_0L}{j\pi}[1-(-1)^j].\]

Even modes have zero generalised force because their positive and negative portions cancel exactly. A point load at \(x_0\) instead yields

\[Q_j=F\phi_j(x_0).\]

Moving an actuator or changing a load footprint can thus suppress a resonance by symmetry without changing the material, tension or natural frequency.

Estimate a cable’s displacement from its first few modes

A laboratory cable spans 2.0 m under 200 N tension and has linear density 0.50 kg/m. Its wave speed is \(20\,\mathrm{m/s}\), so \(f_1=20/(2L)=5.0\,\mathrm{Hz}\) and \(m_j=0.50\,\mathrm{kg}\). Apply a distributed harmonic pressure-equivalent line load \(p_0=0.40\,\mathrm{N/m}\) at 2.0 Hz. For mode one, \(Q_1=2(0.40)(2)/\pi=0.509\,\mathrm N\), and its dynamic stiffness is

\[0.50[(2\pi5)^2-(2\pi2)^2]=414.5\,\mathrm{N/m}.\]

Its modal amplitude is 1.23 mm.

The third mode has \(Q_3=0.170\,\mathrm N\), \(f_3=15\) Hz and amplitude \(0.170/\{0.50[(2\pi15)^2-(2\pi2)^2]\}=0.0390\,\mathrm{mm}\). At the midpoint \(\phi_1=+1\) while \(\phi_3=-1\), so the two-term prediction is approximately 1.19 mm. The next odd mode contributes with the opposite sign again, but is far stiffer at this drive frequency. In the static limit an independent check follows from the parabolic string solution \(w(L/2)=p_0L^2/(8H)=1.00\,\mathrm{mm}\). If a strong measured response appears at the symmetric load’s even-mode resonance, investigate load asymmetry, asymmetric support stiffness or a disturbed initial condition before assigning a nonzero ideal modal projection.

The spatial convergence pattern can be estimated before adding many terms. For odd mode number \(n\) well above the forcing frequency, the uniform-load projection decreases as \(1/n\) while modal stiffness increases as \(n^2\). Each displacement contribution therefore decreases roughly as \(1/n^3\). A point actuator at midspan instead gives modal projections of essentially alternating constant magnitude for odd modes, producing only a \(1/n^2\) displacement decay. Hence a truncation that accurately predicts the response to a distributed flow load can be less accurate for an impulsive local tap. Strain or slope predictions may require still more terms because a spatial derivative introduces an additional factor of mode number. Choose the retained modes for the actual load and observed quantity.

The uniform cable problem admits an independent harmonic calculation without modal truncation. Set \(w(x,t)=W(x)\cos\Omega t\). The governing equation gives

\[W''+\kappa^2 W=-p_0/H,\]
\[\kappa=\Omega\sqrt{\mu/H}.\]

Symmetry about midspan and fixed ends select \(W(x)=[p_0/(\mu\Omega^2)]\{\cos[\kappa(x-L/2)]/\cos(\kappa L/2)-1\}\), provided the denominator is nonzero. For the test above \(\kappa L/2=0.6283\) and \(W(L/2)=0.40[1/\cos(0.6283)-1]/[0.50(2\pi2)^2]=1.196\,\mathrm{mm}\). This direct boundary-value result confirms both the signs and the scale of the first two odd-modal contributions.

As the excitation approaches the first natural frequency, \(\kappa L/2\) approaches \(\pi/2\) and the undamped steady expression becomes singular. An actual time record may instead show progressively growing amplitude before damping or geometric effects intervene. At very low frequency expand \(\sec q\simeq1+q^2/2\); substituting \(q=\Omega L\sqrt{\mu/H}/2\) recovers the static midpoint displacement without subtracting two nearly equal numerical values. The modal and spatial calculations thus furnish independent checks in the static, off-resonant and resonant limits.

What happens to the notch of a tuned absorber?

Attach an absorber of mass 0.10 times the primary mass, tuned to the primary’s undamped frequency. Include 2.5% primary damping. Solve the complex two-mass force balance for primary displacement under harmonic forcing; the absorber spring and dashpot act on relative motion.

Why a local damper couples otherwise separate modes · Vibration and identification in machinery

Undamped mode shapes diagonalise a symmetric mass and stiffness model. Adding a damper at one attachment does not generally leave modal equations separate. Derive the resulting coupling, test its magnitude and learn when a diagonal modal-damping approximation can hide an observable phase error.

Chapter 12 · Finite-amplitude oscillations and model validity

Use conservation of energy and a frequency approximation to quantify when a small-angle pendulum model loses timing accuracy.

From first principles

Derive the pendulum period and identify the small-angle assumption

A point mass \(m\) suspended on a massless rigid link of length \(\ell\) feels a gravitational torque \(-mg\ell\sin\theta\) about the hinge. Assume a stationary pivot and negligible drag. Angular momentum balance gives an exact nonlinear equation. Replacing \(\sin\theta\) by \(\theta\) only for small angles in radians gives a linear oscillator and its predicted period.

\[m\ell^2\ddot\theta=-mg\ell\sin\theta,\]
\[T_{\rm small}=2\pi\sqrt{\ell/g}.\]

To track finite amplitude, release from rest at angle \(A<\pi\) and use mechanical energy conservation. At any intermediate angle, loss of potential energy equals gained rotational kinetic energy.

\[\tfrac12m\ell^2\dot\theta^2=mg\ell(\cos\theta-\cos A),\]
\[T=4\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{2g}}\int_0^A\frac{d\theta}{\sqrt{\cos\theta-\cos A}} .\]

The factor four uses the symmetry of the four quarter cycles. The mass cancels even at large amplitude. The exact restoring torque grows more slowly with deflection than its small-angle approximation, so finite-amplitude swings take longer. Measured variation of period with release angle directly tests the linearisation.

Derivation and applications

Derive the period of an oscillator with purely cubic stiffness

A spring with almost no linear stiffness near equilibrium can have a period set primarily by release amplitude. Consider mass \(m\) on a horizontal guide with restoring force \(-\beta x^3\) for \(\beta>0\). Ignore damping and assume the fitted force law holds between \(-A\) and \(+A\). Release from rest at \(x=A\). Integrating the force law gives a quartic potential, and conservation of energy provides the speed anywhere on the inward leg.

\[\tfrac12m\dot x^2+\tfrac14\beta x^4=\tfrac14\beta A^4,\]
\[|\dot x|=\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{2m}}\sqrt{A^4-x^4}.\]

By symmetry the time from an extreme position to the centre is one quarter of a full cycle. Write \(dt=|dx|/|\dot x|\) and substitute \(u=x/A\) in the integral. This isolates the amplitude dependence without guessing a sinusoidal shape.

\[T=\frac{4}{A}\sqrt{\frac{2m}{\beta}}\int_0^1\frac{du}{\sqrt{1-u^4}}.\]

The dimensionless integral evaluates numerically to 1.31103. Its endpoint singularity is integrable because the speed near the turning point vanishes as the square root of the distance remaining. The resulting period and angular frequency are

\[T=\frac{7.4163}{A}\sqrt{\frac{m}{\beta}},\]
\[\omega=\frac{2\pi}{T}=0.8472\,A\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{m}} .\]

The positive cubic restores the mass for displacements of either sign, but its tangent stiffness at zero is zero. The full nonlinear dynamics therefore predicts a period diverging with decreasing amplitude. Even a weak residual linear spring would eventually dominate in that limit.

Worked example

Predict the timing of two releases

Take \(m=0.500\) kg, \(\beta=2.00\times10^5\) N/m³ and \(A=20.0\) mm. The predicted period is

\[7.4163\sqrt{0.500/(2.00\times10^5)}/0.0200=0.586\,\mathrm{s}.\]

Reducing the release to 10.0 mm doubles the predicted period to 1.17 s. At the larger amplitude the centre-crossing speed is

\[A^2\sqrt{\beta/(2m)}=0.179\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

Change the inputs and inspect this calculation below

Second application

Infer stiffness from a measured period

A second fixture of mass 0.800 kg completes a cycle in 0.750 s after release at 15.0 mm. The implied coefficient is \(\beta=m[7.4163/(AT)]^2=3.48\times10^5\) N/m³. Its predicted restoring-force magnitude at release is

\[\beta A^3=1.17\,\mathrm{N}.\]

An independent static force measurement there tests the dynamic estimate without assuming a sinusoidal motion.

Open this live calculation

Retain the actual restoring moment

A pendulum gives a practical test of a linear model because its length and release angle are easy to set while its period can be measured over many cycles. Approximate the moving body first by a point mass \(m\) at distance \(l\) from a frictionless pivot. With \(\theta\) measured from vertically downwards, gravity produces torque \(-mgl\sin\theta\). Dividing the rotational balance by \(ml^2\) leaves \(\ddot\theta+(g/l)\sin\theta=0\). The linear small-angle frequency \(\omega_0=\sqrt{g/l}\) follows only after replacing \(\sin\theta\) by \(\theta\) in radians.

The leading correction has a definite sign:

\[\sin\theta\simeq\theta-\theta^3/6.\]

At a large positive angle the restoring force magnitude is smaller than the linear extrapolation. The pendulum should take longer to complete its swing. A sign test like this is useful before calculating a period correction.

Derive an integral valid at finite amplitude

For release from rest at amplitude \(A<\pi\), conservation of mechanical energy gives

\[\tfrac12ml^2\dot\theta^2+mgl(1-\cos\theta)=mgl(1-\cos A).\]

During one quarter of the orbit the angle travels between zero and \(A\). Substitute \(\sin(\theta/2)=\sin(A/2)\sin\psi\) into the time integral. Cancelling common square roots gives

\[T=4\sqrt{l/g}\int_0^{\pi/2}\frac{d\psi}{\sqrt{1-k^2\sin^2\psi}},\]
\[k=\sin(A/2).\]

For modest amplitudes expand the integrand as \(1+\tfrac12 k^2\sin^2\psi+\cdots\). The average of \(\sin^2\psi\) over the quarter interval is one half. Therefore \(T/T_0\simeq1+k^2/4\simeq1+A^2/16\) through second order in amplitude. The oscillation period depends on energy, while in the linear spring model all amplitudes share the same period.

Estimate a measurable timing error

Take \(l=0.80\,\mathrm m\), \(g=9.81\,\mathrm{m/s^2}\) and release at \(A=30^\circ=0.5236\,\mathrm{rad}\). The small-angle period is

\[T_0=2\pi\sqrt{0.80/9.81}=1.794\,\mathrm s.\]

The leading correction is 1.71%, giving about 1.825 s. Over ten full oscillations the predicted delay relative to the small-angle model is roughly 0.31 s. Reducing the release angle to 15° reduces the leading relative correction by a factor of four.

A timing series from a damped experimental pendulum may show the period drifting towards \(T_0\) as its amplitude decays. Measuring only the first release angle while averaging many later periods mixes different amplitudes. To test the finite-amplitude law, use consecutive same-direction crossings to measure each local period and estimate its associated amplitude from neighbouring turning points. The correction above is an expansion around zero amplitude. As \(A\) approaches the inverted equilibrium, the exact integral grows without bound and a low-order polynomial is no longer an adequate approximation.

Check the scale of the neglected term

Retaining one further term in the integrand gives \(1+\tfrac12 k^2\sin^2\psi+\tfrac38 k^4\sin^4\psi\). The quarter-interval average of the fourth power of sine is \(3/8\), so \(T/T_0\simeq1+k^2/4+9k^4/64\). At 30° this gives 1.01738, while the leading expansion in amplitude gave 1.01713. The small difference is measurable only if the uncertainty in pendulum length, release angle and crossing times supports the comparison.

Derive an amplitude-dependent resonance relation

A pendulum softens as its amplitude grows because the sine restoring law rises more slowly than its linear tangent. A mechanical flexure can instead harden through geometry. A simple modal model is \(m\ddot x+c\dot x+kx+\beta x^3=F\cos\omega t\). For positive \(\beta\) the restoring stiffness increases with excursion. Assume one dominant harmonic and take \(x\approx A\cos(\omega t-\phi)\). Expanding the cubic gives

\[x^3=A^3[3\cos(\omega t-\phi)+\cos(3\omega t-3\phi)]/4.\]

Keeping only the fundamental turns the cubic term into an amplitude-dependent in-phase force.

Balance in-phase and quadrature components against the input, square and add to eliminate \(\phi\). The first-harmonic amplitude estimate satisfies

\[F^2=A^2\left[\left(k+\tfrac34\beta A^2-m\omega^2\right)^2+(c\omega)^2\right].\]

In a lightly damped free motion the same approximation gives a backbone frequency \(\omega_b^2\approx[k+(3/4)\beta A^2]/m\). This is an amplitude-dependent estimate of conservative oscillation frequency, not a new eigenvalue of the linearised equilibrium. At very small amplitude it recovers \(\sqrt{k/m}\), while a negative cubic coefficient tilts the relation towards lower frequencies. If the cubic approximation is only a local expansion, do not extrapolate it until it predicts an unphysical loss of global stiffness.

Identify a hardening spring from two release amplitudes

A test component has moving mass 1.0 kg and small-amplitude stiffness 400 N/m. Low-level ring-down gives \(\omega_0=20\,\mathrm{rad/s}\). At measured oscillation amplitude 20 mm the dominant free frequency is approximately 22 rad/s. Under the weakly nonlinear one-harmonic model, \(\beta\simeq4m(22^2-20^2)/[3(0.020)^2]=2.80\times10^5\,\mathrm{N/m^3}\). At 10 mm the inferred backbone is \(\sqrt{400+0.75(2.8\times10^5)(0.010)^2}=20.52\,\mathrm{rad/s}\), a direct third reading against which to test the fit.

Now drive near 22 rad/s with viscous coefficient \(c=0.80\,\mathrm{N\,s/m}\). At the 20 mm backbone amplitude the spring and inertia fundamentals cancel, so maintaining this motion takes forcing \(F=c\omega A=0.352\,\mathrm N\) in quadrature with displacement. The neglected cubic third-harmonic force amplitude is

\[\beta A^3/4=0.56\,\mathrm N;\]

its displacement effect can nevertheless be small because the oscillator is driven far from its fundamental resonance at 66 rad/s. A separate mode near that frequency would invalidate this simplification. In a forced frequency sweep the amplitude equation can admit several positive roots near resonance. Its algebra does not establish their stability; slow upward and downward sweeps, each with sufficient settling time, can reveal different stable responses and jump transitions.

An initial static measurement tests the sign of the fitted coefficient independently. At a displacement of 20 mm the linear restoring force is

\[400(0.020)=8.0\,\mathrm N;\]

the cubic contribution is

\[2.8\times10^5(0.020)^3=2.24\,\mathrm N.\]

The predicted static force is therefore 10.24 N, and the tangent stiffness is

\[400+3(2.8\times10^5)(0.020)^2=736\,\mathrm{N/m}.\]

This tangent stiffness applies to small vibrations around a held biased equilibrium. Using it directly for an oscillation centred at zero would overestimate the measured amplitude-dependent frequency because the instantaneous stiffness changes during each cycle. Static bias and free oscillation amplitude are physically different tests of the same nonlinear restoring law.

Repeating a decay test around a deliberately shifted equilibrium distinguishes the two experimentally.

Set an amplitude limit for a pendulum-based timing model

For a rigid pendulum released from rest the exact period ratio is \(2K(\sin^2(\theta_0/2))/\pi\), where the complete elliptic integral here uses its parameter rather than its modulus. Compare it with the first amplitude correction \(1+\theta_0^2/16\), with angle in radians.

Problem sheet 6 · Modal selectivity and finite amplitude

Determine whether a force excites a mode and relate an oscillator’s amplitude to the accuracy of a period measurement.

Excite an antisymmetric mode

Two masses of 1.5 kg have symmetric and antisymmetric mode shapes (1,1) and (1,−1) at natural frequencies 12 and 18 rad/s. Apply harmonic forces (+6,−6) N at 6 rad/s. Find both modal amplitudes and the physical displacement amplitudes with their relative phase.

Reveal the worked solution

Both modal masses are 3 kg. The symmetric projection is zero, and the antisymmetric projection is 12 N. Hence its amplitude is

\[12/[3(18^2-6^2)]=0.01389\,\mathrm m.\]

The physical amplitudes are +13.89 and −13.89 mm in a common phase convention. The cancellation of the symmetric force projection determines the response symmetry.

Set a working stroke

An auxiliary mass moves against a spring attached to a primary machine. An undamped absorber must cancel an 18 N harmonic force at 25 Hz while its own amplitude stays at or below 1.5 mm. Find minimum spring stiffness and corresponding tuned mass. Does tuning ensure a small response at all speeds?

Reveal the worked solution

At exact cancellation \(X_p=0\) and \(|X_a|=F/k_a\). Thus

\[k_a\ge18/0.0015=12000\,\mathrm{N/m}.\]

At the limiting stiffness the tuned mass is

\[12000/(2\pi25)^2=0.486\,\mathrm{kg}.\]

The coupled modes give resonances away from the cancellation frequency. A varying-speed drive can cross these frequencies.

Estimate a release-angle limit

A pendulum experiment tolerates an amplitude-dependent period change of 0.5%. Use the leading finite-amplitude correction to estimate its largest release angle. For a 1.0 m pendulum released at half that angle, estimate the accumulated timing shift over 40 cycles.

Reveal the worked solution

The leading fraction is \(A^2/16\). Setting it to 0.005 gives

\[A=0.2828\,\mathrm{rad}=16.2^\circ.\]

Halving the angle reduces the leading correction to 0.00125. The small-angle period is 2.006 s, giving \(40(2.006)(0.00125)=0.100\,\mathrm s\) delay. Precision beyond the retained order would require a higher-order term or the exact integral.

Specify an isolation mount from two measurements

A 40.0 kg instrument is mounted on springs. Free-vibration peaks of the same sign are 8.0 and 4.0 mm four periods apart. The four periods take 1.60 s. Estimate the equivalent stiffness and damping. A bench beneath the instrument moves sinusoidally at 10 Hz with 0.50 mm amplitude. Predict absolute instrument motion and relative mount travel.

Worked solution

The logarithmic decrement per period is ln(2)/4 = 0.1733 and the damping ratio is δ/√(4π²+δ²) = 0.0276. The damped period is 0.40 s, giving ωd = 15.71 rad/s. The undamped frequency ωn = ωd/√(1−ζ²) is 15.71 rad/s to the displayed precision. The equivalent k = mωn² ≈ 9.88 kN/m and c = 2ζmωn ≈ 34.7 N s/m.

At r = ω/ωn ≈ 4.00 the absolute-motion transmissibility is √[1+(2ζr)²]/√[(1−r²)²+(2ζr)²] ≈ 0.0683. The instrument thus moves about 0.034 mm. The base-relative displacement amplitude instead uses r² in the numerator and is about 1.067 times the input, or 0.533 mm.

These two answers size different things. The instrument sees the small absolute vibration while the spring must accommodate roughly the bench excursion. Near resonance the measured low damping would permit much larger travel; a mount chosen only from the 10 Hz test could bottom during startup. The single-mode fit also assumes the measured peaks lie within a linear range.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: Modal participation and absorber tuning; Finite-amplitude oscillations and model validity. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Chapter 13 · How a longitudinal pulse reflects from a change in rod section

A tap on one end of a rod reaches a receiver after a finite delay. Two rods joined end to end can even return a pulse before the receiver sees a far-end reflection. Derive propagation speed and distinguish the displacement amplitude from the energy carried by a pulse.

From the cut force to a travelling disturbance

Measure the small axial displacement \(u(x,t)\) from a stress-free reference. In a slender homogeneous rod of area A, tensile force is

\[N=EA u_x.\]

A slice of length dx and mass \(\rho A\,dx\) accelerates because its right and left forces differ by \(N_x dx\). For constant properties, cancelling A gives

\[\rho A u_{tt}=(EAu_x)_x,\]
\[u_{tt}=c^2u_{xx},\]
\[c=\sqrt{E/\rho}.\]

Either \(u=f(x-ct)\) or \(u=g(x+ct)\) satisfies this equation by differentiation. Curves of constant phase move right or left with speed c. Long-wavelength assumptions suppress lateral inertia and radial modes. Rod area cancels from wave speed but remains in the force carried at given particle velocity. For a right-moving pulse \(v=u_t=-cu_x\), so tensile force \(N=-Zv\), with mechanical impedance \(Z=\rho Ac=EA/c\). The sign reverses for a left-moving wave.

Match velocity and force at a bonded junction

Let amplitudes I, R and T denote incident, reflected and transmitted particle velocity at the junction. A massless, perfectly bonded interface requires equal velocities and transmitted force. Apply the preceding sign convention separately to each travelling direction. The two equations determine two unknowns.

\[I+R=T,\]
\[-Z_1 I+Z_1 R=-Z_2 T,\]
\[{R\over I}={Z_1-Z_2\over Z_1+Z_2},\]
\[{T\over I}={2Z_1\over Z_1+Z_2}.\]

The instantaneous power transported to the right is \(-Nv=Zv^2\) for a single right-moving wave. For the same pulse shape the reflected energy fraction is \((R/I)^2\) and the transmitted fraction is \((Z_2/Z_1)(T/I)^2\). They sum to one. A transmitted velocity amplitude exceeding the incident amplitude can thus be compatible with conservation of energy.

A stepped rod and a pulse-echo observation

Join two aluminium rods of areas 80 and 160 mm², \(E=70\) GPa and \(\rho=2700\) kg/m³. Both have \(c=5092\) m/s, but the second impedance is twice the first. An incident velocity of 0.30 m/s produces a reflected −0.10 m/s and a transmitted 0.20 m/s. The reflected pulse carries one ninth of the energy. Reversing the experiment makes the reflection positive and the transmitted velocity 0.40 m/s for the same incident amplitude. The two energy fractions are unchanged on reversal.

A receiver at the tap detects a reflected pulse 0.40 ms after launch, before any far-end reflection contaminates it. The return path covers the distance twice, so the inferred junction is \(c(0.00040)/2=1.02\) m away. Measure pulse velocity rather than strain when comparing the signs to the plot. Incident tensile force has sign opposite incident velocity with the convention used here.

Keep the incoming pulse fixed and change the area ratio from 2 to 0.5. Determine which velocity component reverses sign and compare reflected energy fractions. Explain the difference by writing power balance for the two experiments.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · infer a second cross-section

On a uniform material an isolated junction returns 25% of the incident velocity amplitude with the same sign. Infer the area ratio and transmitted energy fraction. How could an energy-only measurement leave the geometry ambiguous?

Show the inverse relation

Let \(z=Z_2/Z_1=A_2/A_1\). Then \(0.25=(1-z)/(1+z)\), so \(z=0.60\). The transmitted velocity is \(1.25 I\). Its energy fraction is

\[0.60(1.25)^2=0.9375.\]

Measuring only the reflected energy gives 0.0625 and loses the sign of the amplitude. A thickening by a factor \(1/0.60\) would give the same energy reflection and the opposite sign in the velocity trace.

Further reading · Bower, Applied Mechanics of Solids · solutions to problems involving vibrations and wave propagation

Chapter 14 · Why the frequencies of a bending beam are not equally spaced

A distributed beam can bend into infinitely many shapes. Derive the relation between wavelength and oscillation frequency, then compare the measured response of a beam with the mode family predicted by its supports.

Combine transverse force, moment and curvature

Take a straight uniform beam with small transverse deflection \(w(x,t)\), density \(\rho\), area A and second moment of area I about the bending axis. Euler–Bernoulli kinematics keeps cross-sections perpendicular to the centreline. The bending resistance is proportional to curvature \(EI w_{xx}\). For a load-free part of the beam the gradient of moment supplies shear and the gradient of shear accelerates the mass. With restoring forces opposing deflection, the resulting equation is

\[\rho A\,w_{tt}+EI\,w_{xxxx}=0 .\]

The fourth spatial derivative calls for four boundary conditions. At a simple support the displacement vanishes and the bending moment is zero, hence

\[w=w_{xx}=0.\]

A clamp instead fixes displacement and slope. Interchanging a pin and a clamp changes the allowed shape family even if the span and material remain fixed.

Obtain the simply supported series

Try \(w=W\sin(kx)\cos(\omega t)\). Two spatial derivatives bring down \(-k^2\), and four give \(+k^4\). Substitution balances the inertia and stiffness terms. The conditions at x=0 hold for every k in this sine family. At x=L the sine must vanish, yielding

\[\omega^2={EI\over\rho A}k^4,\]
\[k_n={n\pi\over L},\]
\[f_n={n^2\pi\over 2L^2}\sqrt{EI\over\rho A}.\]

A long travelling sinusoid has phase velocity \(\omega/k=k\sqrt{EI/(\rho A)}\). The group velocity \(d\omega/dk\) is twice that value in this model. A localized pulse contains many wavelengths, which can therefore separate as it propagates. Predictions at wavelengths near the thickness require accounting for rotary inertia and shear deformation.

Choose sensor positions as carefully as the frequency range

Use a 1 m steel strip 20 mm wide and 4 mm thick with \(E=200\) GPa and \(\rho=7800\) kg/m³. For bending about the thin direction \(I=bh^3/12\) and \(I/A=h^2/12\). The first three simply supported frequencies are roughly 9.18, 36.7 and 82.7 Hz. Doubling the width alone doubles stiffness and mass together and leaves these frequencies unchanged. Doubling thickness doubles each frequency. Shortening the span by 20% increases all three by \(1/0.8^2=1.56\).

At the centre a displacement pickup sees \(\sin(n\pi/2)\). It sees the first mode at its antinode and misses the second mode entirely. At the quarter point it sees that second mode strongly. An absent spectral peak can therefore result from excitation or sensing at a node. Move the measuring point before adjusting a fitted damping value to explain its absence.

Find a common node of the first and third modes and a location that suppresses the second mode alone. Change the thickness and then the span. Use the resulting frequencies to test whether each scaling is linear or quadratic.

Tutorial · distinguish distributed bending from a tensioned string

A simply supported specimen has prominent peaks at 12 Hz and 108 Hz with the receiver at midspan. Infer which bending modes these could be and predict the frequency and receiver location for the missing mode. What would the third-to-first frequency ratio be for an ideal string?

Show the mode test

The ratio \(108/12=9\) agrees with \(n^2\) for \(n=3\). The second bending mode should lie near \(4(12)=48\) Hz. Measure at one quarter span and excite away from its nodes. An ideal uniform string has \(\omega=ck\) and a third-to-first ratio of 3. Checking both the midspan node and the shifted pickup spectrum distinguishes these models more effectively than a single peak.

Chapter 15 · Estimating a cantilever frequency from energy

A clamped beam carries a small sensor at its tip. Its precise mode shape is unknown. Use the static deflection shape to approximate the fundamental frequency and see why concentrating a small mass at the tip has a large effect.

Choose a displacement field that respects the support

Write \(w(x,t)=q(t)\phi(s)\) with \(s=x/L\), taking \(q\) as tip displacement. Choose the shape from a uniform cantilever under a static transverse tip force. The bending moment varies linearly from root to tip. Integrating curvature twice with \(w(0)=w'(0)=0\), then normalizing at \(s=1\), gives

\[\phi=s^2(3-s)/2.\]

The shape satisfies both essential clamp conditions. Its free-tip shear need not match the exact unloaded vibration mode. The energy formulation can use such admissible trial shapes.

Background · Rayleigh quotient
\[R(u)=u^\mathsf TK u/(u^\mathsf TM u)\]

For symmetric stiffness and positive mass this estimates squared angular frequency. A kinematically admissible displacement is required for a useful structural estimate.

Compute effective stiffness and mass from the same field

The strain energy density of a slender beam in bending is \(\tfrac12 EI w_{xx}^2\). Its translational kinetic energy is \(\tfrac12\rho A w_t^2\) per unit length. Changing variables from x to s introduces different powers of L into the two integrals. Include a point mass M at the tip, neglecting its rotary inertia.

\[U={EI q^2\over 2L^3}\int_0^1(\phi'')^2\,ds,\]
\[K={\dot q^2\over2}\left[\rho AL\int_0^1\phi^2\,ds+M\right].\]

Since \(\phi''=3(1-s)\), the stiffness integral is 3. Squaring \(s^2(3-s)/2\) and integrating \( (9s^4-6s^5+s^6)/4\) gives \(33/140\). The Euler–Lagrange equation for q is consequently a single oscillator equation with

\[\omega_R^2={3EI/L^3\over(33/140)\rho AL+M}.\]

For M=0 this gives the dimensionless coefficient \(\sqrt{140/11}=3.5675\). Solving the exact unloaded cantilever condition \(\cosh\beta\cos\beta=-1\) gives

\[\beta_1^2=3.5160.\]

The estimate is high by about 1.5%. The Rayleigh minimum principle explains that direction of error for conservative systems with admissible trial shapes. Restricting motion to a single shape restricts the set over which the energy quotient can attain its minimum.

Choose a sensor after estimating its dynamic effect

Take \(EI=10\) N m², \(L=0.50\) m and total beam mass 0.40 kg. The tip stiffness is 240 N/m and the distributed effective mass is only 0.0943 kg. The estimate gives 8.03 Hz without a sensor and 6.49 Hz with a 0.05 kg tip sensor. The sensor weighs one eighth of the beam, yet it has over half the effective mass associated with the chosen tip coordinate.

Place the same sensor at half-span instead. There \(\phi(0.5)=0.3125\). Its kinetic energy contributes \(M\phi(0.5)^2=0.00488\) kg to the effective mass, so the estimate rises to 7.83 Hz. The sensor measures a smaller local motion as it perturbs the frequency less. Compare that trade-off at different attachment positions with the plot.

Move along the position axis with a fixed sensor mass. Identify the interval in which frequency is most sensitive to changing attachment position. Double the sensor mass and check whether the change in frequency doubles at the tip.

Tutorial · estimate an unknown attached mass

The same bare beam is fitted with a small tip part and resonates at 6.0 Hz. Estimate the attached mass. Give one reason the inferred mass could differ from a scale reading even when the frequency measurement is accurate.

Show the mass estimate

The required effective mass is

\[240/(2\pi\cdot6)^2=0.1689\,\mathrm{kg}.\]

Subtract 0.0943 kg for the beam to obtain about 0.0746 kg. Clamp compliance would reduce the effective stiffness and frequency, which this fit would interpret as extra tip mass. The trial shape and omission of rotary inertia also limit the estimate for a part with appreciable extent.

Chapter 16 · Why a radial slide presses sideways on a rotating guide

A low-friction carriage moves along a radial slot in a horizontal turntable. It can move at constant speed relative to the guide and still require a changing force. Keeping track of the moving basis exposes both acceleration components.

Differentiate the direction vectors

Let \(r(t)\) be displacement from the turntable centre along the guide and \(\theta(t)\) the angle of the guide. In fixed Cartesian components the radial unit vector is \((\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\) and the tangential vector is \((-\sin\theta,\cos\theta)\). Differentiating shows \(\dot{\mathbf e}_r=\dot\theta\mathbf e_\theta\) and \(\dot{\mathbf e}_\theta=-\dot\theta\mathbf e_r\). Apply the product rule to the position \(r\mathbf e_r\) to obtain the velocity and then differentiate both terms.

\[\mathbf v=\dot r\mathbf e_r+r\dot\theta\mathbf e_\theta,\]
\[\mathbf a=(\ddot r-r\dot\theta^2)\mathbf e_r+(r\ddot\theta+2\dot r\dot\theta)\mathbf e_\theta .\]

One contribution to \(2\dot r\dot\theta\) comes from differentiating the radially directed velocity as its basis turns. The other comes from differentiating the magnitude of the tangential velocity \(r\dot\theta\). For positive rotation and outward travel, both point in the positive tangential direction. The expression already gives the acceleration in an inertial frame. Multiplying by mass gives the net real force required on the carriage.

Check sideways force with angular momentum and energy

Hold the angular speed at \(\Omega\), and drive the radial position as \(r=r_0+ut\) with constant u. Gravity and support cancel vertically. The net radial drive force is \(-mr\Omega^2\) and the guide must exert a side force \(2mu\Omega\). The resulting torque about the axis is \(2mru\Omega\). This equals the derivative of the angular momentum \(mr^2\Omega\), providing an independent sign and factor-of-two check.

\[P_r+P_\theta=(-mr\Omega^2)u+(2mu\Omega)(r\Omega)=mru\Omega^2={d\over dt}\left[{m\over2}(u^2+r^2\Omega^2)\right].\]

The side force does work in the laboratory since its contact point moves tangentially. Describing a smooth slot as doing zero work without specifying the frame would lose this energy transfer. The radial actuator absorbs power during outward constant-speed travel.

Forces and power on two passes

A 0.40 kg carriage moves from \(r=0.10\) m outwards at 0.20 m/s, with \(\Omega=5\) rad/s. At \(t=0.50\) s it reaches 0.20 m. Its radial force is −2.0 N, tangential force is +0.80 N and net mechanical power is

\[−2(0.20)+0.80(1)=0.40\,\mathrm{W}.\]

Setting u to zero removes the side force, while the guide still requires a radial restraint to maintain circular travel.

On an inward pass at −0.20 m/s, evaluated at the same radius, the radial force remains −2.0 N. The side force reverses and the turntable now receives angular momentum from the carriage. The magnitude of each force depends on a different combination of radius, radial speed and angular speed. Use the trace to distinguish the constant side load from the changing inward force.

Set radial speed to zero before changing angular speed. Restore outward motion and predict how doubling angular speed changes the two force components at the same radius. Use mechanical power as a separate sign check.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · reconstruct angular acceleration from a side load

At one instant the carriage is at 0.20 m, with mass 0.40 kg, \(\dot r=0.10\) m/s and \(\dot\theta=4\) rad/s. A tangential force transducer measures +0.48 N. Estimate \(\ddot\theta\) and explain what a rigidly fixed carriage would read at the same instant.

Show the reconstruction

The tangential acceleration is

\[0.48/0.40=1.20\,\mathrm{m/s^2}.\]

Subtract \(2(0.10)(4)=0.80\) m/s² to isolate \(r\ddot\theta=0.40\) m/s². Thus \(\ddot\theta=2.0\) rad/s². With no radial sliding the second term disappears and the side load falls to \(0.40(0.20)(2.0)=0.16\) N. The radial centripetal component points along the guide and does not enter this reading.

Further reading · MIT 2.003SC · velocity, acceleration and rotational motion

Chapter 17 · Steady gyroscopic precession and its two possible rates

A spinning axisymmetric rotor supported at one point can keep a constant tilt under gravity. Predict the rate at which its axis turns, examine the fast-spin approximation and determine when slow steady precession is possible.

Identify the changing direction of angular momentum

Let the centre of mass be a distance l along the symmetry axis from a fixed pivot. The symmetry axis is tilted by \(\theta\) from the upward vertical. The moment of inertia about that axis is \(I_3\), and about either transverse axis through the pivot it is \(I_1\). Define \(\omega_3\) as the total component of angular velocity along the symmetry axis. When the spin angular momentum dominates,

\[\mathbf H\simeq I_3\omega_3\mathbf e_3.\]

Under uniform precession at rate \(\Omega\), its tip traces a horizontal circle of radius \(I_3\omega_3\sin\theta\).

\[|\dot{\mathbf H}|\simeq\Omega I_3\omega_3\sin\theta,\]
\[|\boldsymbol\tau_g|=mgl\sin\theta,\]
\[\Omega_{\rm slow}\simeq {mgl\over I_3\omega_3}.\]

The tilt factors cancel for nonvertical orientations. Gravity changes the direction of angular momentum while doing zero work in a steady precession at fixed height. Reversing the spin changes the direction of slow precession. The support force has zero moment about the pivot.

Keep the angular momentum due to precession

For a regular precession at fixed tilt the transverse angular velocity is the part of \(\Omega\mathbf e_z\) perpendicular to \(\mathbf e_3\). Thus \(\mathbf H=I_1\Omega\mathbf e_z+(I_3\omega_3-I_1\Omega\cos\theta)\mathbf e_3\). The vertical first term is constant. The rotating second term has derivative \(\Omega\mathbf e_z\times\mathbf H\). Equating it to the gravitational torque yields

\[I_1\cos\theta\,\Omega^2-I_3\omega_3\Omega+mgl=0 .\]

For \(0<\theta<90^\circ\) and positive spin, two positive real rates exist only if \(D=(I_3\omega_3)^2-4I_1 mgl\cos\theta\ge0\). The smaller root is most accurately evaluated as \(2mgl/(I_3\omega_3+\sqrt D)\), avoiding subtraction of nearly equal numbers at high spin. The larger root corresponds to a different initial angular velocity. Releasing a rotor without providing an appropriate initial precession generally adds nutation.

A tabletop rotor as its spin slows

Take \(m=0.50\) kg, \(l=0.060\) m, \(I_1=0.0030\) kg m², \(I_3=0.0020\) kg m² and \(\theta=60^\circ\). At \(\omega_3=100\) rad/s the first estimate gives 1.47 rad/s. The quadratic gives 1.49 rad/s for the slow branch and 131.8 rad/s for the fast branch. The 1% discrepancy in the slow result is consistent with a small transverse angular momentum.

At \(\omega_3=30\) rad/s the approximation gives 4.91 rad/s and the exact slow rate is 5.72 rad/s. The two rates meet at a spin of roughly 21.0 rad/s. Below this value there is no constant-tilt solution for the prescribed angle and inertias. Plotting the discriminant or using the real branches avoids reporting a fictitious real rate after the branches disappear.

Compare the approximation and exact branch near the minimum admissible spin, then at high spin. Raise the tilt toward a right angle. Predict whether the minimum spin increases or falls before checking the new domain of the curve.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · infer a moment arm from observed motion

For the same inertias and mass, measure \(\omega_3=50\) rad/s, \(\Omega=3.0\) rad/s and \(\theta=60^\circ\). Estimate l from the full relation. Calculate the bias if transverse angular momentum is omitted.

Show the torque estimate

The full torque parameter is \(mgl=I_3\omega_3\Omega-I_1\cos\theta\,\Omega^2=0.300-0.0135=0.2865\) N m. The arm is

\[l=0.2865/(0.50\cdot9.81)=58.4\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Keeping only the first term gives 61.2 mm, overestimating the arm by about 4.7%. Measuring steady precession over several cycles helps distinguish that systematic error from fluctuations caused by nutation.

Critical speed, synchronous whirl and two different rotor centres · Vibration and identification in machinery

A stationary probe observes motion of a rotating shaft surface, while a balance calculation follows an eccentric mass centre. Derive both trajectories from one flexible-rotor model. Their difference explains the apparent recentring of a rotor at high speed and why a single vibration amplitude cannot identify unbalance and damping.

Chapter 18 · Why Coulomb damping loses a fixed amplitude each half-cycle

A sliding carriage on a spring can stop away from zero displacement. Determine each turning point without time stepping, then identify the experimentally observable difference between Coulomb and viscous damping.

Write a different equation for each direction of motion

A mass m slides horizontally against a linear spring k. During motion take friction magnitude \(F_k>0\), with force opposing velocity. At rest allow any friction up to the static limit \(F_s\ge F_k\). The moving equation is

\[m\ddot x+kx=-F_k\,{\rm sgn}(\dot x).\]

Release the mass at \(x=A>0\) with zero speed. If \(kA\le F_s\) static friction can hold it there. If \(kA>F_s\) it starts moving left. While it moves left, friction points right. Shift the coordinate by \(a=F_k/k\) to obtain a homogeneous oscillator:

\[m\ddot x+k(x-a)=0,\]
\[x(t)=a+(A-a)\cos(\omega_n t),\]
\[\omega_n=\sqrt{k/m}.\]

At \(t=\pi/\omega_n\) the next turning point is \(x_1=2a-A\). Test static force balance there before starting another sliding segment. If the mass continues, the sign of friction changes and the next cosine is centred on \(-a\). The time for each complete sliding half-cycle is constant despite the decreasing amplitudes. An integration routine that uses only \({\rm sgn}(0)=0\) can generate spurious chatter near the final state.

Recover the amplitude drop from work done by friction

For a full crossing from a positive peak A to a negative peak \(-B\), with \(A,B>0\), friction dissipates \(F_k(A+B)\). The change in spring energy is \(\tfrac12 k(A^2-B^2)\). Cancel the travelled distance to obtain

\[A-B={2F_k\over k},\]
\[A_j-A_{j+2}={4F_k\over k}\]

for successive magnitudes on opposite sides and successive positive peaks, respectively, while sliding continues. An ordinary viscous oscillator instead has an approximately fixed ratio between successive positive peaks. A mixed friction-damping experiment can exhibit both effects. An ideal Coulomb model also permits a final equilibrium anywhere in the static-friction band \([-F_s/k,F_s/k]\).

Predict a finite stopping time and reconstruct a friction force

Take \(m=1.0\) kg, \(k=100\) N/m, \(F_k=0.50\) N, \(F_s=0.65\) N and \(A=40\) mm. The shift is 5.0 mm and each sliding half-period is 0.314 s. Starting at +40 mm the successive turns are −30, +20, −10 and 0 mm. The mass sticks at the fourth turn after 1.257 s. A release from +43 mm instead reaches −33, +23, −13 and +3 mm. Since \(100(0.003)=0.30\) N lies below 0.65 N, the residual displacement persists under the assumptions.

Suppose a different test with \(k=200\) N/m shows successive positive peaks of 35, 27 and 19 mm. The 8 mm decrement gives

\[F_k=k(0.008)/4=0.40\,\mathrm{N}.\]

A viscous fit would infer logarithmic decrements 0.260 and 0.351 from adjacent pairs. Applying a small constant external force after the final stop can help identify static breakaway independently of the moving-friction estimate.

Compare releases at 40 and 43 mm. Predict the final resting position from the sequence of turning points. Then reduce the release below the static threshold; explain why an initial energy above zero need not imply any motion.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · predict the last turning point even without crossing zero

Use the original mass, stiffness and friction values but release at +8 mm. Does it start, cross zero, and then reverse velocity? Give the final displacement and dissipated energy.

Follow the event conditions

The initial spring force 0.80 N exceeds 0.65 N so it moves left. The next turn is

\[2a-A=10-8=+2\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

It has not crossed zero. At that turn spring force is 0.20 N so it sticks. Friction work is \(0.50(0.008-0.002)=0.0030\) J, equal to \(\tfrac12(100)(0.008^2-0.002^2)\). Blindly subtracting a positive amplitude decrement would have assigned an impossible negative magnitude.

Further reading · University of Alberta, Vibrations and Sound · viscous and Coulomb damping

Chapter 19 · When the third harmonic matters more than the fundamental

A machine applies a short force once per cycle. Its repetition rate looks safely below a structural resonance, but one of its harmonics can lie close to that resonance. Build the forcing spectrum from a pulse and apply a complex response coefficient to each term.

Integrate over the part of the period where force is present

Let an even rectangular force have peak P, period T and on-time dT centred on zero, where \(0<d<1\). Use an even periodic continuation. Its mean is Pd. Sine coefficients vanish by symmetry. With \(\Omega=2\pi/T\), integrate the cosine terms over the active interval and exploit periodicity:

\[F(t)=Pd+\sum_{n=1}^\infty a_n\cos(n\Omega t),\]
\[a_n={2\over T}\int_{-dT/2}^{dT/2}P\cos(n\Omega t)\,dt={2P\over n\pi}\sin(n\pi d).\]

The coefficients are signed. A negative coefficient adds a phase reversal; taking its magnitude before summing the time response loses that information. The mean drives a static offset. When \(nd\) is an integer, that harmonic vanishes even though the force still has abrupt edges. At a jump the Fourier series converges to the mean of the adjacent values. These details matter when reconstructing force but can have much smaller effects on the filtered displacement.

Solve each harmonic separately and keep phase

For \(m\ddot x+c\dot x+kx=F(t)\) the steady particular solution follows from \(H(i\omega)=1/(k-m\omega^2+ic\omega)\). Define \(\omega_n=\sqrt{k/m}\),

\[\zeta=c/(2\sqrt{km}),\]
\[r_j=j\Omega/\omega_n.\]

The jth response contribution is the real part of \(a_jH(ij\Omega)e^{ij\Omega t}\). Thus

\[x(t)={Pd\over k}+\sum_{j=1}^\infty {a_j\over k}\,{(1-r_j^2)\cos(j\Omega t)+2\zeta r_j\sin(j\Omega t)\over(1-r_j^2)^2+(2\zeta r_j)^2}.\]

Far above resonance each force coefficient is attenuated roughly by another \(j^{-2}\) in displacement. Acceleration weights a displacement coefficient by \(j^2\) again, so an adequate displacement truncation can be inadequate for acceleration. Start-up transients are omitted because the expression is the settled periodic response for positive damping.

Compare harmonics before selecting an operating speed

Take \(P=10\) N, \(d=0.25,\ k=1000\) N/m, natural frequency 12 Hz, damping ratio 0.04, and repetition frequency 4 Hz. The input mean is 2.50 N and \(a_1=4.50\) N while \(a_3=1.50\) N. The first harmonic has \(r=1/3\), so its displacement amplitude is about 5.06 mm. The third is exactly resonant and gives

\[1.50/(1000\cdot0.08)=18.8\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

A single harmonic based on the largest force coefficient therefore misses the dominant response.

Choose a new duty \(d=1/3\) while leaving peak force and repetition frequency unchanged. Now \(\sin(3\pi d)=0\), so the resonant third harmonic disappears. The static offset rises from 2.50 to 3.33 mm, and the first two harmonics remain. Holding mean force fixed instead would require rescaling P. A small timing error can reintroduce the third harmonic, especially important when damping is low. Move the duty around one third and compare the full signal with its fundamental-only prediction.

Use 4 Hz and tune the duty as close as the slider allows to one third. Read the residual third harmonic and compare the waveform to the fundamental-only trace. Next shift repetition frequency and distinguish removing the forcing coefficient from moving away from resonance.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · estimate sensitivity to an on-time error

Near \(d=1/3\) let the duty increase by 0.010 while keeping P fixed. Estimate the newly excited third force harmonic to first order and the resulting displacement near exact resonance. Can this signed contribution be added directly to the static peak displacement?

Differentiate the coefficient, then use the transfer function

From \(a_3=2P\sin(3\pi d)/(3\pi)\), its derivative is \(2P\cos(3\pi d)=-20\) N per unit duty at \(d=1/3\). A change of 0.010 gives about −0.20 N. At resonance the displacement amplitude is

\[0.20/[1000(0.08)]=2.50\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

The sign sets its phase, and the dynamic transfer adds a quarter-cycle lag at resonance. The largest combined displacement requires summing instantaneous contributions with their correct phases, rather than adding every harmonic magnitude as if their crests coincided.

Parametric resonance under periodic stiffness modulation · Vibration and identification in machinery

A carriage supported by a spring whose stiffness changes periodically can gain oscillation energy with no additive force at its natural frequency. Compare this mechanism with an ordinary forced resonance, estimate the first instability band and learn how to measure growth before nonlinear saturation invalidates the prediction.

Chapter 20 · What a passenger and a tyre feel on the same road

Two vehicles encounter the same nearly sinusoidal series of road undulations. In one vehicle the occupant feels little vibration, yet its contact force varies substantially. The other transmits a more noticeable body acceleration. Build a two-degree-of-freedom model from force balances, calculate the two responses to a prescribed road geometry, and test which changes in suspension damping improve one measurement at the expense of another.

Choose coordinates around a loaded equilibrium

Represent one vehicle corner by a sprung mass \(m_s\) and an unsprung mass \(m_u\). The first collects the share of the body supported at this corner. The second collects the wheel assembly and the portion of the suspension moving with it. Let \(x_s\) and \(x_u\) be upward displacements from static equilibrium. A suspension spring \(k_s\) and damper \(c\) connect the masses. A tyre spring \(k_t\) connects the wheel coordinate to the road height \(y(t)\). The increments in the tyre force describe deviations about a substantial static force.

Draw the two masses separately. When the body sits momentarily above the wheel relative to its equilibrium separation, the suspension force on the body pulls down. The same force acts upward on the wheel. The tyre pushes upward on the wheel when the road rises more than the wheel. Applying Newton's law to each free body gives

\[m_s\ddot x_s=-k_s(x_s-x_u)-c(\dot x_s-\dot x_u),\]
\[m_u\ddot x_u=k_s(x_s-x_u)+c(\dot x_s-\dot x_u)-k_t(x_u-y).\]

Gravity has disappeared because the coordinates measure changes from an equilibrium that already carries weight. To test continued tyre contact, restore the static force at the end. Replacing the input by a force \(k_ty\) on the wheel is an algebraic convenience. The tyre still extends between two moving positions, and its incremental force still depends on their difference. Overlooking this distinction predicts a changing contact load even for an arbitrarily slow rigid translation of the whole vehicle.

For fixed road height, multiply the equations by their respective velocities and sum. The spring terms form the time derivatives of \(k_s(x_s-x_u)^2/2\) and \(k_t(x_u-y)^2/2\). The remaining internal power is \(-c(\dot x_s-\dot x_u)^2\). With a moving road the contact point additionally supplies \(k_t(y-x_u)\dot y\). This check detects a damper force accidentally applied with the same sign to both masses. It also identifies the mechanical source of energy in the driven oscillation.

Determine the bounce and wheel modes before adding damping

Set \(c=0\) and \(y=0\). For a displacement vector \(\hat x\) varying as \(e^{i\omega t}\), the homogeneous equations become

\[(K-\omega^2M)\hat x=0.\]

Here \(M=\mathrm{diag}(m_s,m_u)\) and the stiffness matrix has diagonal entries \(k_s,k_s+k_t\) and off-diagonal entry \(-k_s\). Nonzero motion requires the determinant to vanish. Expanding the determinant produces a quadratic in \(\omega^2\):

\[m_sm_u\omega^4-\big[m_s(k_s+k_t)+m_uk_s\big]\omega^2+k_sk_t=0.\]

The two roots are not determined by assigning one spring rigidly to each mass. In the lower mode the wheel follows part of the body motion and the tyre deflects slightly. In the upper mode the wheel moves strongly, while the much heavier body has only a small counter-motion. The approximate estimates \(\sqrt{k_s/m_s}\) and \(\sqrt{(k_s+k_t)/m_u}\) are reasons to look in two frequency bands. The exact eigenvectors determine whether the chosen sensor has enough motion in each band to reveal the mode.

Use \(m_s=250\) kg, \(m_u=35\) kg, \(k_s=15\) kN/m and \(k_t=160\) kN/m. Mass-normalising the stiffness by \(M^{-1/2}\) gives an ordinary real symmetric eigenproblem. Its eigenvectors must be mapped back by \(M^{-1/2}\) to recover displacement ratios. The frequencies are 1.178 and 11.26 Hz. The ratios \(x_s/x_u\) are approximately 11.54 and \(-0.012\). The opposite sign in the second ratio does not imply that the body strikes the wheel. It identifies a phase difference of half a cycle in a small-displacement eigenmode.

Open editable notebook Download .ipynb

Project spatial geometry onto a temporal response

Suppose the road profile is \(y(s)=Y\cos(2\pi s/\lambda)\), where \(s\) is the distance travelled along the road. At constant speed \(v\), substitute \(s=vt\). The resulting temporal angular frequency is

\[\omega=2\pi v/\lambda.\]

A road with 4 m spacing at 12 m/s therefore supplies a 3 Hz harmonic excitation. Comparing two travel speeds on the same road involves moving along a frequency-response curve with a fixed height amplitude. This differs from holding the input acceleration amplitude fixed while sweeping frequency on a shaker.

Introduce \(S=k_s+i\omega c\), \(A=S-m_s\omega^2\) and \(B=S+k_t-m_u\omega^2\). The complex amplitudes satisfy \(AX_s-SX_u=0\) and \(-SX_s+BX_u=k_tY\). Invert this two-by-two system once and retain the phase of each result:

\[\frac{X_s}{Y}=\frac{k_tS}{AB-S^2},\]
\[\frac{X_u}{Y}=\frac{k_tA}{AB-S^2}.\]

The body accelerometer reads the amplitude \(\omega^2|X_s|\). The dynamic tyre force amplitude is \(k_t|Y-X_u|\). Subtract the complex displacements before taking an absolute value. The difference between their magnitudes cannot in general recover the extension of the tyre. For example, two signals of equal amplitude and opposite phase differ by twice that amplitude at the peak. Treating them as equal simply because their magnitudes match would predict no force change.

Change the physical and numerical inputs of the notebook model. Compare the updated curves and balance checks with the recorded notebook figures. Each plot uses the same equations and assumptions documented in that notebook; its readout names any analytical or reduced-model reference.
